EEC-301 · Power Systems-II · End-Term Companion

Three Clocks

Protection and switchgear at full depth, stability, dispatch and load-frequency control at revision depth — the whole course, weighted for the end-term

A power system has exactly one job: keep every machine on it turning at the same speed, as cheaply as possible, no matter what breaks. That single sentence is the entire course. What makes it look like several unrelated subjects is that the job has to be done on three completely different clocks at once.

Hours. Which generators do we even switch on tomorrow, and how do we split the load between the ones that are running? That is unit commitment and economic dispatch, and the only thing at stake is money.

Seconds to minutes. The load just rose by 1%, frequency has sagged, and something has to push it back to 50 Hz. That is governor droop and load-frequency control, and the only thing at stake is frequency.

Milliseconds. A line just faulted. Two questions, in this order: can we disconnect the broken piece — and only the broken piece — fast enough? And if we do, will the machines still be in step when it is over? That is protection and transient stability, and what is at stake is the whole system.

Those last two are not two topics that happen to sit in one syllabus. They are a specification and the thing that has to meet it. The equal-area criterion hands you a number — the critical clearing time, often well under half a second. The protection engineer's entire job is to make relay time plus breaker time come in under it. Stability sets the deadline; protection meets it. Once you see that link, the two halves of this course stop being separate.

This volume teaches the protection and switchgear half from scratch, because that is where the end-term's marks are and where you have the least material. Three chapters compress the pre-midterm half you have already been examined on — but they add the worked numericals and the derivations your four-page revision sheet leaves out, because the question style carries over even when the topic does not.

12 chapters 50 worked examples 33 figures 5 interactive ~69,200 words

The exam, in the professor's own words
End-term: 14 November 2026, 40 of the course's 100 marks (class work 15, practicals 25, mid-term 20). His revision sheet of 10 November says the end-term is "the only paper that examines all four modules together", and that Module 4, protection and switchgear, "is the largest module and carries a correspondingly large share of the paper". His conventions for it: angles in radians inside stability formulae; powers in per unit unless MW is written; in Module 4, currents are relay (secondary) currents unless "A primary" is written.
Weighting
Measured, not guessed, as of 28 Oct 2026. The Autumn 2025 end-term paper has been recovered and tallied: it gave 20 of its 60 marks to pre-midterm material (load-frequency control 10, economic dispatch 10) and 40 to protection and switchgear — a 33/67 split. These pages are roughly 25/75, so the pre-midterm chapters are if anything a little thin relative to the real paper. Read the next row: one number on it should change how you revise.
What the real end-term actually asked
An earlier version of this row said no end-term paper existed and that the weighting was an inference. The paper has since been found (Autumn 2025, 60 marks, 180 min) and it changes two things. First: rotor-angle stability and the equal-area criterion scored zero. Chapter 2 is a whole chapter on a topic that paper did not touch — do not let it eat your revision time. Second: the protection half was not the protection you would predict from the quizzes. There was no overcurrent, IDMT, PSM/TMS or directional-relay question at all — the topics Chapters 6 and 7 are built around. Instead it asked restriking voltage from the R–L–C circuit (10), circuit-breaker current chopping and fuses (8), generator differential protection (8), distance relaying with zero-sequence compensation (10) and transformer inrush (4). Chapters 8 to 11 are where those live. One paper is one sample, and the quizzes still say overcurrent matters for the internal marks — but if you are choosing what to drill the night before, drill Chapters 8–11 and the two revision chapters 3 and 4 first. But do not skip Chapter 2 entirely: the professor's 10 November sheet says this year's paper examines all four modules, and stability is Module 1. Give it its worked examples, not a full reread.

How to use these notes

Two kinds of question, one paper. This course examines you with two instruments that could hardly be less alike, and you have to be ready for both.

The mid-termThe quizzes
ShapeSix long multi-part numericals, 4–9 marks eachMany short fill-in blanks, 1–3 marks each
Time40 marks in 90 minutesAbout 2 minutes per question
MarkingStep marks; assumptions and units are markedNo step marking. The number is right or it is not
What it rewardsRigour, stated assumptions, and an interpretation at the endSpeed, and the fastest correct route
Where it lives hereChapters 2–4, and the long examples in 6–11The numbered procedures and two-minute routes in 5–11, and all of §12.3

So each numerical topic in this document is given twice: once as a full derivation with every assumption on the page, and once as a numbered procedure you can run under time pressure. The procedures are labelled. Use them.

If you have a week. Read Chapters 2 to 4 quickly — it is revision and you have been examined on it already. Then work slowly through Chapters 5 to 11 with paper beside you, redoing each worked example yourself before reading its answer. Chapters 6, 9, 10 and 11 are the four highest-value topics; if your week gets eaten, protect those. Save Chapter 12 for the last two days.

If you have two days. Chapter 12 first, for the shape of the whole thing — and sit both quizzes in §12.3 cold, before revising, so you find out where you actually stand. Then Chapters 6, 9, 10 and 11 in that order. Skim the revision chapters only for their worked examples.

If you have one evening. Go straight to the triage box at the end of Chapter 12. It names the specific sections, in order.

Derivations worth doing with a pen rather than reading: the equal-area criterion in §2.3, the penalty-factor result in §3.3, the two-area tie-line equations in §4.5, the bias characteristic in §9.4, and the RRRV result in §11.4. Everything else can be read.

One chapter is deliberately out of textbook order. Transformer protection (Chapter 8) comes before differential protection (Chapter 9), which is backwards from how most books do it. The reason is that percentage bias and harmonic restraint are not features in their own right — they are the cure for magnetising inrush. Meeting the disease first makes the cure obvious instead of arbitrary.

Two disciplines this examiner marks explicitly

These are free marks, and they are the ones most commonly thrown away. They appear as instructions on his own paper, so they are not a guess:

  • "All angles inside stability formulae must be used in radians; report final angles in degrees." He wrote that himself, which means he expects people to get it wrong. Every stability example in Chapter 2 shows the unit at every line.
  • "State all assumptions. Answers without units will not receive full credit." Also his own words. Every worked example in this document carries its units through the arithmetic rather than attaching them at the end.

And one he does not state, but marks anyway

Every single question on the mid-term ends by asking you to say something about the number you just computed — "comment on their relative magnitudes", "show that your answer is optimal", "quantifying each contribution as a percentage", "justifying your decision fully", "interpret both results". That is roughly a quarter of the paper, and no formula sheet can reach it. So every worked example in this document ends with an INTERPRETATION paragraph written as the answer he is asking for — not a generic remark, but the actual sentence that would score.

The boxes, and what each one means

BoxWhat it contains
WHY IT HAPPENS The mechanism behind a result just stated. If you only remember the result you will not be able to rebuild it — this box is how you rebuild it.
KEY RESULT Worth memorising verbatim, with every symbol named and the condition under which it holds.
WORKED EXAMPLE A number in, a number out, units carried throughout — and always an interpretation paragraph at the end. Most are taken from the real papers and quizzes.
PROCEDURE A numbered step list for one type of numerical, written to be followed under time pressure without the surrounding prose. The quiz half of this course is won here.
WATCH OUT Where the standard shortcut is wrong, or where this examiner has trapped people before.
EXAM NOTE How the topic has actually been asked, with the paper and the marks. This is the box that makes the document targeted rather than merely correct.
CHECKPOINT A question to answer before reading on. The answer follows immediately — try it first anyway.

Notation

Matched to the examiner's own, because answers that use his symbols are easier to mark:

One collision to be careful of. $R$ means governor droop in Chapters 3–4 and resistance everywhere in Chapters 5–11; $B$ means load damping in Chapter 4 and susceptance in the loss formula in Chapter 3. Both collisions are the examiner's, not this document's, and every use below says which is meant.

Chapter 1 orientation

Three Clocks

This course looks like three unrelated subjects — machines swinging, generators being scheduled, relays tripping. It is one subject with one job, done on three different clocks. Twelve pages from now you will be deep in relay settings; this chapter exists so that when you get there you know what the relay is for.

1.1 One job

A power system has exactly one job:

Key result — the whole course in one sentence

Keep every machine on the system turning at the same speed, as cheaply as possible, no matter what breaks.

Three demands, and they conflict. "Same speed" is frequency control. "As cheaply as possible" is economic operation. "No matter what breaks" is stability and protection. Satisfying any one of them perfectly would compromise the others, so the real system satisfies all three approximately, each on its own timescale.

Why does "same speed" matter so much? Because every generator on an AC grid is locked to every other one through the network. They are not independent machines that happen to share wires; they are a single mechanical system, coupled electrically. A generator that speeds up relative to its neighbours advances in rotor angle, which increases the power it exports, which slows it back down. That negative feedback is what holds the system together, and it has a limit. Push past the limit and the machine loses synchronism — it stops being part of the system and has to be disconnected.

So "same speed" is not a quality target. It is the condition for the system existing at all.

1.2 Three clocks

Here is the frame to carry through the whole document. The job above has to be done continuously, but the three parts of it happen at wildly different speeds — and almost nothing in this course makes sense until you know which clock you are on.

ClockThe questionWhat actsAt stakeChapters
Milliseconds Something just broke. Can we cut out the broken piece — and only that piece — before the machines fall out of step? Relays, then breakers. No human involved, and no time for one. The whole system. Get this wrong and you lose generators, not just a line. 2, 5–11
Seconds to minutes The load just changed. Frequency has moved off 50 Hz. What pushes it back? Governor droop first, automatically and incompletely; then automatic generation control, which finishes the job. Frequency. Equipment and clocks care; nothing collapses immediately. 4
Minutes to hours Which generators should even be running tomorrow, and how should the load be split between the ones that are? An optimisation, run by a control-room computer and reviewed by people. Money. Nothing breaks if you get it wrong; it just costs more. 3
Key result — your lecturer says the same thing, in his own words

The closing paragraph of Prof. Avinash Kumar's revision sheet, verbatim:

"The thread running through all three modules. Module 1 asks whether the system survives the next ten seconds. Module 3 asks who holds 50 Hz for the next thirty minutes. Module 2 asks what it costs to run for the next twenty-four hours."

That is Figure 1.1 in three sentences, written by the person setting your paper. If a question ever asks you to relate the parts of this course to one another, those three sentences are the answer — and the fact that he wrote them down means he thinks this way about his own subject.

1.3 The link: stability sets the deadline, protection meets it

Chapters 2 and 5–11 are not two topics that happen to share a syllabus. They are a specification and the thing that has to meet it, and seeing that is worth more than any individual formula in either.

Here is the chain. A fault occurs. While it persists, the faulted generator is exporting far less power than its turbine is feeding in, so it accelerates and its rotor angle advances. The equal-area criterion of Chapter 2 answers the question "how far can it advance and still come back?" — and converts that angle into a time:

Key result — the inequality that joins the two halves of the course

$$t_{\text{relay}} \;+\; t_{\text{breaker}} \;<\; t_{cr}$$

The right-hand side is computed by stability analysis: for the terminal fault worked in §2.6, $t_{cr} = 0.2213\ \text{s}$. The left-hand side is delivered by protection engineering: typically 20–40 ms for the relay plus about 100 ms for the breaker, so roughly 140 ms.

That is why a relay is expected to decide in one or two cycles, and why Chapter 9's differential protection — which needs no grading delay at all — is worth its cost on anything expensive.

Why it happens — and the uncomfortable consequence for backup protection

Work the numbers the other way and something important falls out. In §7.4 the backup relay clears a fault at the far end of its graded section in 0.94 s — more than four times the critical clearing time.

So backup protection cannot save stability. It is not fast enough and it was never going to be: the grading margin that makes it selective is itself larger than $t_{cr}$. What backup protection is for is limiting equipment damage once stability has already been lost, and making sure the fault is eventually cleared by somebody.

That is not a design failure, it is a deliberate division of labour — and it is the honest answer to "why do we need differential and distance protection when we already have graded overcurrent?" Because graded overcurrent is fast enough to save the conductor, and only unit protection is fast enough to save the system.

1.4 Three ideas that recur on all three clocks

Your lecturer named these too, and they are the reason the course hangs together mathematically rather than only thematically.

IdeaWhere it appears
A quadratic whose discriminant decides feasibility. Not "what is the answer" but "does an answer exist at all". Voltage stability (§2.9): if the discriminant of the voltage quadratic is negative, the load cannot be delivered at any voltage and the load flow diverges. Economic operation (§3.3): the cost curves are quadratics and the constraint set decides whether the dispatch is feasible.
One common variable coordinating many machines. Each machine responds to a single system-wide number, and never needs to know about the others. $\lambda$ in economic dispatch (§3.3) — every unit loads itself until its own incremental cost equals $\lambda$. Frequency $f$ in load-frequency control (§4.2) — every governor responds to the one frequency. Decentralised control through a shared scalar, twice.
A deliberate steady-state error that is the coordination mechanism. Governor droop (§4.2). A governor that held frequency exactly would fight every other governor on the system for control. By letting frequency sag in proportion to load, droop makes the machines share load automatically and stably — and the residual frequency error is then cleaned up on the next clock down, by AGC. The error is not a defect; it is the signal.

His own comment on these three: "If you can explain those three sentences, you understand the course." Chapter 12 returns to them.

1.5 What changed after the midterm

Your midterm covered Modules 1–3: the stability, economic-operation and load-frequency-control material — the second and third clocks, plus the stability half of the first. Everything since has been protection and switchgear: the other half of the millisecond clock, and the part of the course with no examined precedent.

This document is weighted accordingly, and the reasoning is on the cover. Three chapters compress Modules 1–3 to revision depth; seven teach protection from scratch.

Watch out — the revision chapters are not just a summary of the summary

You already have Prof. Kumar's four-page revision sheet, and it is genuinely good: every formula is there and the traps are flagged. What it has none of is derivations and worked examples — and on a paper of five-part numerical questions, that is the whole gap.

So Chapters 2 to 4 are not a shorter version of that sheet. They assume you have the formulas and supply what it leaves out: where each one comes from, one fully worked numerical per question type, and the interpretation sentence at the end that §1.6 explains.

1.6 Two answer registers, and why both matter

This course examines you with two instruments that could hardly be less alike, and preparing for one does not prepare you for the other.

The midterm was six long multi-part numericals, 40 marks in 90 minutes, with step marking — and every single question ended by asking you to say something about the number you had just computed: "comment on their relative magnitudes", "show that your answer is optimal", "justifying your decision fully", "interpret both results". That is roughly a quarter of the paper, and no formula sheet reaches it.

The post-midterm quizzes were short fill-in blanks, about two minutes each, with no step marking printed on the paper. There the skill being tested is speed and the fastest correct route, and a beautifully reasoned answer with one arithmetic slip scores exactly zero.

Key result — how this document serves both
  • Every numerical topic appears twice: once as a full derivation with assumptions stated, and once as a numbered PROCEDURE box you can run under time pressure. The procedure boxes are the quiz register.
  • Every worked example ends with an interpretation paragraph written as the sentence the examiner is asking for — not a generic remark. That is the midterm register.
  • Every protection worked example ends by stating the action in words, because the quizzes always ask for the action as a separate blank and it is the one most often left empty (§5.10).

1.7 Two disciplines worth more than they look

These are printed on the examiner's own paper, so they are instructions rather than advice. They are also the cheapest marks available.

"All angles inside stability formulae must be used in radians; report final angles in degrees." He wrote that himself, which tells you people get it wrong. The reason the distinction bites is that the equal-area criterion integrates $P_m\,d\delta$ — and that integral is only dimensionally right if $\delta$ is in radians, because radians are the dimensionless measure. Use degrees and your areas are wrong by $180/\pi = 57.3$. Every stability example in Chapter 2 shows the unit at every line.

"State all assumptions. Answers without units will not receive full credit." Also his words. Every worked example here carries its units through the arithmetic rather than attaching them at the end, which is slower to write and much harder to get wrong.

Checkpoint

Before reading on: a 500 MW generator trips off an interconnected system. Name what happens on each of the three clocks, in order, and say which one actually restores 50 Hz.

Answer. Milliseconds to ~2 s: the remaining machines' stored rotational energy supplies the deficit, so they all slow down together — this is inertia, and it sets the initial rate of change of frequency. Nothing is controlling anything yet. 2 to 30 s: every governor on the system senses the falling frequency and opens its valve in proportion to the error — droop. This arrests the fall and settles at a new, lower frequency, because droop by design leaves a steady-state error (§4.3). 30 s to 10 min: AGC integrates the area control error and shifts the speed-changer setpoints until the error is driven to zero. This is the one that actually restores 50 Hz — droop never does. 10 min onward: economic dispatch re-optimises which units carry the new load pattern, and unit commitment may start another machine.

Notice that nothing in that sequence involves protection: no fault occurred, so there was nothing to clear. If the generator had tripped because of a fault, the millisecond clock would have been busy first — and whether the rest of the sequence happened at all would depend on §1.3's inequality.

Chapter 2 pre-midterm · revision

Revision: Rotor-Angle Stability and the Equal-Area Criterion

You have been examined on this once already, so this chapter does not re-teach it from nothing. It supplies the two things your revision sheet leaves out — where each formula comes from, and a worked numerical of each type — plus the one output that the whole protection half of the course depends on: the critical clearing time.

2.1 The swing equation, and what $H$ physically is

A generator rotor is a flywheel with a turbine pushing it one way and the electrical load pulling it the other. Newton's second law for rotation, written in per unit, is the swing equation:

Key result — the swing equation

$$\frac{2H}{\omega_s}\,\frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} \;=\; P_m - P_e \;=\; P_a$$

with $P_e = P_{\max}\sin\delta$ and $P_{\max} = E'V/X$.

$\delta$ is the rotor angle in radians, $\omega_s$ the synchronous speed in rad/s, $P_a$ the accelerating power in per unit, and $H$ the inertia constant in MJ/MVA — which is the one symbol worth a sentence of explanation.

Why it happens — $H$ is a time, disguised as an energy

$H$ is defined as the stored rotational kinetic energy at synchronous speed divided by the machine's MVA rating:

$$H = \frac{\tfrac12 J \omega_s^2}{S_{\text{rated}}}\ \ \text{MJ/MVA}$$

MJ/MVA has the units of seconds, and the number means something concrete: $H$ is roughly how many seconds the machine could supply its own rated output from stored rotational energy alone, with no fuel at all. A typical steam turbogenerator has $H \approx 3$–$5\ \text{s}$; a hydro set, being slower and heavier, perhaps 2–4; a wind turbine with a power-electronic interface contributes almost nothing.

That reading explains why $H$ appears where it does. A large $H$ means a large $d^2\delta/dt^2$ denominator, so the rotor angle changes slowly for a given disturbance — more time before anything goes wrong, and therefore a longer critical clearing time. Inertia buys you time, and that is the entire reason the millisecond clock of §1.2 exists at all. A grid with no inertia would have to clear faults in microseconds.

2.2 The power-angle curve and its two equilibria

Set $P_e = P_m$ and the rotor is in equilibrium. Because $\sin\delta$ takes each value twice between 0 and $\pi$, there are two solutions:

$$\delta_0 = \sin^{-1}\!\left(\frac{P_m}{P_{\max}}\right) \qquad\text{and}\qquad \pi - \delta_0$$

Why it happens — why only the first one is real

Test each by nudging it. At $\delta_0$, which is below 90°, pushing $\delta$ up increases $P_e$ (the sine is still rising), so $P_a = P_m - P_e$ goes negative and the rotor is pushed back. Stable.

At $\pi - \delta_0$, which is above 90°, pushing $\delta$ up decreases $P_e$ (the sine is falling), so $P_a$ goes positive and the rotor accelerates further away. Unstable — a runaway.

So the system can only sit at $\delta_0$. The second root is not a second operating point; it is the far edge of the region you can return from, and that is exactly the role it plays in §2.4. The quantity $dP_e/d\delta = P_{\max}\cos\delta$ is the synchronising power coefficient, positive below 90° and negative above — and it is the same $T_{12}$ that appears in two-area load-frequency control in §4.5, which is not a coincidence.

Watch out — your revision sheet flags this one explicitly

"The rotor does not stop at 90° — that is the static limit, not a transient wall."

In steady state you cannot operate beyond 90°, because there is no stable equilibrium there. But during a transient the rotor routinely swings well past 90° and comes back, and the worked example below has it reaching 150°. The condition for returning is the equal-area criterion, not $\delta < 90°$. Confusing the static limit with the transient one is a standard error.

2.3 The equal-area criterion, derived

Integrating the swing equation once turns a differential equation into an area comparison, and this is worth doing rather than quoting because the derivation is three lines and it explains the word "area".

Proof — the equal-area criterion

Multiply the swing equation by $2\,d\delta/dt$:

$$\frac{2H}{\omega_s}\cdot 2\frac{d\delta}{dt}\frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = 2 P_a \frac{d\delta}{dt}$$

The left side is a perfect derivative, $\dfrac{d}{dt}\left[\left(\dfrac{d\delta}{dt} \right)^{\!2}\right]$ times $2H/\omega_s$. Integrating both sides with respect to $t$, and converting the right-hand integral to one over $\delta$:

$$\frac{2H}{\omega_s}\left(\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right)^{\!2} = 2\int_{\delta_0}^{\delta} P_a\,d\delta$$

Now the physical condition. The rotor's swing stops — reaches its furthest excursion — when $d\delta/dt = 0$. Setting the left side to zero:

$$\boxed{\;\int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_{\max}} \bigl(P_m - P_e\bigr)\,d\delta \;=\; 0\;}$$

The integrand is positive where $P_m > P_e$ (the rotor is accelerating) and negative where $P_m < P_e$ (decelerating). For the total to vanish, the positive area must equal the negative area: $A_1 = A_2$. HP.

Why it happens — the areas are energy

$\int P_a\,d\delta$ has the dimensions of energy per unit, because power integrated over angle is work. So:

$A_1$ is the excess kinetic energy the rotor absorbs while the fault is on and the turbine is overpowering the electrical load. The rotor speeds up and stores that energy as extra rotation.

$A_2$ is the energy the system can take back out after the fault is cleared, while the electrical load exceeds the turbine input and brakes the rotor.

If the available $A_2$ is at least as large as $A_1$, the rotor gives back everything it absorbed, stops, and swings home. If not, it arrives at the unstable equilibrium $\pi - \delta_0$ still turning too fast, passes it, and never returns. Stability is an energy budget, and the "equal area" is just the budget balancing.

Where the picture breaks: this argument needs a single machine against an infinite bus, and it gives you only the first swing. A real multi-machine system needs numerical integration (§2.8), and a system that survives the first swing can still lose synchronism on the third or fourth through poorly damped oscillation. The equal-area criterion is a first-swing test, and saying so is worth a mark if asked about its limitations.

2.4 Case A — a sudden increase in mechanical power

The machine is delivering $P_{m0}$ at $\delta_0$ and the turbine input is stepped up to $P_{m1}$. The new equilibrium is at $\delta_1 = \sin^{-1}(P_{m1}/P_{\max})$, but the rotor overshoots it, and the question is by how much.

Key result — Case A areas

$$A_1 = P_{m1}(\delta_1 - \delta_0) - P_{\max}\bigl(\cos\delta_0 - \cos\delta_1\bigr)$$

$$A_2 = P_{\max}\bigl(\cos\delta_1 - \cos\delta_{\max}\bigr) - P_{m1}(\delta_{\max} - \delta_1)$$

and the largest step the machine can survive is given by the transcendental condition

$$\bigl(\pi - \delta_0 - \delta_1\bigr)\sin\delta_1 = \cos\delta_0 + \cos\delta_1$$

which has no closed-form solution and must be solved by iteration or graphically. Note that it is a condition on $\delta_1$ — once you have $\delta_1$, the maximum step is $P_{m1} = P_{\max}\sin\delta_1$.

Watch out — $\delta_{\max}$ belongs to the post-fault curve

Your revision sheet flags this and it is the most common error in all three cases. $\delta_{\max}$ is where the rotor stops, and the limit of the region it can return from is the unstable equilibrium of whatever power-angle curve applies after clearing:

$$\delta_{\max} = \pi - \sin^{-1}\!\left(\frac{P_m}{P_{\max}^{\text{post}}}\right)$$

For Case A and for a terminal fault, $P_{\max}^{\text{post}} = P_{\max}^{\text{pre}}$, so $\delta_{\max} = \pi - \delta_0$ and the distinction is invisible. For Case C — where a line is lost — the post-fault curve is lower, so $\delta_{\max}$ is smaller and using the pre-fault value overestimates stability. That is precisely the trap.

2.5 Case B — a three-phase fault at the machine terminals

This is the case the midterm asked, and the only one with a closed-form answer.

A solid three-phase fault at the terminals collapses the terminal voltage to zero, so the machine can export no power at all: $P_e = 0$ throughout the fault. The whole turbine output goes into accelerating the rotor, and $P_a = P_m$, a constant.

Key result — Case B, both results

Setting $A_1 = A_2$ with $P_e = 0$ during the fault and $\delta_{\max} = \pi - \delta_0$ after it:

$$\boxed{\;\cos\delta_{cr} = \bigl(\pi - 2\delta_0\bigr)\sin\delta_0 - \cos\delta_0\;}$$

and then, converting the critical angle to a critical time:

$$\boxed{\;t_{cr} = \sqrt{\frac{4H\bigl(\delta_{cr} - \delta_0\bigr)} {\omega_s P_m}}\;}$$

with every angle in radians and $\omega_s = 2\pi f$ in rad/s.

Why it happens — why a closed form exists only here

Your revision sheet says it in one line: "A closed-form $t_{cr}$ exists only here, because $P_a = P_m$ is constant so $\delta(t)$ is an exact parabola."

Unpack that. With $P_e = 0$ the swing equation becomes

$$\frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = \frac{\omega_s P_m}{2H} = \text{constant}$$

which is the equation of constant acceleration. Integrating twice from rest:

$$\delta(t) = \delta_0 + \frac{\omega_s P_m}{4H}\,t^2$$

an exact parabola, which inverts directly to give $t$ in terms of $\delta$. Put $\delta = \delta_{cr}$ and you have the boxed result.

In every other case $P_e = P_{\max}\sin\delta$ is a function of $\delta$, so the acceleration varies as the rotor moves, the equation is non-linear, and $\delta(t)$ has no closed form. You can still get $\delta_{cr}$ from the areas — that only needs integrals of $\sin\delta$, which are elementary — but converting it to a time requires numerical integration (§2.8).

This is exactly what midterm Q6(e) asked: "comment on whether the critical clearing time can be obtained for this case in the same way as for a terminal fault." The answer is no, and the reason is the two sentences above.

2.6 The equal-area criterion, live

Before the worked example, spend a minute with the figure. Drag the clearing angle and watch the two areas: $A_1$ grows as you clear later, the available $A_2$ shrinks, and stability is lost at the exact angle where they cross.

Procedure — critical clearing angle and time, terminal fault
  1. Initial angle. $\delta_0 = \sin^{-1}(P_m/P_{\max})$. Keep it in radians for everything that follows.
  2. Critical angle. $\cos\delta_{cr} = (\pi - 2\delta_0)\sin\delta_0 - \cos\delta_0$, then $\delta_{cr} = \cos^{-1}(\cdot)$. Both $\pi$ and $\delta_0$ in radians — this is where degrees destroy the answer.
  3. Sanity-check. $\delta_{cr}$ must lie between $\delta_0$ and $\pi - \delta_0$. If it does not, you have used degrees somewhere.
  4. Critical time. $t_{cr} = \sqrt{4H(\delta_{cr} - \delta_0)/(\omega_s P_m)}$, with $\omega_s = 2\pi f$.
  5. Report. The angle in degrees, the time in seconds, and — because it is the useful engineering form — in cycles ($t_{cr} \times f$).
Worked example — the midterm's own question Midterm Q1 · tablet p.14

A generator with $H = 4\ \text{MJ/MVA}$ delivers $P_m = 0.9\ \text{pu}$ to an infinite bus through a reactance giving $P_{\max} = 1.8\ \text{pu}$, at 50 Hz. A three-phase fault occurs at the machine terminals. Find the critical clearing angle and the critical clearing time.

1. $\displaystyle \delta_0 = \sin^{-1}\!\left(\frac{0.9}{1.8}\right) = \sin^{-1}(0.5) = 0.5236\ \text{rad} = 30.00°$

2. With $\pi = 3.14159$ and $\delta_0 = 0.5236\ \text{rad}$:

$$\cos\delta_{cr} = (3.14159 - 2 \times 0.5236)\sin(30°) - \cos(30°)$$

$$= (3.14159 - 1.04720)(0.5) - 0.86603 = (2.09439)(0.5) - 0.86603 = 1.04720 - 0.86603 = 0.18117$$

$$\delta_{cr} = \cos^{-1}(0.18117) = 1.38856\ \text{rad} = \mathbf{79.56°}$$

3. Check: $30° < 79.56° < 150°$. ✓

4. $\omega_s = 2\pi(50) = 314.159\ \text{rad/s}$, and $\delta_{cr} - \delta_0 = 1.38856 - 0.52360 = 0.86496\ \text{rad}$:

$$t_{cr} = \sqrt{\frac{4 (4)(0.86496)}{(314.159)(0.9)}} = \sqrt{\frac{13.8394}{282.743}} = \sqrt{0.048947} = \mathbf{0.2213\ s}$$

5. $\delta_{cr} = 79.56°$, $t_{cr} = 0.2213\ \text{s}$, which at 50 Hz is 11.07 cycles.

Eleven cycles is the number to remember, because it is the specification the entire second half of this course has to meet. A relay takes one to two cycles and a breaker about five, so a well-protected fault is cleared in about seven — comfortably inside eleven, but not by a wide margin, and only if the primary protection works. If it fails and the backup relay of §7.4 has to act at 0.94 s, that is 47 cycles, four times over the limit, and this machine loses synchronism.

Note also what makes $t_{cr}$ large or small. It rises as $\sqrt{H}$, so doubling inertia buys only a 41% longer clearing time — inertia helps, but not as much as you might hope. And it falls as $1/\sqrt{P_m}$: a lightly loaded machine survives much longer, because $\delta_0$ is small and there is more of the power-angle curve left to decelerate into. A machine at 90% of $P_{\max}$ has almost no margin at all, which is why heavily loaded systems are the fragile ones.

2.7 Case C — a fault on one of two parallel lines

The hardest of the three, worth 9 marks on the midterm, and the one where the network changes three times.

Two lines in parallel connect the machine to the bus. A fault occurs partway along one of them. There are now three different power-angle curves, because there are three different networks:

StageNetwork$P_{\max}$
Pre-faultBoth lines healthy, in parallel $P_{\max}^{\text{pre}}$ — the largest
During faultBoth lines present but one is faulted partway along, so there is a shunt path to ground. Needs a star–delta network reduction. $P_{\max}^{\text{fault}} = r_1 P_{\max}^{\text{pre}}$ — the smallest, and not zero unless the fault is at the bus
Post-faultThe faulted line is disconnected at both ends, leaving one line $P_{\max}^{\text{post}} = r_2 P_{\max}^{\text{pre}}$ — in between: one line has twice the reactance of two in parallel, so $r_2 \approx 0.5$
Key result — Case C, with $r_1 = P_{\max}^{\text{fault}}/P_{\max}^{\text{pre}}$ and $r_2 = P_{\max}^{\text{post}}/P_{\max}^{\text{pre}}$

$$\cos\delta_{cr} = \frac{\dfrac{P_m}{P_{\max}^{\text{pre}}}\bigl(\delta_{\max} - \delta_0\bigr) + r_2\cos\delta_{\max} - r_1\cos\delta_0}{r_2 - r_1}$$

$$\delta_{\max} = \pi - \sin^{-1}\!\left(\frac{P_m}{r_2 P_{\max}^{\text{pre}}}\right)$$

and there is no closed form for $t_{cr}$ — the during-fault power is $r_1 P_{\max}\sin\delta$, which varies with $\delta$, so §2.5's parabola argument fails. You must integrate numerically (§2.8).

Why it happens — where the star–delta reduction comes in, and why $r_1 \ne 0$

A fault partway along line 1 is a shunt connection to ground at an intermediate point — so the network is no longer a simple series chain and you cannot just add reactances. The standard route is:

  1. Draw the during-fault network: machine reactance, the two line halves of the faulted line, the healthy line, and the fault's shunt branch to the reference node.
  2. You now have a star (three branches meeting at the fault point). Convert it to a delta. The delta branch directly between the machine node and the bus node is the transfer reactance $X_{\text{transfer}}$.
  3. $P_{\max}^{\text{fault}} = E'V/X_{\text{transfer}}$.

Two things fall out. First, $P_{\max}^{\text{fault}}$ is not zero: the healthy line still carries power to the bus, so the machine can still export some. Only a fault at the machine terminals gives $P_e = 0$. Second, the further along the line the fault is, the larger $r_1$ and the less severe the disturbance — which is why a question will always tell you where the fault is.

And the trap from §2.4 bites hardest here: $\delta_{\max}$ uses $r_2 P_{\max}^{\text{pre}}$, the post-fault peak, which is about half the pre-fault one. Using the pre-fault value gives a $\delta_{\max}$ that is too large, an $A_2$ that is too generous, and a $\delta_{cr}$ that says the machine is safe when it is not.

2.8 When there is no closed form: point-by-point integration

For Case C and for any multi-machine problem, $\delta(t)$ has to be computed numerically. Write the swing equation as two first-order equations:

$$\frac{d\delta}{dt} = \omega, \qquad \frac{d\omega}{dt} = \frac{\omega_s}{2H}\bigl(P_m - P_{\max}\sin\delta\bigr)$$

Key result — the point-by-point method your notes give

$$\Delta\delta_n = \Delta\delta_{n-1} + k\,P_{a(n-1)}, \qquad \delta_n = \delta_{n-1} + \Delta\delta_n, \qquad k = \frac{180\, f_0 (\Delta t)^2}{H}\ \text{deg/pu}$$

Note that $k$ is quoted in degrees per unit — this particular formulation has the degree conversion built into the constant, which is the one place in stability work where degrees are correct. The $180/\pi$ has been absorbed, and multiplying by $180$ rather than $\pi$ is what does it.

At a switching instant — the moment the fault is applied or cleared — $P_a$ jumps discontinuously, so your notes prescribe the average: $P_a \approx \tfrac12\bigl(P_a^- + P_a^+\bigr)$. Forgetting this is a standard source of error in a hand-worked table.

MethodOrderComment
Euler1stSimplest; error accumulates quickly. Rarely adequate for a full swing.
Modified Euler2ndPredictor–corrector. Good enough for hand calculation.
Runge–Kutta 44thThe industry standard. Four function evaluations per step for fourth-order accuracy.

Accuracy: RK4 > Modified Euler > Euler, at a cost in function evaluations per step. If asked which to use and why, that ordering plus "fourth-order accuracy for four evaluations" is the answer.

2.9 Voltage stability — a recognition note

Watch out — this topic scored zero on the midterm, and is here for insurance

Voltage stability is on your syllabus and in your notes (notebook p. 19–20, revision sheet Module 1(b)), but it was not examined on the midterm at all. It is therefore Tier 3: this section is deliberately short — enough to recognise a question and not panic, not enough to answer a long one. If it appears, it is likely to be worth about 4 marks.

The question is different from rotor-angle stability. There, the worry was machines falling out of step. Here, the worry is that a load cannot be supplied at any voltage.

Key result — the voltage quadratic and its discriminant

For a load $P + jQ$ at the end of a reactance $X$ fed from a source $E$:

$$V^2 = \frac{E^2}{2} - QX \pm \sqrt{\frac{E^4}{4} - X^2 P^2 - X Q E^2}$$

Two roots: the upper one is the normal operating voltage, the lower one is a physically valid but unstable solution. They meet at the nose of the P–V curve. And if the discriminant is negative, there is no real solution — the load cannot be delivered at all, and a load-flow program just fails to converge.

Check the discriminant before solving for $V$. Your revision sheet says so explicitly, and it is the fastest possible answer to "is this operating point feasible?"

Key result — the two numbers worth memorising

At unity power factor ($Q = 0$):

$$P_{\max} = \frac{E^2}{2X}, \qquad V_{\text{crit}} = \frac{E}{\sqrt 2} \approx 0.707\,E \quad\text{at a load angle of }45°$$

At a general power-factor angle $\varphi$:

$$P_{\max}(\varphi) = \frac{\cos\varphi}{1 + \sin\varphi}\cdot\frac{E^2}{2X}, \qquad V_{\text{crit}}(\varphi) = \frac{E}{\sqrt{2(1 + \sin\varphi)}}$$

Read the lagging case ($\varphi > 0$): both $P_{\max}$ and $V_{\text{crit}}$ fall. A lagging load reduces the power the line can deliver and brings the collapse point up to a higher voltage — which is why reactive compensation is the standard remedy.

Why it happens — the collapse chain, and the counter-intuitive part

The sequence your notes give: a disturbance makes voltage dip → on-load tap changers and thermostatic loads act to restore the load they were delivering → restoring load at a lower voltage means drawing more current → more current means more reactive loss in the network → voltage dips further → generators hit their field current limits, so a PV bus becomes a PQ bus and stops holding voltage → collapse.

Notice that the load's own control action is what drives the instability. This is why the constant-power ZIP load model ($P \propto V^0$) is the most onerous: it restores load perfectly and so feeds the chain hardest. A constant-impedance load ($P \propto V^2$) is benign, because its demand falls with voltage and relieves the network.

And the headline your revision sheet puts in bold: voltage instability is not the same as low voltage. A well-compensated system can sit at $V \approx 1.0\ \text{pu}$ and be one small disturbance from the nose. Proximity to collapse is about margin to the nose, not about the voltage reading — which is the whole reason you compute the discriminant rather than looking at a voltmeter.

2.10 The traps, collected

Your revision sheet lists these under "Remember / common mistakes", and every one of them has cost somebody a question.

TrapHow to avoid it
Degrees inside an area integral or inside $t_{cr}$ Radians everywhere in the formula; convert to degrees only on the final line. The $(\pi - 2\delta_0)$ term is the giveaway — if $\delta_0$ is in degrees, you are subtracting 60 from 3.14.
$\delta_{\max}$ taken from the pre-fault curve $\delta_{\max} = \pi - \sin^{-1}(P_m / P_{\max}^{\text{post}})$. Only equal to $\pi - \delta_0$ when the post-fault network is the pre-fault one.
Shading the areas before identifying the regions Mark where $P_m > P_e$ (accelerating, $A_1$) and where $P_m < P_e$ (decelerating, $A_2$) first. Figure 2.1 shows what the result should look like.
Treating 90° as a wall It is the static limit, not the transient one. The rotor reaches 150° in the worked example and comes back.
Expecting a closed-form $t_{cr}$ in Case C Only Case B has one, and the reason is in §2.5. Midterm Q6(e) asked exactly this.
Forgetting the average $P_a$ at a switching instant $P_a \approx \tfrac12(P_a^- + P_a^+)$ in the point-by-point table.
Exam note — how stability was actually asked

13 of the midterm's 40 marks, in two questions:

  • Q1 [4 marks] — Case B, exactly as worked in §2.6. And this is the significant part: it is a lecture example reproduced verbatim, with the same $H = 4$, $P_{\max} = 1.8$, $P_m = 0.9$, and the answer $\delta_{cr} = 79.56°$ already written on page 14 of the tablet notes.
  • Q6 [9 marks] — Case C, with the network reduction, and part (e) asking whether $t_{cr}$ can be found the same way as for a terminal fault.

The transferable fact is the first one. This examiner re-asks his own worked examples with the numbers unchanged. That makes every worked example in your tablet notes a live end-term candidate, and it is the single most useful thing to know about how he sets papers. Work them, do not just read them.

Checkpoint

The machine in §2.6 is re-rated so that it now delivers $P_m = 1.5\ \text{pu}$ with the same $P_{\max} = 1.8$ and $H = 4$. Compute the new $t_{cr}$ and say what has happened.

Answer. $\delta_0 = \sin^{-1}(1.5/1.8) = \sin^{-1}(0.8333) = 0.9851\ \text{rad} = 56.44°$. Then $\cos\delta_{cr} = (3.14159 - 1.97022)(0.83333) - 0.55277 = 0.97614 - 0.55277 = 0.42337$, so $\delta_{cr} = 1.13363\ \text{rad} = 64.95°$. And $t_{cr} = \sqrt{4(4)(1.13363 - 0.98511)/(314.159 \times 1.5)} = \sqrt{2.37632/471.239} = \sqrt{0.0050427} = \mathbf{0.0710\ s}$ — just 3.55 cycles.

What happened: loading the machine from 50% to 83% of $P_{\max}$ cut the critical clearing time from 11.07 cycles to 3.55 — by a factor of more than three. Two effects compound: $\delta_0$ has moved much closer to $\delta_{cr}$ (only 8.51° of margin instead of 49.56°), and the larger $P_m$ accelerates the rotor across that margin faster. 3.55 cycles is faster than a breaker can clear, so this machine cannot be protected against a terminal fault at this loading by conventional means at all. That is the quantitative reason heavily loaded systems are operated with stability margins rather than purely economically — and it is where Chapter 3's cheapest-dispatch answer runs into Chapter 2's physics.

Chapter 3 pre-midterm · revision

Revision: Economic Operation and Unit Commitment

The slowest clock, and the only one where nothing breaks if you get it wrong — it merely costs more. Fifteen of the midterm's 40 marks came from here. This chapter also contains the one place in the pre-midterm half where the rigorous method and the examiner's method disagree, and under "no step marking" the rigorous one scores zero. That is §3.5.

3.1 What a cost curve is made of

A thermal generating unit's fuel cost over an hour, as a function of its output, is modelled as a quadratic:

Key result — the cost curve and its slope

$$C_i(P_i) = a_i P_i^2 + b_i P_i + c_i \quad [\text{Rs./h}] \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \text{IC}_i = \frac{dC_i}{dP_i} = 2a_i P_i + b_i \quad [\text{Rs./MWh}]$$

The incremental cost IC is the cost of the next megawatt, and it is the quantity every dispatch decision is made on. Note that it is a straight line in $P_i$ even though the cost itself is a parabola.

Why it happens — each of the three terms means something physical

This is worth knowing because it explains why the dispatch condition ignores one of them and the commitment decision does not.

  • $c_i$ — the no-load cost. What the unit costs per hour just to be synchronised and spinning at zero output: fuel to overcome windage and friction, auxiliary power, staff. It does not depend on output at all, so it vanishes from IC.
  • $b_i P_i$ — the fuel at ideal efficiency. If the unit's efficiency were constant, this linear term would be the whole fuel bill, and $b_i$ would be the heat rate times the fuel price.
  • $a_i P_i^2$ — the efficiency penalty. Real units get less efficient as they are loaded harder — throttling losses, higher exhaust temperature, more auxiliary load. The quadratic term is that worsening, and it is what makes the incremental cost rise with output and therefore makes the dispatch problem interesting at all.

If $a_i$ were zero for every unit, the cheapest dispatch would be trivial: load the unit with the smallest $b$ to its maximum, then the next, and so on. It is the rising incremental cost that makes sharing load between units optimal.

3.2 The lossless condition: equal incremental cost

Minimise $\sum_i C_i(P_i)$ subject to $\sum_i P_i = P_D$. Form the Lagrangian:

Proof — why equal $\lambda$ is optimal

$$\mathcal{L} = \sum_i C_i(P_i) - \lambda\left(\sum_i P_i - P_D\right)$$

$$\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial P_i} = \frac{dC_i}{dP_i} - \lambda = 0 \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \boxed{\;\frac{dC_i}{dP_i} = \lambda \ \text{ for every unit}\;}$$

HP. But the reason is more useful than the algebra, and it is a one-line argument you can give without any calculus:

Suppose two units had different incremental costs, say $\text{IC}_1 = 20$ and $\text{IC}_2 = 25$. Then move one megawatt from unit 2 to unit 1. You save 25 and spend 20, so the total cost falls by 5 — the dispatch was not optimal. You can keep doing this until the incremental costs are equal, and then no such move helps. That is the whole theorem.

Key result — three readings of $\lambda$

$\lambda$ is not just a Lagrange multiplier. It is simultaneously:

  • the system marginal cost — what it costs to serve one more MW of demand;
  • the price of the next megawatt, which is why it is the number a control room actually watches;
  • the market-clearing price in an electricity market, which is how the same mathematics turns into a price paid to generators.

And structurally it is the second instance of §1.4's pattern: one common variable coordinating many machines. Each unit needs to know only $\lambda$ and its own cost curve — never anything about any other unit.

3.3 When a unit hits a limit

Real units have $P_i^{\min}$ and $P_i^{\max}$. If the equal-$\lambda$ solution asks a unit for more than it can give, you cannot have it.

Procedure — dispatch with generation limits
  1. Solve as if unconstrained. Set all IC equal to $\lambda$, write each $P_i$ in terms of $\lambda$, and use $\sum P_i = P_D$ to find $\lambda$.
  2. Check every unit against its limits.
  3. Clamp the worst violator at the limit it broke, and remove it from the $\lambda$ equation — its output is now a fixed number, so subtract it from $P_D$ and re-solve for the remaining free units.
  4. Re-check every remaining free unit. One clamp changes $\lambda$, which can push a second unit over its own limit. Repeat until no unit violates.
  5. Verify optimality with the KKT test below, because that is what "show that your answer is optimal" is asking for.

Step 4 is the one people skip. Your revision sheet flags it: "then re-check every free unit (one clamp may cause another)."

Key result — the KKT optimality test, and how to say it in words

At the optimum, every unit satisfies one of three conditions:

Unit stateConditionWhat it means in words
Free (between its limits)$\text{IC}_i = \lambda$ Marginal — it sets the price.
At $P^{\max}$$\text{IC}_i \le \lambda$ "A bargain we ran out of." It is cheaper than the market price, so we want more of it and cannot have it.
At $P^{\min}$$\text{IC}_i \ge \lambda$ "A burden we are stuck with." It is dearer than the market price, so we want less of it and are not allowed less.

Those two phrases are from your revision sheet, and they are the fastest way to get the inequality directions the right way round under exam pressure. If a maxed-out unit had $\text{IC} > \lambda$, you would want to back it off — so it cannot be at its maximum in an optimal solution.

3.4 With losses: penalty factors

Transmission loses power, and the loss depends on which unit generates. A distant unit's cheap megawatt arrives partly as heat in the lines, so it is not as cheap as its cost curve says.

Key result — the coordination equation

$$L_i \cdot \text{IC}_i = \lambda, \qquad L_i = \frac{1}{1 - \dfrac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i}}, \qquad P_L = \sum_i\sum_j P_i B_{ij} P_j$$

$L_i$ is the penalty factor and $\partial P_L/\partial P_i$ is the incremental transmission loss. The power balance also changes, to $\sum_i P_i = P_D + P_L$.

Why it happens — which way a penalty factor pushes a unit

This is the trap, so reason it out rather than memorising.

$L_i \cdot \text{IC}_i = \lambda$ rearranges to $\text{IC}_i = \lambda / L_i$. So a unit with a large $L_i$ is required to operate at a smaller incremental cost — which, since IC rises with output, means a smaller output. It gets backed off.

And $L_i$ is large when $\partial P_L/\partial P_i$ is large and positive — that is, a unit whose extra output increases system losses. Typically a remote plant, far from the load.

Your revision sheet puts it as: "$L_i > 1$: remote plant, its cheap MW are taxed by the MW lost in transit. $L_i < 1$: a plant whose output reduces losses — a subsidy." A penalty factor below 1 is possible and means the unit is well sited: generating more there relieves flows elsewhere and reduces total loss, so it deserves to run harder than its own cost curve suggests.

One-line check: remote plant → high loss sensitivity → high $L$ → low required IC → backed off.

3.5 Two routes to a penalty factor, and only one scores

Watch out — this is the one conflict in the pre-midterm half, and it is a whole question

The coordination equation $L_i \cdot \text{IC}_i = \lambda$ is implicit: $L_i$ depends on $P_i$, and $P_i$ depends on $L_i$. There are two ways to deal with that, they give different numbers, and the master copy uses the second.

Route A — rigorousRoute B — this course
Method Solve $L_i \text{IC}_i = \lambda$ simultaneously with $L_i = 1/(1 - \partial P_L/\partial P_i)$ Find the lossless dispatch first from $\text{IC}_i = \lambda$, then evaluate the penalty factors at that operating point
On the quiz data$L_1 = 1.071$, $L_2 = 1.061$ $L_1 = \mathbf{1.111}$, $L_2 = \mathbf{1.071}$
Master copy—1.11 and 1.07 ✓

Use Route B. It is a first-iteration approximation of Route A — and in fact it is exactly the first pass of the $\lambda$-iteration you would run to solve Route A properly. It is a legitimate engineering method and it is what this examiner marks. Under "no step marking", Route A's beautifully rigorous 1.071 scores zero.

If an end-term question gives you room to write, say which you are doing in one line — "evaluating penalty factors at the lossless dispatch point, as a first iteration" — and you are protected either way.

Worked example — penalty factors, the examiner's route Major Quiz Q6 · Lab Quiz Q2

$\dfrac{dC_1}{dP_1} = 20 + 0.02P_1$, $\dfrac{dC_2}{dP_2} = 15 + 0.03P_2$, $P_L = 0.0002P_1^2 + 0.0001P_2^2$, and $\lambda = 25$. Find $L_1$ and $L_2$ — and the optimal generations.

1 — the lossless dispatch. Set each IC equal to $\lambda$:

$$20 + 0.02 P_1 = 25 \;\Longrightarrow\; P_1 = \frac{5}{0.02} = \mathbf{250\ MW}$$

$$15 + 0.03 P_2 = 25 \;\Longrightarrow\; P_2 = \frac{10}{0.03} = \mathbf{333.3\ MW}$$

(These two numbers are the complete answer to Lab Quiz Q2, which is the same data asked as a separate question.)

2 — the incremental losses, differentiating $P_L$:

$$\frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_1} = 0.0004\,P_1 = 0.0004 \times 250 = 0.10$$

$$\frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_2} = 0.0002\,P_2 = 0.0002 \times 333.3 = 0.0667$$

3 — the penalty factors.

$$L_1 = \frac{1}{1 - 0.10} = \frac{1}{0.90} = \mathbf{1.111}$$

$$L_2 = \frac{1}{1 - 0.0667} = \frac{1}{0.9333} = \mathbf{1.071}$$

Both penalty factors exceed 1, so both units are being taxed for the losses their output causes — unsurprising, since the loss formula is positive-definite and has no cross-terms, so every unit's extra megawatt adds loss.

And $L_1 > L_2$: unit 1 is penalised more heavily. Read why from the loss formula — $\partial P_L/\partial P_1 = 0.0004 P_1$ against $\partial P_L/\partial P_2 = 0.0002 P_2$, so unit 1's loss sensitivity coefficient is twice unit 2's. Unit 1 is the electrically remote plant. Properly accounting for that would back unit 1 off below 250 MW and let unit 2 pick up the difference — which is exactly what Route A's 166.7 MW and 285.7 MW do. That is the reading of the penalty factors: they are the correction that moves generation towards the load.

What cannot be answered from this data: the total cost. Only the incremental costs are given, so $a$, $b$ are recoverable but the no-load constants $c_i$ are not — and the total cost needs them. If a question asks for total cost it must supply the full cost curves; if it does not, say so rather than inventing $c = 0$.

3.6 Unit commitment — a different question

Economic dispatch answers how much each running unit should produce. Unit commitment answers which units should be running at all, hour by hour, and it is a much harder problem.

Key result — the unit-commitment problem

With $u_{it} \in \{0,1\}$ the on/off decision for unit $i$ in hour $t$:

$$\min \sum_t \sum_i \Bigl[\,u_{it}\,C_i(P_{it}) + SU_{it}\,\Bigr]$$

subject to, in every hour,

$$\sum_i u_{it} P_{it} = P_{D,t} \qquad\text{and}\qquad \sum_i u_{it} P_i^{\max} \ge P_{D,t} + R_t$$

The second constraint is the spinning reserve requirement: the committed units must be able to cover not just the load but a margin above it, so that losing the largest unit does not cause a blackout.

Plus minimum up and down times — a large thermal unit cannot be cycled freely; its metal has to heat and cool slowly — and start-up costs $SU_{it}$, which depend on how long the unit has been off.

Why it happens — why this is not just dispatch with extra steps

Dispatch is a continuous optimisation and calculus solves it. Commitment is combinatorial: the variables are binary, so there is no derivative to set to zero.

And the search space is brutal. With $N$ units over $T$ hours there are $2^{N \times T}$ combinations. Your revision sheet does the arithmetic: 10 units over 24 hours is about $10^{72}$ cases — more than the number of atoms in the observable universe. Brute force is not slow, it is impossible.

Worse, the hours are coupled. Minimum down time means a decision in hour 3 constrains hour 7, so you cannot optimise each hour independently either. Real systems use Lagrangian relaxation, dynamic programming or mixed-integer programming. For hand calculation there is the priority-list method below, which is a good heuristic rather than an optimum — and saying that is worth a mark.

3.7 The priority-list method

Procedure — priority-list unit commitment
  1. Rank the units by full-load average cost: $$\text{FLAC}_i = \frac{C_i(P_i^{\max})}{P_i^{\max}}\quad[\text{Rs./MWh}]$$ cheapest first. This is the priority list, and it is fixed for the whole study.
  2. Commit down the list, hour by hour, until the committed capacity covers the load plus the reserve: $\sum u_i P_i^{\max} \ge P_{D,t} + R_t$.
  3. Cost the hour by running an equal-$\lambda$ economic dispatch (§3.2–3.3) over the committed set only.
  4. On falling load, apply two tests before decommitting the marginal unit:
    • (a) Feasibility — does its minimum down time allow it to be back when it is next needed? If not, it must stay on regardless of cost.
    • (b) Economics — is the cost of riding through the trough with it on, greater than the cost of shutting down and restarting? Compare the hourly premium against the start-up cost.
Watch out — the error your revision sheet calls "the most common in Module 2"

"Average cost ranks the list; incremental cost dispatches the set."

Two different cost measures, two different jobs, and they give different orderings.

The ranking uses full-load average cost, which includes the no-load constant $c_i$ — because the commitment decision is precisely the decision whether to pay $c_i$ at all. A unit with a huge $c$ and a tiny incremental cost is expensive to have on and cheap to load; the average cost captures that and the incremental cost does not.

The dispatch uses incremental cost, which ignores $c_i$ — because once the unit is committed, $c_i$ is being paid whatever you do, so it cannot influence how much to load it. A sunk cost is irrelevant to a marginal decision.

Mixing them — ranking by incremental cost, or dispatching by average cost — is the classic error, and it is the sort of thing a question asking you to "justify your decision fully" is testing.

Exam note — how economic operation was actually asked

15 of the midterm's 40 marks, in two questions, plus two quiz blanks:

  • Midterm Q2 [7 marks, five parts] — economic dispatch with losses: penalty factors, $\lambda$, and then "show that your answer is optimal" (the KKT test of §3.3) and the total cost.
  • Midterm Q4 [8 marks] — priority-list unit commitment over a 4-hour schedule, including a decommit decision with the two tests of §3.7 stated as named options.
  • Major Quiz Q6 and Lab Quiz Q2 — the penalty-factor and lossless-dispatch pair worked in §3.5.

Two things to note. The mid-term questions were the only ones on the paper worth 7 and 8 marks respectively with five and four parts — so this topic rewards a structured, step-by-step answer more than any other. And the quiz questions are the same data as each other, split across two papers, which is this examiner's reuse habit (§2.10) showing up again.

Checkpoint

Two units: $C_1 = 0.01P_1^2 + 20P_1 + 500$ and $C_2 = 0.02P_2^2 + 15P_2 + 1200$, both with $P^{\max} = 200\ \text{MW}$ and $P^{\min} = 50\ \text{MW}$. Demand is 100 MW with no reserve requirement. Which single unit would a priority list commit — and is that the cheapest way to serve 100 MW?

Answer. Full-load average costs: $\text{FLAC}_1 = C_1(200)/200 = (400 + 4000 + 500)/200 = 4900/200 = 24.5$; $\text{FLAC}_2 = C_2(200)/200 = (800 + 3000 + 1200)/200 = 5000/200 = 25.0$. So the priority list commits unit 1, and running it alone at 100 MW costs $C_1(100) = 100 + 2000 + 500 = \mathbf{2600\ Rs./h}$.

Is that cheapest? Check the alternative. Unit 2 alone at 100 MW costs $C_2(100) = 200 + 1500 + 1200 = 2900$ — worse. Both on, with $P^{\min} = 50$ each, forces $P_1 = P_2 = 50$: $C_1(50) + C_2(50) = (25 + 1000 + 500) + (50 + 750 + 1200) = 1525 + 2000 = 3525$ — much worse, because you are now paying both no-load costs. So yes, the priority list got it right here, and the reason it did is that the no-load costs dominate at this low demand.

The instructive part is why ranking by incremental cost would have got it wrong: at 100 MW, $\text{IC}_1 = 20 + 0.02(100) = 22$ while $\text{IC}_2 = 15 + 0.04(100) = 19$ — unit 2 looks cheaper on the margin and is 300 Rs./h more expensive in total, because its $c$ is 1200 against 500. That is §3.7's watch-out, in numbers.

Chapter 4 pre-midterm · revision

Revision: Load-Frequency Control

The middle clock, and 12 of the midterm's 40 marks. The whole subject turns on one idea that looks like a defect: governor droop deliberately leaves a steady-state frequency error, and that error is not a failure of the design — it is the design. Everything else in the chapter follows from understanding why.

4.1 The four stages of a frequency event

A load increase or a generator trip sets off a sequence that your revision sheet calls the timescale ladder. Each stage hands over to the next, and each does something the previous one could not.

StageTimescaleMechanismWhat it achieves
Inertia0–2 s No control at all. The machines' stored rotational energy supplies the deficit, so they all slow down together. Sets the rate of change of frequency (RoCoF) and buys time. Arrests nothing.
Primary — droop2–30 s Every governor on the system opens its valve in proportion to the frequency error. Arrests the fall and settles at a new frequency — not 50 Hz.
Secondary — AGC30 s–10 min An integral controller shifts speed-changer setpoints until the area control error is zero. Restores 50 Hz exactly, and restores the scheduled tie-line flows.
Tertiary — dispatch10 min+ Economic dispatch (Chapter 3) re-optimises which units carry the new load. Makes it cheap again.

4.2 Droop, and why it is deliberate

A governor could in principle be built to hold frequency exactly: sense any error and integrate until it is gone. That is called an isochronous governor and it is used — on machines running alone.

Why it happens — why an isochronous governor cannot be used on a grid

Put two isochronous machines on the same system. Each one's target is 50.00 Hz, but no two sensors agree perfectly — say one reads 50.00 when the truth is 50.01.

Machine A thinks the frequency is low and opens its valve. Machine B thinks it is high and closes. The frequency is now right, so neither stops: A keeps opening and B keeps closing, until A is at full load and B is at zero. They fight, and the load sharing is decided by sensor offsets rather than by anything sensible.

Droop fixes this by giving each machine a sloping characteristic instead of a target. Each governor's output depends on the frequency, not on the frequency error, so there is exactly one frequency at which the total generation matches the load — and at that frequency every machine is at a well-defined, stable output. The machines no longer have individual targets to fight over.

The price is a residual frequency error. The payoff is stable, predictable, decentralised load sharing with no communication between machines at all. This is §1.4's third pattern: a deliberate steady-state error that is the coordination mechanism.

Key result — droop and the frequency it settles at

The governor characteristic is

$$\Delta P_m = -\frac{\Delta f}{R}$$

where $R$ is the droop in Hz per pu MW. The minus sign says that falling frequency produces more power, which is the negative feedback that makes the thing work.

Load itself also helps, because most load draws less power at lower frequency — that is the damping $B$, in pu MW/Hz. Combining the two, a load step $\Delta P_D$ settles at

$$\boxed{\;\Delta f = \frac{-\Delta P_D}{B + \dfrac{1}{R}} = \frac{-\Delta P_D}{\beta}\;} \qquad \beta = \frac{1}{R} + B$$

$\beta$ is the area frequency response characteristic, or area stiffness, in pu MW/Hz. It is the single number that says how much the frequency moves for a given load change — the larger $\beta$, the stiffer the system.

Since usually $1/R \gg B$, the useful approximation is $\Delta f \approx -R\,\Delta P_D$.

Watch out — $R$ versus $1/R$, and the sign

Two traps your revision sheet flags, and both are easy to fall into.

$R$ or $1/R$? The steady-state result uses $R$: $\Delta f \approx -R\,\Delta P_D$. But the block diagram's feedback path carries $1/R$, because the block converts a frequency into a power. Both are correct in their own place; writing $1/R$ in the steady-state formula inverts your answer by a factor of $R^2$.

The sign. A load increase makes $\Delta f$ negative. Your revision sheet puts it bluntly: "a positive answer is wrong even if the magnitude is right." Check the sign before you check anything else — it costs nothing and it is marked.

4.3 Why droop alone can never reach 50 Hz, and what does

Look at the boxed formula. $\Delta f = 0$ requires $\Delta P_D = 0$ — that is, no load change at all. For any real load change, droop leaves an error. It is not a tuning problem; it is what the equation says.

Adding the speed changer — a setpoint shift $\Delta P_C$ commanded from outside the governor — changes that:

$$\Delta f = \frac{\Delta P_C - \Delta P_D}{B + 1/R}$$

so if $\Delta P_C = \Delta P_D$, then $\Delta f = 0$ exactly. The job of automatic generation control is to find that $\Delta P_C$ without being told what $\Delta P_D$ was.

Key result — why the controller must be an integrator

AGC applies an integral controller to the frequency error (for an isolated area) or to the area control error (for an interconnected one). The reason it must be an integral controller and not a proportional one is structural:

A proportional controller produces an output proportional to the error, so it needs a non-zero error to produce any output at all — which is exactly the problem droop already has. An integral controller accumulates the error, so its output keeps growing as long as any error remains, and it only stops when the error is exactly zero. Zero steady-state error is the defining property of integral action.

The cost, which your revision sheet notes: it adds one to the system order, so the response can overshoot and the integral gain has to be kept small enough not to fight the governors. That is why AGC acts over minutes while droop acts over seconds — and deliberately so.

One elegant consequence: $\int \text{ACE}\,dt$, the accumulated area control error, is the clock-time error of a synchronous clock driven from the grid. Grid frequency is literally a timekeeping standard, and the integral of the error is how many seconds slow the clock on the wall has become.

Key result — the dynamic response, and the blocks it comes from

The plant is three first-order blocks in series: governor $K_{sg}/(1 + T_{sg}s)$, turbine $K_t/(1 + T_t s)$, and generator–load $K_{ps}/(1 + T_{ps}s)$, with

$$K_{ps} = \frac{1}{B}, \qquad T_{ps} = \frac{2H}{B f^0}$$

Typical values: $T_{sg} = 0.4\ \text{s}$, $T_t = 0.5\ \text{s}$, $T_{ps} = 20\ \text{s}$. Because $T_{sg}, T_t \ll T_{ps}$, they are usually neglected, and the closed-loop response to a load step is a single exponential:

$$\Delta f(t) = -\left[\frac{R\,K_{ps}}{R + K_{ps}}\right] \left\{1 - \exp\!\left[-\frac{t}{T_{ps}}\cdot\frac{R + K_{ps}}{R}\right]\right\} \Delta P_D$$

Why it happens — droop makes the system 34 times faster

Read the exponent. The closed-loop time constant is

$$T_{\text{closed}} = T_{ps}\,\frac{R}{R + K_{ps}}$$

With the course's values $T_{ps} = 20\ \text{s}$, $R = 3$, $K_{ps} = 100$:

$$T_{\text{closed}} = 20 \times \frac{3}{103} = 0.58\ \text{s}$$

against an open-loop $T_{ps}$ of 20 s — a factor of 34 times faster, and settling in under two seconds instead of a minute.

That is what the droop feedback loop buys, and it is worth noticing because it is easy to read droop as purely a sharing mechanism. It is also a stabiliser: a smaller $R$ (tighter droop) gives both a smaller steady-state error and a faster response. The limit on how small $R$ can be is governor hunting and the mechanical wear of valves moving constantly — not the mathematics.

4.4 Load sharing between machines

Key result — droop in per cent, and the base trap

Droop is usually quoted as a percentage: a "5% droop" machine goes from no load to full load for a 5% change in frequency. In that form,

$$\frac{\Delta f}{f} = R_{\%} \times \frac{\text{MW carried}}{\text{own MW rating}}$$

which rearranges to the form used in the worked example below.

And the trap: a droop in per cent is referred to the machine's own base. Two machines of different ratings with the same percentage droop have different $R$ in Hz/pu MW on a common system base. The conversion is

$$R_{\text{sys}} = R_{\text{mach}} \times \frac{S_{\text{sys}}}{S_{\text{mach}}}$$

Your revision sheet's warning: "droop percentages are not comparable until referred to a common base." And the corollary, which is the useful design fact: equal percentage droop makes machines share load in proportion to their ratings — which is exactly what you want, and is why utilities standardise droop settings at 4–5%.

Worked example — droop sharing Major Quiz Q10

Two generators operate in parallel supplying a total load of 200 MW. G1 is rated 100 MW with 5% droop; G2 is rated 150 MW with 4% droop. The frequency falls from 50 Hz to 49.5 Hz. Find each machine's change in output.

1 — the per-unit frequency change.

$$\frac{\Delta f}{f} = \frac{0.5}{50} = 0.01\ \ (1\%)$$

2 — each machine's pickup. Rearranging the boxed relation, $\Delta P = \dfrac{\Delta f}{f} \times \dfrac{P_{\text{rated}}}{R_{\%}}$:

$$\Delta P_1 = 0.01 \times \frac{100}{0.05} = \mathbf{20\ MW}$$

$$\Delta P_2 = 0.01 \times \frac{150}{0.04} = \mathbf{37.5\ MW}$$

G2 picks up nearly twice as much — 37.5 MW against 20 — and there are two compounding reasons. It is 1.5 times larger, which alone would give it 1.5 times the pickup. And its droop is tighter (4% against 5%), so it responds more aggressively per unit of rating, multiplying its share by a further 5/4. Together: $1.5 \times 1.25 = 1.875$, and indeed $37.5/20 = 1.875$.

That is the quantitative reason droop settings are standardised across a fleet. If these two machines are meant to share proportionally to rating, G2 is carrying more than its share of every disturbance and will wear its valves faster. The fix is to set both to the same percentage.

A sanity check worth doing: the two pick up 57.5 MW between them, so the load must have risen by about that much (plus whatever the damping $B$ absorbed). The 200 MW in the question is the pre-disturbance load and does not enter the calculation — a distractor, like the ones in §11.4.

4.5 Two areas, a tie line, and ACE

Connect two control areas by a tie line and the problem changes character: each area now has to care not only about frequency but about how much power is crossing the boundary, because that flow is contractually scheduled.

Key result — the tie line is an integrator

$$\Delta P_{\text{tie},1} = T_{12}\bigl(\Delta\delta_1 - \Delta\delta_2\bigr), \qquad T_{12} = \frac{|V_1||V_2|}{P_{r1}X_{12}}\cos\bigl(\delta_1^0 - \delta_2^0\bigr)$$

$T_{12}$ is the synchronising coefficient — and notice it is the same quantity as the synchronising power coefficient of §2.2, $dP_e/d\delta$, evaluated at the operating point. The two halves of the pre-midterm course share a derivative.

Now the key structural point, in your revision sheet's words: "since angle = $\int$ frequency, the tie line is itself an integrator":

$$\Delta P_{\text{tie},1}(s) = \frac{2\pi T_{12}}{s}\bigl[\Delta F_1 - \Delta F_2\bigr]$$

And from the other area's point of view, with $a_{12} = P_{r1}/P_{r2}$:

$$T_{21} = a_{12}\,T_{12}, \qquad \Delta P_{\text{tie},2} = -a_{12}\,\Delta P_{\text{tie},1}$$

This course uses $a_{12} = +P_{r1}/P_{r2}$, positive. Some textbooks define it with a minus sign and move the sign elsewhere; the course's convention is internally consistent and is the one to use.

Why it happens — and why that integrator matters

Tie-line power depends on the angle difference, and angle is the integral of frequency. So a persistent frequency difference between the two areas produces an ever-growing angle difference and therefore an ever-growing tie-line flow — until something saturates or trips.

That is why the tie line does not merely transmit power: it forces the two areas to the same frequency. Any difference is integrated into a flow that loads the fast area and relieves the slow one, until they match. The interconnection is a coupling, not a pipe.

Key result — ACE, and what each area's ACE is telling you

$$\text{ACE}_i = \Delta P_{\text{tie},i} + b_i\,\Delta f_i, \qquad b_i \approx \beta_i$$

Each area's AGC drives its own ACE to zero. And with the standard choice $b_i = \beta_i$, something remarkable happens. For a load step $\Delta P_{D1}$ in area 1, with primary control only:

$$\Delta f = \frac{-\Delta P_{D1}}{\beta_1 + \dfrac{\beta_2}{a_{12}}}, \qquad \Delta P_{\text{tie},1} = \frac{\beta_2\,\Delta f}{a_{12}}$$

and substituting these into the ACE definitions gives

$$\text{ACE}_1 = -\Delta P_{D1} \qquad\text{and}\qquad \boxed{\;\text{ACE}_2 = 0\;}$$

The area that caused the disturbance sees its own disturbance in its ACE; the area that helped sees nothing at all. So area 2's AGC does not act, area 1's AGC corrects exactly its own load change, and the tie-line flow returns to schedule. That is non-interactive control, and it is the entire reason $b_i$ is chosen equal to $\beta_i$ — a genuinely elegant result and a very likely "interpret both results" question.

Watch out — the standard error in every two-area problem

Your revision sheet states it directly: "In two-area problems $\beta_2$ must be divided by $a_{12}$ — writing $\beta_1 + \beta_2$ is the standard error."

The reason is base conversion, the same issue as §4.4. $\beta_1$ and $\beta_2$ are each expressed on their own area's base, and you cannot add quantities on different bases. The $a_{12} = P_{r1}/P_{r2}$ factor refers area 2's stiffness to area 1's base. Writing $\beta_1 + \beta_2$ silently assumes the two areas are the same size.

Two further traps in the same family: ACE = $\Delta f$ only for an isolated area (there is no tie line, so the first term vanishes), and the sign of $\Delta P_{\text{tie}}$ must be stated as a direction — "2 MW from area 1 to area 2" — not left as a bare number, because the midterm asked for exactly that.

Worked example — isolated-area steady state and dynamics

An isolated area has $K_{ps} = 100\ \text{Hz/pu MW}$, $T_{ps} = 20\ \text{s}$ and a governor droop $R = 3\ \text{Hz/pu MW}$, at $f^0 = 50\ \text{Hz}$. A load step of $\Delta P_D = 0.01\ \text{pu}$ occurs. Find $B$, $H$, $\beta$, the steady-state frequency error, and the settling time.

1 — damping, from $K_{ps} = 1/B$:

$$B = \frac{1}{K_{ps}} = \frac{1}{100} = 0.01\ \text{pu MW/Hz}$$

2 — inertia, from $T_{ps} = 2H/(Bf^0)$:

$$H = \frac{T_{ps}\,B\,f^0}{2} = \frac{20 \times 0.01 \times 50}{2} = 5\ \text{MJ/MVA}$$

3 — area frequency response:

$$\beta = \frac{1}{R} + B = \frac{1}{3} + 0.01 = 0.3333 + 0.0100 = \mathbf{0.3433\ pu\ MW/Hz}$$

4 — steady-state error:

$$\Delta f = \frac{-\Delta P_D}{\beta} = \frac{-0.01}{0.3433} = \mathbf{-0.0291\ Hz}$$

Cross-check with the dynamic expression's final value, $-\bigl[R K_{ps}/(R + K_{ps})\bigr]\Delta P_D = -(300/103)(0.01) = -0.0291$. ✓

5 — settling time. The closed-loop time constant is $T_{ps}R/(R+K_{ps}) = 20 \times 3/103 = 0.58\ \text{s}$, so the response is essentially complete in $3\tau \approx \mathbf{1.7\ s}$.

Read the three numbers together. $\Delta f = -0.029\ \text{Hz}$ is tiny — 0.06% of 50 Hz — because $\beta$ is large: the governors are stiff. But it is not zero, and it will stay there indefinitely unless AGC acts. That is the whole case for secondary control.

And note which term dominates $\beta$: $1/R = 0.333$ against $B = 0.010$, a ratio of 33 to 1. The governors do 97% of the work and the load's own damping does 3%. That is why $\Delta f \approx -R\,\Delta P_D$ is such a good approximation, and it is also why a system with few governors on free control — one dominated by inverter-connected generation that does not respond to frequency — has a much smaller $\beta$ and far larger frequency excursions for the same load step.

Worked example — two-area tie-line deviation Lab Quiz Q3

$B_1 = 20\ \text{MW/Hz}$, $B_2 = 25\ \text{MW/Hz}$. The steady-state $\Delta f$ after a 100 MW load increase in Area 1 is 0.1 Hz. Find the tie-line power deviation from Area 1 to Area 2.

Take $\Delta f$ as given and apply $\text{ACE}_1 = 0$, the condition AGC drives the area to:

$$\text{ACE}_1 = \Delta P_{\text{tie},1} + B_1 \Delta f_1 = 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; \bigl|\Delta P_{\text{tie},1}\bigr| = B_1 \bigl|\Delta f\bigr| = 20 \times 0.1 = \mathbf{2\ MW}$$

The direction: Area 1 has the extra load, so it imports — the flow is 2 MW from Area 2 into Area 1, which makes $\Delta P_{\text{tie},1}$ negative under the usual sign convention. Always state the direction; a bare "2 MW" is half an answer.

Note that $B_2 = 25$ is not used. Under the $\text{ACE}_1 = 0$ route only area 1's own bias matters, so the other area's figure is a distractor.

Watch out — this question's data is internally inconsistent

If you try to derive $\Delta f$ from the 100 MW step rather than taking it as given, you get

$$\Delta f = \frac{-\Delta P_{D1}}{\beta_1 + \beta_2} = \frac{-100}{45} = -2.22\ \text{Hz}$$

which is nowhere near the 0.1 Hz the question states — and a 2.2 Hz excursion would black the system out. The two pieces of data cannot both be right.

Treat the 0.1 Hz as supplied and use $\text{ACE} = 0$, which is what reproduces the master-copy answer of 2 MW. Do not try to reconcile it with the 100 MW figure.

Note also that the $B$ symbols here are being used as bias settings ($b_i$ in §4.5), not as load damping — 20 and 25 MW/Hz are plausible bias values for whole areas and implausible as damping coefficients. If an exam question mixes them, say which reading you are taking. This is the second $B$ collision the cover warned about.

Exam note — how load-frequency control was actually asked

12 of the midterm's 40 marks, plus two quiz blanks:

  • Midterm Q3 [4 marks] — isolated-area LFC: $K_{ps}$, $T_{ps}$, $\beta$, steady-state $\Delta f$. Exactly §4.3's worked example.
  • Midterm Q5 [8 marks] — two-area: $T_{12}$, $a_{12}$, $\beta_i$, tie-line power with direction, and "interpret both results" — which is the $\text{ACE}_1 = -\Delta P_{D1}$, $\text{ACE}_2 = 0$ result of §4.5.
  • Major Quiz Q10 — droop sharing, worked in §4.4.
