  • Lab Quiz Q3 — the two-area ACE blank, worked above.

The 8-mark two-area question is the one to prepare properly, and the part most people lose is not the arithmetic — it is the interpretation. If you can write "$\text{ACE}_2 = 0$, so Area 2's AGC does not respond; the disturbance appears only in Area 1's ACE, which is why its own AGC corrects it and the tie-line flow returns to schedule", you have the marks that most answers leave on the table.

Checkpoint

An isolated 2000 MW area has $\beta = 400\ \text{MW/Hz}$. A 600 MW generator trips. What is the steady-state frequency, and does under-frequency load shedding — set at 48.8 Hz — operate?

Answer. A generator tripping is equivalent to a load step of $+600\ \text{MW}$, so $\Delta f = -600/400 = \mathbf{-1.5\ Hz}$, settling at 48.5 Hz. That is below the 48.8 Hz setting, so yes, load shedding operates — and it would have operated even sooner, because the nadir is lower than the steady state (Figure 4.1), so the frequency passes 48.8 Hz on the way down within the first second or two.

The engineering reading: this area cannot survive the loss of a 600 MW unit on droop alone. Either the largest unit must be smaller relative to the area, or $\beta$ must be larger — more governors on free control, or more spinning reserve — or the area must be interconnected so that a neighbour's $\beta$ contributes too. That last option is the one real systems take, and it is why §4.5 exists: interconnection is bought primarily for this, not for energy trading. It is also why the spinning-reserve constraint in §3.6's unit commitment is written as "cover the load plus a margin" — the margin is sized by exactly this calculation.

Chapter 5 post-midterm · core

What a Relay Must Decide, and What It Sees

A fault is a few milliseconds of emergency. Somewhere in the network a device has to notice, decide that the trouble is its problem and not the next device's, and act — all before the machines fall out of step. This chapter is about that decision: who makes it, what information they get, and why the information is never the real thing.

5.1 The clock we are now on

Chapters 2 to 4 lived on the slow clocks. Unit commitment thinks in hours, economic dispatch in minutes, load-frequency control in tens of seconds. Nothing in those chapters happens fast enough for you to notice it happening.

Protection lives on the fast clock, and the numbers are brutal. A relay is expected to make its decision in one to two cycles — 20 to 40 milliseconds on a 50 Hz system. The breaker then needs about five cycles, 100 ms, to actually part its contacts and interrupt the arc. Add them up and a well-protected fault is gone in about 140 ms.

Why that number and not some other? Because Chapter 2 already fixed it. The equal-area criterion produced a critical clearing time — for the terminal fault worked in §2.6, $t_{cr} = 0.2213\ \text{s}$. Clear the fault inside that and the machine swings out and comes back. Miss it and the machine never recovers, and you have turned a faulted line into a lost generator.

Key result — the specification protection has to meet

$$t_{\text{relay}} + t_{\text{breaker}} \;<\; t_{cr}$$

Stability analysis computes the right-hand side. Protection engineering delivers the left. That inequality is the entire reason the two halves of this course sit in one syllabus, and it is worth saying in one line in any answer that asks why fast clearing matters.

5.2 The crash team and the backup team

Here is the picture to carry through the next seven chapters. A hospital has a crash team for each ward. When a patient in ward 3 collapses, ward 3's team goes — not the whole hospital. That is the first requirement, and it has a name: selectivity. Disconnect the faulted piece, and only the faulted piece.

But crash teams fail. Someone is off shift, a pager dies, the defibrillator does not charge. So the hospital also has a rule: if ward 3's team has not responded within a set delay, the team from ward 2 next door comes instead. They are slower and they will disturb more patients, because they have to walk through ward 2 to get there. But they come. That is backup protection, and the deliberate delay before it acts is the single most important number in relay coordination. Section 5.12 separates the two kinds of back-up (one in the same substation, one upstream) that the professor's revision sheet distinguishes.

Everything in protection falls out of those two ideas: a primary responder for each piece of equipment, and a slower neighbour who acts only if the primary did not.

5.3 Zones, and why they must overlap

Divide the network into protection zones. Each zone is one piece of equipment — a generator, a busbar, a transformer, a line — with its own relays whose job is to trip for faults inside the zone and stay still for faults outside it.

The boundary of a zone is not a point on a map. It is defined by where the current transformers are, because a relay only knows about current it can measure. And this forces a design decision that catches people out: adjacent zones must overlap, not merely touch.

Why it happens

Suppose two zones met exactly at a single CT. A fault occurring at that precise point — between the CT and the breaker — would be seen by the CT as outside one zone and outside the other. It would sit in a blind spot and nobody would trip for it.

So the zones are drawn to overlap across each breaker, with the CTs on opposite sides. A fault in the overlap is seen by both zones, so both trip. That is wasteful — you disconnect more than you needed to — but a fault that is cleared too broadly is survivable and a fault that is never cleared is not. The overlap is deliberately chosen to fail safe.

5.4 The six things a protection scheme is judged on

Examiners like this list because it is short, it is checkable, and each item has a real engineering meaning rather than being a synonym for the others. Learn what each one trades against, not just its name.

AttributeWhat it meansWhat it costs
Selectivity
(discrimination)		 Trip the smallest possible section — the faulted one only. Needs grading delays, so the upstream relay is deliberately made slower.
Sensitivity Detect the smallest fault current that can occur, including a high-resistance earth fault. Set the pickup too low and normal load or inrush looks like a fault.
Speed Clear inside $t_{cr}$, and limit the $I^2t$ energy dumped into the equipment. Directly fights selectivity — the faster the upstream relay, the worse the grading.
Reliability
(dependability)		 It operates when it should. Measured as "no failure to trip". Achieved with duplicated relays and backup, which costs money and increases the chance of a false trip.
Stability It stays still when it should — most importantly during a through fault, one outside its own zone. The direct opposite of sensitivity. Chapter 9 is almost entirely about buying stability back.
Economy The scheme costs a sensible fraction of what it protects. A 500 MW generator justifies duplicated everything; an 11 kV feeder gets one relay and a fuse.

The professor's end-term sheet counts five requirements rather than six, and splits reliability into dependability and security. Section 5.11 reconciles the two lists.

Watch out — stability means two different things in this course

In Chapters 2 and 4, stability means rotor-angle stability: do the machines stay in step. In Chapters 5 to 11 it means relay stability: does the relay refrain from tripping on a fault outside its zone. The two are unrelated and both appear in this syllabus. If a question says "comment on stability", read which half of the course it is in.

5.5 The coordination time interval, term by term

Back to the backup team. Relay B protects the far section; relay A sits upstream of it and is B's backup. For A to be a genuine backup rather than a nuisance, A must wait long enough that B has definitely had its chance — and no longer.

How long is "definitely had its chance"? Four things have to finish:

Key result — the grading inequality this course uses

$$T_{RA} \;\ge\; T_{RB} + T_{CB,B} + T_{OS,A}$$

where $T_{RA}$ is the upstream relay's operating time, $T_{RB}$ the downstream relay's, $T_{CB,B}$ the downstream breaker's operating time, and $T_{OS,A}$ the upstream relay's overshoot allowance — the time its moving element keeps travelling after the current has already gone.

The course takes overshoot as 10% of everything before it:

$$T_{OS,A} = 0.1\,\bigl(T_{RB} + T_{CB,B}\bigr) \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \boxed{\,T_{RA} \;\ge\; 1.1\,\bigl(T_{RB} + T_{CB,B}\bigr)\,}$$

Typical numbers in this course's own examples: $T_{CB,B} = 0.5\ \text{s}$ and $T_{RB} = 0.2\ \text{s}$, giving $T_{RA} \ge 0.77\ \text{s}$. You will use this exact arithmetic in §7.4.

The revision sheet writes the same margin differently, as a single coordination time interval of about 0.3 to 0.4 s. The two are one idea in two forms; §7.6 reconciles them and says which to use when.

5.6 What the relay actually sees — the current transformer

A relay is a small device with a coil, or these days a microprocessor with a 5 V input. A faulted 132 kV line carries 40,000 amperes. Those two facts cannot meet directly, so everything a relay knows arrives through a current transformer (CT): a transformer whose primary is the line itself and whose secondary delivers a scaled-down replica into the relay.

A CT rated 400/5 A produces 5 A in its secondary when 400 A flows in the line. The ratio is all you need for the step that opens almost every numerical in this half of the course:

Key result — referring a current through a CT

$$I_{\text{secondary}} = I_{\text{primary}} \times \frac{\text{CT secondary rating}} {\text{CT primary rating}} = \frac{I_{\text{primary}}}{\text{CT ratio}}$$

A 400/5 CT has ratio $400/5 = 80$. A 4000 A fault therefore presents $4000/80 = 50\ \text{A}$ to the relay. Every overcurrent, differential and distance numerical in Chapters 6 to 10 begins with this division, and getting it backwards is the most common way to lose a whole question.

Watch out — a gap in your own source material, now partly filled revision sheet p. 1

The tablet notes carry the note "I class missed (CT, PT, OCR)" at the point where instrument transformers were taught. The lecture happened; the notes for it do not exist. So this section and §5.7 were written from standard protection practice. The professor's revision sheet (p. 1) has since confirmed the points it stresses: never open a CT secondary, protection class against metering class, burden, knee-point voltage, and the CVT transient. Those are taught with the sheet as their source in §5.13. The CT-ratio division above is certain either way, because every quiz question in Chapters 6 to 10 uses it and the master-copy answers confirm it.

Why a protective CT is not a metering CT

Both are current transformers. They are built for opposite jobs, and a question on this is really a question about where on the curve each one has to be accurate.

A metering CT feeds an energy meter. It needs to be very accurate around normal load — say 50% to 120% of rating — because that is what the customer is billed for. What it does during a 20-times-rated fault is irrelevant; in fact a metering CT is often designed to saturate early, which protects the delicate meter behind it.

A protective CT has the reverse requirement. Nobody cares whether it is 1% or 3% accurate at normal load, because at normal load the relay is doing nothing. It must stay accurate at twenty times rated current, because that is the only moment it matters. So it is built with a bigger core and a longer linear region.

Why it happens — saturation, in one sentence

A transformer works because flux in the core links primary to secondary, and flux needs iron that is not yet magnetically full. Push enough current and the iron saturates: extra primary current produces almost no extra flux, so the secondary current stops rising. The relay then sees a current far lower than the real fault current — and under-reads a fault, which means it either operates too slowly or not at all. Everything about protective-CT design is the fight to postpone that moment.

Two numbers describe the fight. The burden is the impedance connected to the secondary (the relay plus the leads), in ohms or VA; a heavier burden needs more secondary voltage for the same current, which drives the core towards saturation sooner. The accuracy limit factor (ALF) is the multiple of rated primary current up to which the CT stays within its accuracy class at its rated burden — so a "5P20" CT is 5% accurate up to 20 times rating.

Watch out — never open-circuit a CT secondary

With the secondary short-circuited through a relay, the secondary ampere-turns oppose the primary's and the net core flux stays small. Open the secondary and that opposition vanishes: the full primary ampere-turns now drive the core, which saturates hard, and the induced secondary voltage rises to a dangerous peak — kilovolts on a 5 A winding. This is why CT secondaries are shorted before any work, and it is a standard one-line viva question. The matching PT rule is the opposite: never short-circuit a PT secondary, because a PT is a voltage source behind a small impedance and a short is a fault.

5.7 Voltage transformers, and residual current

A relay that needs to know direction or impedance — Chapters 7 and 10 — needs voltage as well as current. That comes from a potential transformer (PT), usually stepping down to 110 V between phases, or from a capacitive voltage transformer (CVT) at the highest voltages, where a wound PT would be impractically large and a capacitor divider with a tuned reactor is cheaper. A CVT has a price: its stored energy makes its output ring after a sudden voltage collapse (§5.13).

One derived quantity is worth defining now because three later chapters use it. Add the three phase currents:

$$I_{res} = I_a + I_b + I_c$$

In a healthy balanced system the three phasors are 120° apart and equal, so they sum to zero. They also sum to zero for any fault that does not involve earth — a phase-to-phase fault just moves current between phases. So a non-zero $I_{res}$ means one thing: current is returning through the earth. It is three times the zero-sequence current, $I_{res} = 3I_0$, and it is the cleanest earth-fault signal available. Chapter 7 uses it to drive an earth-fault relay; Chapter 10 uses it to correct a distance relay's impedance measurement.

5.8 The R–X plane

Set this up now and Chapter 10's central figure becomes readable instead of mysterious.

A relay fed from both a CT and a PT can divide one by the other. The result has units of ohms and is called the apparent impedance:

$$Z_{app} = \frac{V_{\text{relay}}}{I_{\text{relay}}} = R + jX$$

Plot that complex number as a point, with resistance along the horizontal axis and reactance up the vertical. That picture is the R–X plane (or impedance plane), and the whole of distance protection is drawing regions on it.

Why is it useful? Because the three operating states of a line land in three clearly different places:

ConditionWhat the relay computesWhere it lands
Normal load Full voltage divided by modest load current Far out from the origin, and at a small angle because load is near unity power factor
Fault at distance $x$ along the line Collapsed voltage divided by large fault current Close in, at $x \times Z_{\text{line}}$ — on the line's own impedance vector
Fault behind the relay Same magnitudes, reversed current direction In the opposite quadrant, which is how one relay can be made directional

So the line itself appears on this plane as a straight vector from the origin, at the angle $\theta_L = \arctan(X_L/R_L)$, and a fault partway along the line appears as a point partway along that vector. Measure the length and you have measured the distance to the fault. That is the whole idea of distance protection, and it is why this plane is worth learning before Chapter 10 rather than during it.

5.9 Numerical relays: what "sampling" buys and costs

An electromechanical relay responds to current directly — a disc turns, a contact closes. A numerical relay instead measures the waveform at regular instants, converts each measurement to a number, and computes. Everything else about protection is unchanged; only the implementation moves into software.

The one quantity examined here is the simplest possible one. If the relay samples at frequency $f_s$ and the power system runs at $f$, then each cycle contains

Key result

$$N = \frac{f_s}{f} \quad\text{samples per cycle}$$

Worked example — samples per cycle Major Quiz Q7

A numerical relay samples at 1.92 kHz on a 50 Hz system. How many samples per cycle?

$$N = \frac{1920\ \text{Hz}}{50\ \text{Hz}} = 38.4\ \text{samples per cycle}$$

A non-integer answer is not a mistake and you should not round it. It means the sampling clock is not locked to the power frequency, so successive cycles are sampled at different points on the waveform. Real relays either accept that and correct for it in software, or run a phase-locked loop so that $N$ stays an exact integer — 16, 20 or 32 samples per cycle are the usual choices. If this question had asked for a comment, that is the comment: 38.4 is fine arithmetically but reveals an unsynchronised clock.

Why you cannot just sample slowly

Sampling at $f_s$ means the relay can faithfully reconstruct components only up to $f_s/2$ — the Nyquist frequency. Anything above it does not disappear; it aliases, appearing as a false low-frequency component the relay cannot distinguish from the real thing. A fault waveform is full of high-frequency content, so without protection against this a numerical relay would compute a fundamental-frequency current that is just wrong.

The fix is an anti-aliasing filter: an analogue low-pass filter placed before the sampler, cutting everything above $f_s/2$ so that it never reaches the converter. It must be analogue and it must come first — a digital filter after sampling is too late, because by then the aliased components are indistinguishable from genuine ones.

Checkpoint

A relay samples at 600 Hz on a 50 Hz system. A fault produces a strong 550 Hz component. With no anti-aliasing filter, at what frequency does the relay think it sees that component?

Answer. Nyquist is 300 Hz, so 550 Hz aliases. It reflects about the sampling frequency: $|550 - 600| = 50\ \text{Hz}$. The relay sees it as extra fundamental current — the worst possible outcome, because that is exactly the quantity its trip decision is based on. This is why the filter is not optional.

5.10 The habit that earns marks in every later chapter

Look at how the quiz questions in this course are actually built. Nearly every one asks you to compute a number and then, in a separate blank, state what the relay does:

  • "Actual V/f = ______ kV/Hz. Action: ______"
  • "Its magnitude is ______ Ω. The reach setting is ______ Ω. The relay will ______"
  • "PSM = ______. Final action: ______"

The action blank is worth the same as the arithmetic blank, there is no step marking, and it takes four words to fill. Yet it is the one most often left empty, because a student who has finished the sum feels finished.

5.11 Dependability against security: the five requirements revision sheet p. 1

Think of a smoke alarm. You want it to go off in a fire. You also want it to stay quiet when someone burns the toast. Those are two different wishes, and you cannot buy both for free: make the alarm touchier and it catches every fire but also every toast; make it calmer and the toast is safe but a slow smoulder might get through. A protection relay lives with exactly that bargain, and the professor's sheet says every design decision in Module 4 is a choice of where to sit on it.

The sheet gives five requirements. Each one gets a mechanism here, not just a name:

RequirementIn one lineWhat it trades against
Selectivity Isolate only the faulted element. Speed: grading delays buy selectivity.
Speed Limit damage and keep $t_{\text{relay}} + t_{\text{breaker}} < t_{cr}$ (§5.1). Selectivity and security: a relay that decides in half a cycle has had less evidence.
Sensitivity Detect the minimum fault current. Security: a low pickup also sees load and inrush.
Reliability Two halves: dependability (trips when it must) and security (does not trip when it must not). Each half against the other.
Economy Spend in proportion to what is protected. Everything above costs money.
Why it happens — the two halves of reliability pull apart

Every lever that makes a relay more eager to trip raises dependability and lowers security, and every lever that makes it more cautious does the reverse. The levers are concrete: a lower pickup, a shorter delay, a wider operating region, fewer confirmations required. Redundancy shows the effect most cleanly, as the worked example below does with numbers.

Worked example 2 — trading dependability for security with two relays revision sheet p. 1

One relay fails to trip on a real fault with probability $p_m = 0.01$, and trips wrongly with probability $p_f = 0.02$ per year. Compare two relays wired so that either one can trip ("1-out-of-2") with two wired so that both must agree ("2-out-of-2"). Assume the relays fail independently.

Either trips (1-out-of-2). The fault is missed only if both miss it, and a false trip happens if either one misfires:

$$P_{\text{miss}} = p_m^{2} = 10^{-4} \qquad P_{\text{false}} \approx 2p_f = 0.04\ \text{per year}$$

Both must agree (2-out-of-2). Now the fault is missed if either one misses it, and a false trip needs both to misfire together:

$$P_{\text{miss}} \approx 2p_m = 0.02 \qquad P_{\text{false}} = p_f^{2} = 4\times10^{-4}\ \text{per year}$$

1-out-of-2 cuts the chance of a missed fault a hundredfold (0.01 to 0.0001) and doubles the chance of a wrong trip. 2-out-of-2 does the exact opposite. No wiring improves both at once, which is the sheet's point. Which side to favour depends on cost: a missed fault on a generator is a catastrophe, a wrong trip on a feeder is an inconvenience. The probabilities are illustrative, not course data, and real relays also share failure causes (same battery, same CT), so the true improvement is smaller than the independent-failure arithmetic shows.

Watch out — six requirements or five? notebook §5.4 vs revision sheet p. 1

Your notebook lists six attributes (§5.4): selectivity, sensitivity, speed, reliability, stability, economy. The professor's sheet lists five: selectivity, speed, sensitivity, reliability, economy, and splits reliability into dependability plus security. They do not disagree. Notebook "stability" (a relay that stays still on a through fault) is the security half of reliability. So the five-item answer is the six-item answer with one row folded in.

For the end-term, write the sheet's five with the dependability/security split, and say in one line that stability (no trip on an external fault) is the security half. That answer satisfies either marking scheme.

5.12 Primary, back-up, unit and non-unit: how a scheme is arranged revision sheet pp. 1–2

Section 5.2 introduced the crash team and the neighbour who comes if they fail. The sheet sharpens the second half: there are two kinds of back-up, and they fail differently.

LayerWhere it sitsSpeedWeakness
Primary Its own zone only (§5.3). Fastest. If it or its breaker fails, nothing else acts in that zone.
Local back-up
(breaker-failure relay)		 Same substation, same battery. When the primary has told the breaker to trip and current still flows, it trips every neighbouring breaker on the bus. Fast. Shares failure modes with the primary: one dead battery takes out both.
Remote back-up The upstream relay (an overcurrent relay graded in Chapters 6–7, or Zone 2 and Zone 3 of a distance relay). Slow: it waits out the coordination time of §5.5. Independent of the primary's station, but trips more of the network.

Separately, schemes divide by how their boundary is drawn. Unit schemes (differential protection, Chapter 9) have a boundary fixed by CT positions, so they are absolutely selective and need no time grading, but they say nothing about faults outside the zone: no back-up. Non-unit schemes (overcurrent, distance) respond to quantities that reach past their own element, so they must be graded in time, and that same reach is what lets them act as back-up. Every scheme in the rest of the course is one or the other.

Total clearing time, and where the trip power comes from

A relay does not clear a fault. It closes a small contact; the breaker does the clearing. So

Key result — total fault clearing time

$$t_{\text{clear}} = t_{\text{relay}} + t_{\text{breaker}}$$

$t_{\text{relay}}$ is measurement plus any intentional delay. $t_{\text{breaker}}$ is the mechanism travelling plus the arc being interrupted. It is this sum, not the relay's time alone, that must stay below $t_{cr}$ (§5.1).

The coil that opens the breaker (the trip coil) is fed from the station battery, not from the CT. The CT feeds only the relay's measurement. This is deliberate: a fault can collapse the ac supply, and a relay that needed that supply to trip would fail in exactly the situation it was bought for.

Worked example 3 — clearing time against the stability limit revision sheet p. 1

A relay decides in 1.5 cycles and its breaker needs 5 cycles on a 50 Hz system. The terminal fault of §2.6 has $t_{cr} = 0.2213\ \text{s}$. Does primary protection clear in time? What about local and remote back-up?

One cycle is 20 ms. Primary:

$$t_{\text{clear}} = 1.5(20) + 5(20) = 30 + 100 = 130\ \text{ms} < 221.3\ \text{ms}$$

That leaves $221.3 - 130 = 91.3\ \text{ms}$ of margin. Now suppose the primary breaker fails. A breaker-failure relay typically waits about 150 ms (an illustrative figure: it must exceed the breaker's own time) before ordering neighbouring breakers to open, then those breakers take 100 ms:

$$t_{\text{local}} \approx 30 + 150 + 100 = 280\ \text{ms} > 221.3\ \text{ms}$$

Remote back-up in the Chapter 7 example clears the same fault no earlier than 0.77 s.

Only the primary beats the stability limit, with 91 ms to spare. Local back-up misses it by about 60 ms and remote back-up by more than half a second. Back-up protection limits equipment damage and the size of the outage; it does not save stability. That is the quantitative reason the primary is made fast and duplicated for the expensive equipment.

Watch out — the sheet's "Remember" items for §4(a) revision sheet p. 2
  • Overlap is not a design error. A gap is. Two zones tripping for a fault between CTs is the price of having no unprotected region (§5.3).
  • Unit protection is selective but gives no back-up; non-unit gives back-up but must be graded. Name which one a scheme is before you describe it.
  • The relay does not clear the fault, and relay time is not clearing time. Always add the breaker.

5.13 Reading a CT's data plate: class, burden, knee point revision sheet p. 1

Section 5.6 said a protective CT must stay accurate up to many times rated current. The sheet gives the vocabulary examiners use for that, and each term has a number behind it.

MarkingReads as
5P10 Protection class (the P): error at most 5% (the "composite error") at an accuracy limit factor of 10, meaning up to 10 times rated current at rated burden.
10P20 10% error, up to 20 times rated current. The 20 is the ALF of §5.6.
PS Special class, defined not by a percentage but by its knee-point voltage and the magnetising current there. Used where two CTs must match, as in differential and busbar schemes (Chapter 9).
Metering class Accurate near rated current and built to saturate early, which shields the instrument behind it (§5.6).

Burden is the total VA the secondary must drive: relay plus leads plus meters. At a rated secondary of 5 A, a burden in VA converts to ohms as $Z = \text{VA}/I^2$. The CT guarantees its class only up to its rated burden, because a heavier burden means more voltage is needed for the same current, and the core runs out of flux sooner.

Worked example 4 — what extra burden does to the accuracy limit revision sheet p. 1

A 10P20, 15 VA, 5 A CT has a secondary winding resistance of 0.2 Ω. A long lead run raises the external burden to 1.2 Ω. Estimate the highest current it still reproduces accurately.

The rated external burden is $15/5^{2} = 0.6\ \Omega$. At 20 times rated current the secondary carries $20 \times 5 = 100\ \text{A}$, and the voltage the core must supply is $$E = 100\,(0.6 + 0.2) = 80\ \text{V}.$$ That voltage is the most the core can deliver before it saturates, to first order. With the heavier burden the same 80 V supports only

$$I_{\max} = \frac{80}{1.2 + 0.2} = 57.1\ \text{A} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \text{ALF}_{\text{eff}} = \frac{57.1}{5} = 11.4$$

The lead resistance cut the effective accuracy limit from 20 to about 11. A relay set on the assumption that this CT is linear to 20 times rating would under-read any fault above 11 times: it sees a clipped current and operates slowly or not at all. This is a first-order estimate, standard practice rather than course data; a manufacturer's curve is the exact answer.

The knee point

Drive a CT's core with more and more voltage and the magnetising current needed stays tiny for a while, then climbs steeply as the iron fills with flux. The knee-point voltage $V_k$ is where that bend happens, and the sheet gives the working definition: a 10% rise in voltage needs a 50% rise in magnetising current. Below the knee the CT is a good copy of the primary. Above it, every extra volt costs a lot of current, and that current is stolen from what the relay should have received.

Worked example 5 — reading the knee numerically revision sheet p. 1

A PS-class CT has $V_k = 200\ \text{V}$ with 40 mA magnetising current at the knee. What does the sheet's definition say about 220 V? If this CT drives a 5 Ω burden at a worst-case secondary fault current of 30 A, is the core still below the knee?

At 220 V (10% above the knee), magnetising current is $1.5 \times 40 = 60\ \text{mA}$. For the burden check, the voltage needed is (ignoring winding resistance, to keep the arithmetic visible):

$$V = I\,Z = 30 \times 5 = 150\ \text{V} < V_k = 200\ \text{V}$$

150 V is 25% below the knee, so the core is still in its cheap region and the CT will deliver close to the true current. A burden of 6.7 Ω (or a 40 A fault) would put the demand at the knee, and each further volt would start to cost steep magnetising current. The "10% for 50%" rule is a definition of where the knee is, not a statement about every point of the curve: well above the knee the ratio gets worse.

The CVT transient, and why distance relays under-reach close in

A capacitive voltage transformer (§5.7) stores energy in its capacitors and tuning reactor. When the line voltage suddenly collapses, as it does for a close-in fault, the CVT's output does not fall instantly: the stored energy rings down over a cycle or more. A distance relay divides voltage by current. If the voltage it is given is still too high for the first few milliseconds, the impedance it computes is too large, so a close-in fault looks further away than it is and the relay momentarily under-reaches (Chapter 10). Relay designers add short delays or filtering for this reason, which is one more case of buying security with a little speed.

5.14 Inside the induction-disc relay: the three-torque condition revision sheet pp. 1–2

The IDMT characteristics of Chapter 6 were first built in metal, and the metal explains their shape. An induction-disc relay is a light aluminium disc that can turn between the poles of two magnets. A current coil sets up alternating flux through the disc; that flux induces eddy currents in the disc, and the eddy currents push against the flux to produce a turning force.

Key result — the pickup condition

$$T_{\text{deflecting}} \;>\; T_{\text{controlling}} + T_{\text{damping}}$$

Deflecting torque comes from the current and tries to turn the disc. Controlling torque is from the control spring, which holds the disc at rest. Damping torque is from a permanent magnet that drags on the moving disc, an eddy-current brake, and it is proportional to the disc's speed.

Now the step that makes the exam question easy. A disc that has not yet started to move has speed zero, so its damping torque is zero. At the instant of pickup the condition collapses to

$$T_{\text{deflecting}} > T_{\text{controlling}}$$

so the spring sets the pickup and the magnet sets the time. The spring decides how much current is needed to start the disc. The magnet decides how fast the disc then travels, and therefore how long the contact takes to close.

One more ingredient. A single alternating flux through a disc produces no net turning force; you need two fluxes that are out of phase. Torque goes as $\phi_1\phi_2\sin\theta$, where $\theta$ is the phase angle between them. If $\theta$ is zero, $\sin\theta = 0$ and the torque vanishes whatever the current. The shading coil (a copper ring on part of the pole) delays the flux through that part of the pole and supplies the displacement.

Worked example 6 — why the "$-1$" in the IDMT formula is the spring revision sheet pp. 1–2

Take deflecting torque proportional to $I^2$, with the spring torque exactly equal to it at pickup (PSM = 1), so the spring torque is 1 unit. Find the net torque and the relative operating speed at PSM = 2 and PSM = 8.

Deflecting torque is $\text{PSM}^2$ units. The disc turns only on the excess:

$$T_{\text{net}} = \text{PSM}^2 - 1 \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad T_{\text{net}}(2) = 3,\quad T_{\text{net}}(8) = 63$$

The disc speeds up until the damping brake, proportional to speed, balances the net torque, so steady speed $\propto T_{\text{net}}$ and operating time $\propto 1/T_{\text{net}}$.

$$\frac{t(2)}{t(8)} = \frac{63}{3} = 21$$

The relay is 21 times slower at PSM 2 than at PSM 8, and the expression $1/(\text{PSM}^{2}-1)$ is the extremely inverse formula of §6.4 with $\alpha = 2$. The "$-1$" is the control spring: at PSM 1 the net torque is zero and the disc never moves. Real iron saturates at high current, so torque rises more slowly than $I^2$, which is why the other curves have smaller $\alpha$ and flatten (§6.8). This is a simplified model of the disc, not a complete design calculation.

Watch out — damping is zero at standstill revision sheet p. 2

Asked "what sets the pickup of an induction-disc relay?", the answer is the control spring, not the damping magnet. Pickup is a spring question and operating time is a magnet question. Mixing them up is the mistake the sheet flags.

Exam note — the three-part answer shape

For the rest of this document, every protection worked example ends the same way, and so should yours:

  1. The measured quantity — what the relay computes from its CT and PT inputs, with units.
  2. The setting it is compared against — the threshold, reach or pickup, in the same units.
  3. The comparison, and the action in words — "35.39 Ω > 28.86 Ω, so the fault is outside Zone 1: no trip."

Write the comparison out as an inequality even when it is obvious. It costs one line and it is what turns a number into an answer.

What the revision sheet stresses in this chapter (sheet pp. 1–2): the five requirements with dependability against security (§5.11); overlap by CT placement, primary against local and remote back-up, unit against non-unit schemes, and $t_{\text{clear}} = t_{\text{relay}} + t_{\text{breaker}}$ with a battery-fed trip coil (§5.12); never open a CT, the 5P10/10P20/PS and metering classes, burden, and the knee-point rule (§5.13); and the induction disc, where damping is zero at standstill so the spring sets pickup and the magnet sets time (§5.14).

With that, you have everything a relay has to work with: a scaled current, possibly a scaled voltage, a plane to plot their ratio on, and a clock that says how long it may take. The next six chapters are six different ways of turning those into a trip decision.

Chapter 6 post-midterm · highest yield

Overcurrent Protection: Pickup, PSM, TMS and the IDMT Curves

This is the most-asked topic in the course. It appears three times across the two post-midterm quizzes, it is the method every other relay chapter borrows its arithmetic from, and the whole thing rests on one formula with two numbers in it. Learn this chapter properly and you have the cheapest marks in the paper.

6.1 The simplest possible fault detector, and why it is not enough

A fault draws more current than a load does. So the crudest possible relay is a threshold: if the current exceeds some value, trip. That device exists — it is called an instantaneous overcurrent relay — and it is genuinely used. But used alone it breaks the first requirement from §5.2.

Picture a radial feeder: source → bus A → bus B → bus C, with a relay at each bus. A fault near C is seen by all three relays, because the same current flows through all of them on its way to the fault. If all three are plain thresholds set the same way, all three trip, and you have disconnected the entire feeder to clear a fault on its last section. That is a total loss of selectivity.

So the relays have to be made to act at different times — the nearest one first, the others waiting as backup. There are three ways to arrange that, and this course uses the third.

Grading byHowThe problem with it
Time alone
(definite-time)		 Each relay upstream waits a fixed extra delay. Relay C at 0.1 s, B at 0.6 s, A at 1.1 s. The worst fault — right at the source, where the current is largest and the damage fastest — is cleared by the slowest relay. Exactly backwards.
Current alone Each relay set just above the largest fault current its downstream neighbour can see. Fault current depends on source impedance and network configuration, which change. And near the end of a long line the current barely differs between sections, so the settings cannot be separated.
Both — an inverse time/current characteristic The relay's operating time falls as the current rises. Heavy faults are cleared fast; light ones are cleared slowly, which leaves room to grade. Needs care in setting, which is what the rest of this chapter is about.
Why it happens — the inverse shape is not an engineering convenience

There are two independent reasons the characteristic should be inverse, and it is worth being able to state both.

The damage reason. The heat dumped into a conductor by a fault is $\int i^2 R\,dt$, so for a given tolerable temperature rise the permissible time scales roughly as $1/I^2$. Equipment damage curves are inverse. A protection characteristic that is also inverse sits parallel to them, so one setting protects the equipment across the whole range of fault currents instead of at one point.

The grading reason. The further along a feeder a fault is, the higher the impedance to it and the lower the fault current. An inverse characteristic therefore makes distant faults slow and close faults fast — automatically. The relay nearest the fault always sees the highest multiple of its own setting, so it is always the fastest, without anyone having to arrange it.

6.2 Three settings, and what each one does

Every inverse-time overcurrent relay has exactly three things you can choose, and questions are built out of confusing them. Separate them once, here.

1. The CT ratio — chosen first, and not really a relay setting

The CT scales the line current down to something the relay can accept. You pick it so that the maximum load current produces roughly the relay's rated secondary current (1 A or 5 A). From §5.6, the relay sees $I_{\text{relay}} = I_{\text{primary}}/\text{CT ratio}$.

2. The plug setting — how much current is needed to start the relay

The plug setting (PS) is the relay's own pickup level, expressed as a percentage of its rated secondary current. A plug setting of 125% on a 5 A relay means the relay begins to operate at $1.25 \times 5 = 6.25\ \text{A}$ secondary.

So the pickup current referred back to the primary is $I_{pu} = \text{PS} \times I_{\text{rated}} \times \text{CT ratio}$. Choosing it is a squeeze between two constraints:

Key result — the pickup window

$$I_{L,\max} \;<\; I_{pu} \;<\; I_{f,\min}$$

Above the largest load current — including a reasonable overload margin, which this course takes as 25% — or the relay trips on healthy load. Below the smallest fault current the relay must detect, or it misses real faults. If those two bounds cross, no plug setting works and the scheme has to change.

The plug setting is therefore the sensitivity control.

Watch out — your own notes contain this inequality backwards

Page 5 of the protective-relaying notebook writes the window as $I_{f,\min} < I_{pu} < I_{f,\max}$. That is a slip in the note-taking, and it is contradicted twice within your own material: tablet notes p. 60 and notebook p. 7 both give $I_{L,\max} < I_{pu} < I_{f,\min}$, which is also what the diagram on that same page 5 shows.

Sanity-check it from the physics rather than memorising it. The relay must ignore load and catch faults, so its threshold sits between the biggest thing it ignores and the smallest thing it catches. If your pickup were above $I_{f,\min}$ the relay would sit there while the line burned.

3. The time multiplier setting — how long the relay waits

The time multiplier setting (TMS) is a dial from about 0.05 to 1.0 that scales the entire operating time by a constant factor. It does not change the shape of the characteristic and it does not change the current at which the relay picks up. It slides the whole curve up or down.

Key result — the two settings do two different jobs

$$\textbf{PS} = \text{sensitivity} \qquad\qquad \textbf{TMS} = \text{selectivity}$$

This line is written in your own notes as a general rule, and it is the cleanest one-sentence answer to "what is the difference between plug setting and time multiplier setting". The plug setting decides whether the relay responds at all; the time multiplier decides when, and therefore who goes first.

6.3 PSM — the number the characteristic is actually a function of

A relay does not care about amperes. It cares how far above its own threshold it is being driven, because that is what makes its characteristic reusable across every rating. That ratio is the plug setting multiplier:

Key result — PSM, three equivalent ways to write it

$$\text{PSM} \;=\; \frac{I_{\text{relay}}}{\text{PS} \times I_{\text{rated}}} \;=\; \frac{I_{\text{fault,primary}}}{\text{CT ratio} \times \text{PS} \times I_{\text{rated}}} \;=\; \frac{I_{\text{fault,primary}}}{I_{pu,\text{primary}}}$$

All three are the same thing — fault current as a multiple of pickup current. Work in whichever units the question gives you, but do not mix: both the numerator and the denominator must be on the same side of the CT.

Watch out — the three-way unit trap

This is where marks go. A question gives you a CT ratio like 400/5, a plug setting like 125%, and a fault current like 4000 A. There are now three current scales in play: primary amperes (4000), secondary amperes (50), and multiples of pickup (8). Each looks like a plausible answer and only one is PSM.

The safe habit: get everything onto the secondary side first, in amperes, before you divide. Fault current $4000/80 = 50\ \text{A}$; pickup $1.25 \times 5 = 6.25\ \text{A}$; now divide, and PSM is dimensionless by construction.

6.4 The IDMT formula, and this course's constants

IDMT stands for inverse definite minimum time — inverse over the working range, but flattening to a definite minimum at very high currents, because beyond some multiple there is nothing to be gained by going faster and the relay's own mechanics set a floor. In the professor's revision sheet the floor has a specific cause: the relay's magnetic core saturates, so extra current stops producing extra torque, and that saturation is the "definite minimum time" (§6.8).

Key result — the operating-time formula

$$t_{op} \;=\; \frac{k \times \text{TMS}}{(\text{PSM})^{\alpha} - 1}$$

and this course uses the IEC constants, written out explicitly on page 3 of your protective-relaying notebook and confirmed by the examiner's own marginal notes on the lab quiz:

Characteristic$k$$\alpha$Operating timeUsed for
Normal (standard) inverse0.140.02 $\dfrac{0.14\,\text{TMS}}{\text{PSM}^{0.02} - 1}$ General feeders — the default
Very inverse13.51 $\dfrac{13.5\,\text{TMS}}{\text{PSM} - 1}$ Where fault current varies strongly with fault position
Extremely inverse802 $\dfrac{80\,\text{TMS}}{\text{PSM}^{2} - 1}$ Grading against fuses and transformer inrush, which are themselves very inverse
Watch out — do not use the IEEE constants

Many textbooks and all American practice use a different family (IEEE moderately/very/extremely inverse) with constants like $A = 0.0515$, $B = 0.114$, $p = 0.02$ and an added offset term. Those give different numbers for the same PSM. Your course uses IEC — the three pairs in the table above and nothing else. With no step marking on the quiz, using an IEEE constant scores zero even though the method is identical.

The memory hook is that the three $(k, \alpha)$ pairs are each roughly a factor of six apart in $k$ and go $0.02 \to 1 \to 2$ in $\alpha$: 0.14/0.02, 13.5/1, 80/2. Write them in the margin of your answer sheet in the first minute of the exam.

Why it happens — where the "$-1$" comes from

Notice the denominator is $\text{PSM}^{\alpha} - 1$, not $\text{PSM}^{\alpha}$. The $-1$ forces the right behaviour at the threshold: as $\text{PSM} \to 1$ — that is, as the current falls to exactly the pickup value — the denominator goes to zero and $t_{op} \to \infty$. The relay never operates at its pickup current, only above it, which is precisely what "pickup" should mean.

It also explains why PSM values close to 1 give alarming operating times. At $\text{PSM} = 1.28$ on an extremely inverse curve you will get 25 seconds, and that is not an arithmetic slip — see the worked example in §6.6.

6.5 The three curves, side by side

Plot $t_{op}$ against PSM on logarithmic axes and the three characteristics become three straight-ish lines of different steepness, all diving towards infinity at $\text{PSM} = 1$. The figure below is live: move the sliders and watch the operating point travel.

Checkpoint

Looking at the figure: you need to grade an upstream relay against a downstream fuse, whose melting characteristic is very steep. Which of the three characteristics do you choose, and why?

Answer. Extremely inverse. Grading means keeping your curve above the fuse's across the whole current range, and if your curve is shallower than the fuse's the two will cross — above the crossing your relay would beat the fuse and trip the whole feeder for a fault the fuse should have cleared alone. Matching the steepness keeps the curves roughly parallel so one setting holds everywhere. This is exactly why the extremely inverse curve exists, and it is the standard answer to "where would you use extremely inverse".

6.6 The procedure, and three worked examples

Procedure — operating time of an inverse-time overcurrent relay
  1. Get the CT ratio as a single number. A 400/5 CT has ratio $400/5 = 80$.
  2. Refer the fault current to the secondary. $I_{\text{relay}} = I_f / \text{CT ratio}$.
  3. Compute the pickup in secondary amperes. $I_{pu} = \text{PS} \times I_{\text{rated}}$, where $I_{\text{rated}}$ is 1 A or 5 A as the question states. A plug setting given as "125%" means $\text{PS} = 1.25$.
  4. Divide for PSM. $\text{PSM} = I_{\text{relay}}/I_{pu}$. Check it is dimensionless and greater than 1 — if it is below 1 the relay does not operate at all, and that is the answer.
  5. Pick $(k,\alpha)$ from the characteristic named in the question: 0.14/0.02, 13.5/1 or 80/2.
  6. Substitute. $t_{op} = k\,\text{TMS}/(\text{PSM}^{\alpha}-1)$. Keep four significant figures through the denominator — for normal inverse, $\text{PSM}^{0.02}-1$ is a small number and rounding it early wrecks the answer.
  7. State the action. "The relay operates in $t_{op}$ seconds", or if a coordination time was given, compare and say whether it grades.
Watch out — step 6 is where normal inverse punishes rounding

For $\text{PSM} = 8$: $8^{0.02} = 1.042466$, so the denominator is $0.042466$. Round that to $0.04$ and your answer moves by 6%. Round $8^{0.02}$ to $1.04$ and it moves by 11%. On a no-step-marking quiz that is the difference between a mark and nothing. Carry the power to six figures, or keep it in the calculator and never write it down.

Worked example 1 — normal inverse notebook p.4

A relay with CT ratio 400/5 A is set at a plug setting of 125% with TMS = 0.6. A fault of 4000 A occurs. Find the operating time (inverse relay).

1–2. CT ratio $= 400/5 = 80$, so $I_{\text{relay}} = 4000/80 = 50\ \text{A}$.

3. $I_{pu} = 1.25 \times 5 = 6.25\ \text{A}$.

4. $\text{PSM} = 50/6.25 = 8$.

5–6. "Inverse relay" means normal inverse, so $k = 0.14$, $\alpha = 0.02$:

$$t_{op} = \frac{0.14 \times 0.6}{8^{0.02}-1} = \frac{0.084}{1.042466 - 1} = \frac{0.084}{0.042466} = 1.978\ \text{s}$$

7. The relay operates in 1.98 seconds.

Two seconds sounds slow for a 4000 A fault, and it is — this relay is not the primary protection for that fault, it is somebody's backup. That is what a TMS of 0.6 on a normal inverse curve means in practice: deliberately held back so a faster relay downstream gets first refusal. If this were the primary relay you would expect a TMS near 0.1, giving about 0.33 s. Reading an operating time therefore tells you where in the grading hierarchy a relay sits, which is exactly the kind of comment this examiner asks for.

Worked example 2 — extremely inverse, and a PSM barely above 1 Lab Quiz Q5

A 5 A relay at plug setting 125% with TMS = 0.2 carries a relay current of 8 A. Find the operating time on an extremely inverse characteristic.

No CT ratio is given, and none is needed: the 8 A is already the relay current. This is a deliberate simplification, not an omission — step 2 is skipped.

3. $I_{pu} = 1.25 \times 5 = 6.25\ \text{A}$.

4. $\text{PSM} = 8/6.25 = 1.28$.

5–6. Extremely inverse: $k = 80$, $\alpha = 2$.

$$t_{op} = \frac{80 \times 0.2}{1.28^{2}-1} = \frac{16}{1.6384 - 1} = \frac{16}{0.6384} = 25.06\ \text{s}$$

7. 25.06 seconds.

Twenty-five seconds is not an error and you should resist the urge to "fix" it. PSM is only 1.28 — the current is barely above pickup, which means this is not a fault at all but a modest overload. An extremely inverse relay is meant to ride through that: a cable can carry 128% of its rating for well over a minute, and tripping the feeder for it would be a nuisance. Push PSM to 5 and the same relay operates in $16/24 = 0.67\ \text{s}$. The enormous spread between those two times, from the same relay with the same settings, is the entire point of an extremely inverse characteristic.

Worked example 3 — very inverse, PSM given directly Lab Quiz Q7

PSM = 5, TMS = 0.1, very inverse characteristic. Find the operating time.

Steps 1 to 4 are already done for you. Very inverse: $k = 13.5$, $\alpha = 1$.

$$t_{op} = \frac{13.5 \times 0.1}{5 - 1} = \frac{1.35}{4} = 0.3375\ \text{s}$$

A third of a second, from a relay set to its near-minimum TMS of 0.1 — this is a primary relay doing its job. Note how much easier the arithmetic is when $\alpha = 1$: the denominator is just $\text{PSM}-1$, with no fractional power to evaluate. If a question gives you a free choice and you are short of time, a very inverse calculation is the one to do in your head.

Exam note — how this topic is actually asked

Three appearances across the two post-midterm quizzes, all the same shape:

  • Lab Quiz Q5 — extremely inverse, PSM to be computed from plug setting and relay current. Annotated in the examiner's own hand "(IC = 80, α = 2)".
  • Lab Quiz Q7 — very inverse, PSM given. Annotated "(IC = 13.5, α = 1)".
  • Major Quiz Q4 — PSM as one blank inside a directional-relay question (Chapter 7). The PSM arithmetic is identical; only the surrounding question changes.

What the revision sheet adds (sheet pp. 2–3): the three characteristics and why IDMT flattens, the long-time inverse curve ($K = 120$, $\alpha = 1$), the 0.989 s worked check, when IDMT degenerates, and earth-fault protection by residual and core-balance CTs. They are in §6.8 to §6.10.

Two things follow. First, the examiner writes the constants on the paper for you when he wants a particular curve — so read the margin before deciding which $(k,\alpha)$ to use. Second, PSM is a component of questions that are nominally about something else, so this arithmetic is worth more marks than its own chapter suggests.

6.7 Instantaneous units, and why a relay has both

An IDMT relay is usually fitted with a second, separate element: a plain threshold with no intentional time delay, called the instantaneous or high-set unit. It is set well above the maximum fault current that the next section can produce — typically 1.3 times the maximum fault current at the far end of the protected line.

The two elements then divide the line between them. A fault close to the relay produces current above the high-set threshold, and the instantaneous unit clears it in one cycle. A fault further out produces less current, falls below the high-set threshold, and is left to the IDMT element with its graded delay.

Why it happens — this is the fix for the one flaw in inverse grading

Inverse grading gives you fast clearance for high-current faults relative to the relay's own pickup, but the upstream relay still has to sit behind a grading margin. So a fault right at the source — the most damaging one there is — is still cleared with the full coordination delay stacked on top.

The instantaneous unit removes that. Because it is set above anything the downstream section can produce, it cannot mis-grade: if it sees that much current, the fault must be in its own section, so there is nobody to wait for. It buys back speed exactly where inverse grading is weakest, at the cost of only protecting part of the line.

That trade — speed for a limited reach — is the seed of Chapter 10. A distance relay is essentially an instantaneous unit whose reach is set in ohms instead of amperes, which makes it independent of how much fault current the source happens to supply.

6.8 Instantaneous, definite-time and IDMT: why the curve flattens, and the long-time curve revision sheet pp. 2–3

The professor's sheet names three characteristics. The first two are the ends of the scale; IDMT is the compromise between them.

CharacteristicShapeSet by
Instantaneous No intentional delay; trips the moment current passes the threshold. Above the maximum fault current of the next section down (§6.7).
DTOC (definite-time overcurrent) Flat: once picked up, the delay is a fixed time, whatever the current. Time dial set independently of current.
IDMT (inverse definite minimum time) Falls as current rises (hyperbolic), then flattens to a floor at high current. PS for pickup, TMS for height.

The two knobs differ in kind, which is why they never interfere. Plug setting is electrical: it chooses how much coil current starts the relay, and therefore whether it operates. TMS is mechanical: in a disc relay it sets how far the disc must travel before the contact closes, and therefore how fast. TMS multiplies the whole curve and never changes its shape.

Why it happens — the "definite minimum time" is core saturation

In the induction-disc model of §5.14, the disc's deflecting torque grows with current squared and the time falls as torque rises. That would make the time fall without limit as current grows. It does not, because the relay's magnetic core saturates. Past a certain multiple of pickup, more coil current puts almost no more flux in the iron, torque stops rising, and the disc moves no faster. The time flattens at a floor, the definite minimum time. The floor is a property of the iron, not a setting, and it is the reason IDMT curves have the DTOC-like tail visible at the right of Figure 6.2.

The sheet adds a fourth IEC-style curve to the table of §6.4, for a different job.

Key result — long-time inverse revision sheet p. 2

$$t_{op} = \frac{120\,\text{TMS}}{\text{PSM} - 1} \qquad (K = 120,\ \alpha = 1)$$

Same shape as very inverse, but with $K$ almost nine times larger, so it is very slow. It is used for machine thermal protection: a motor or generator tolerates a moderate overload for minutes, and the relay should be patient for exactly as long as the machine's winding takes to heat.

Worked example 4 — the sheet's normal-inverse check revision sheet p. 2

CT 400/5, plug setting 125%, TMS = 0.30, primary fault current 4000 A, normal inverse. Find the operating time.

Follow the §6.6 procedure. CT ratio $400/5 = 80$, so $I_{\text{sec}} = 4000/80 = 50.0\ \text{A}$. Pickup is $1.25 \times 5 = 6.25\ \text{A}$ (a 125% plug setting on a 5 A relay, not 5 A). So $\text{PSM} = 50/6.25 = 8.00$ and

$$t_{op} = \frac{0.14 \times 0.30}{8^{0.02} - 1} = \frac{0.042}{0.042466} = 0.989\ \text{s}$$

This is Worked example 1 with TMS halved from 0.6 to 0.3, and the time halves from 1.978 s to 0.989 s. That is the proof that TMS only scales the curve: same PSM, same shape, half the height. It is also a practical check on your arithmetic: if your TMS 0.30 answer is not exactly half your TMS 0.60 answer, one of them is wrong.

Worked example 5 — a machine thermal curve revision sheet p. 2

A motor relay uses the long-time inverse curve with TMS = 0.5. Find the operating time at PSM = 2 and at PSM = 4.

$$t(2) = \frac{120 \times 0.5}{2 - 1} = 60\ \text{s} \qquad t(4) = \frac{120 \times 0.5}{4 - 1} = 20\ \text{s}$$

A current at twice pickup is tolerated for a full minute; at four times, 20 seconds. Compare very inverse at TMS 0.5, which would give 6.75 s at PSM 2 and 2.25 s at PSM 4. The long-time curve is about nine times more patient, which is what a heating winding needs. The shape matches the $1/I^2$ heating law only roughly; the curve is a practical match to the machine's thermal limit, not a derivation of it.

6.9 When IDMT stops helping: source impedance dominates revision sheet p. 2 · Paithankar & Bhide §2.7.1

Section 6.1 gave the grading reason for the inverse shape: faults further along a feeder have lower current, so a relay sees a smaller PSM and takes longer. That argument silently assumes the current does fall with distance. It falls only when the line's own impedance is a large part of the total impedance between the source and the fault. If the source impedance dominates, the fault current barely changes from one end of the line to the other, every relay sees nearly the same PSM, and the inverse curve has nothing to work with.

Worked example 6 — the same line behind a strong and a weak source revision sheet p. 2 · P&B §2.7.1

An 11 kV feeder is protected by a normal-inverse relay with a pickup of 500 A primary and TMS 0.1. Compare two cases. (a) Source impedance $Z_s = 5\ \Omega$, line impedance 1 Ω. (b) $Z_s = 0.5\ \Omega$, line impedance 4 Ω. Find the fault current and operating time for a fault at the relay and at the far end.

Phase voltage is $11000/\sqrt{3} = 6351\ \text{V}$, and a three-phase fault current is that divided by the impedance to the fault.

Fault at relayFault at far endRatio
(a) current1270 A1058 A1.2
(a) PSM2.542.12
(a) $t_{op}$0.744 s0.926 s1.2
(b) current12,702 A1411 A9.0
(b) PSM25.42.82
(b) $t_{op}$0.209 s0.668 s3.2

In case (a) the fault takes 0.744 s at the relay and 0.926 s at the far end, a spread of 0.18 s. The curve is not discriminating by position: it behaves like a fixed delay of about 0.8 s, which is what a DTOC relay would do, and a DTOC relay is simpler to set and to explain. Worse, the worst fault (at the source) is still cleared slowly. In case (b) the current drops ninefold along the line, the time more than triples, and IDMT earns its place. The rule from the sheet: IDMT is worth using only when line impedance dominates, so that fault current falls appreciably with distance; where source impedance dominates, use DTOC. The currents here are my illustrative numbers, not course data.

6.10 Earth-fault protection: residual current and the core-balance CT revision sheet p. 3

A phase-overcurrent relay must ignore load, so its pickup sits above the full-load current of the feeder, say 500 A. But many faults involve earth, and a high-resistance earth fault may carry far less than that. To catch them you want a relay that does not see load at all, so its pickup can be very low. The residual current of §5.7 provides it.

Add the three phase currents:

$$I_{\text{res}} = I_a + I_b + I_c = 3I_0$$

For balanced load the three currents cancel, however large they are. For any fault without earth (phase-to-phase, three-phase) they also cancel. Only current returning through the earth fails to cancel. So the earth-fault relay sees zero in normal operation and can be set far more sensitively than the phase relays: a pickup of 10 to 20% of the CT rating instead of 100% or more.

There are two ways to form the sum.

  • Residual connection. Three CTs, one per phase, with their secondaries wired in parallel through the earth-fault relay. The sum is made in the wiring.
  • Core-balance CT. A single ring-shaped CT through which all three conductors pass. The core adds the three fluxes. Balanced currents leave zero net flux; only the residual current links the secondary.

The core-balance CT wins on sensitivity precisely because there is nothing to mismatch: three separate CTs, even from the same batch, differ slightly in magnetising current, and on a heavy phase fault that small difference appears as a spurious residual that the relay must be set above. The cost is mechanical: the ring must fit around the cable or busbar, so it is common on cables and rare on overhead lines.

What limits sensitivity: the neutral earthing

The earth-fault relay can only be as sensitive as the earth-fault current is large, and that is decided by how the system neutral is earthed. A solidly earthed neutral lets thousands of amperes flow. A neutral earthed through a resistance or reactance deliberately limits the fault current to protect the equipment, and a relay that is too insensitive will miss exactly the faults the earthing was designed to allow.

Worked example 7 — can the earth-fault relay see through the neutral resistor? revision sheet p. 3

An 11 kV system has its neutral earthed through a 20 Ω resistor. The feeder CT is 400/5. The phase relay is set at 500 A primary. The earth-fault relay uses the residual connection with a plug setting of 20%. Find the earth-fault pickup, the fault current for a solid earth fault, and whether the relay sees a fault with 60 Ω of fault resistance.

Earth-fault pickup: $0.20 \times 5 = 1\ \text{A}$ secondary, so $1 \times 80 = 80\ \text{A}$ primary. The fault current is limited by the resistor in series with any fault resistance:

$$I_f = \frac{6351}{20 + R_f} \qquad I_f(0) = 318\ \text{A},\quad I_f(60) = \frac{6351}{80} = 79.4\ \text{A}$$

A solid earth fault carries 318 A, which is below the 500 A phase pickup, so the phase relay is blind to it and the earth-fault relay is essential. The earth-fault relay catches it with a PSM of $318/80 = 4.0$. With 60 Ω of fault resistance (a tree branch or dry ground) the current falls to 79.4 A, just under the 80 A pickup, so even this relay misses it. A lower plug setting, a core-balance CT and a neutral resistor sized no larger than needed are the three levers. The values are illustrative, not course data.

Watch out — the sheet's "Remember" items for §4(b), on settings revision sheet p. 3
  • PSM is a multiple of pickup, not of the CT secondary rating. Dividing 50 A by 5 A instead of by 6.25 A gives 10 instead of 8, and the time is wrong by 20%. The sheet calls it the single most common arithmetic slip in the module.
  • PS = 125% on a 5 A relay is 6.25 A, not 5 A.
  • TMS is a multiplier, not a time. Never add a time interval to a TMS (Chapter 7, §7.6).
  • Plug settings come from maximum load plus overload margin, never from fault current. Fault current only checks that the window of §6.2 is open.
Chapter 7 post-midterm · core

Directional Relays, the MTA, and Coordination

Chapter 6 gave every relay a characteristic. This chapter gives them two things they still lack: the ability to tell which way the fault current is flowing, and a disciplined way of deciding who goes first. The second half contains the full worked coordination example from your notes, every number of which reproduces exactly — which makes it the most reliable template in the course.

7.1 Where a plain overcurrent relay is blind

On the radial feeder of §6.1 the fault current only ever flows one way: from the source towards the fault. A relay that measures magnitude alone is enough.

Now connect two feeders in parallel between the same two buses — a perfectly ordinary arrangement, done for reliability — and put a relay at each end of each feeder. A fault on feeder 1 draws current from the source through both feeders: directly down feeder 1, and also down feeder 2, along the far bus and backwards up feeder 1 into the fault.

So the relay at the far end of the healthy feeder 2 sees substantial fault current flowing backwards through it. A magnitude-only relay trips, and now both feeders are gone for a fault on one of them. The whole reliability benefit of the parallel arrangement is destroyed by the protection.

Key result — when directionality is required

A plain overcurrent relay is sufficient only where fault current can flow in one direction. Directional relays are needed on:

  • parallel feeders — the case above;
  • ring mains — current can reach any fault from either way round;
  • any network with generation at more than one point, because infeed from the "downstream" end reverses the flow.

The fix is to add a second condition to the trip decision: operate only if the current magnitude is high and it is flowing away from the busbar into the protected line. The relay becomes an AND of an overcurrent element and a direction element.

7.2 How a relay measures direction

Current magnitude carries no direction information — $|I|$ is the same whichever way it flows. Direction is a relative quantity, so the relay needs a reference against which to compare the current's phase. That reference is called the polarising quantity, and it is normally a voltage from the PT.

The relay then produces a torque proportional to the cosine of the angle between the current and a design direction. From the universal torque equation of §10.2, keeping only the $VI$ term:

Key result — the directional element's torque

$$T \;\propto\; V I \cos(\theta - \tau)$$

where $\theta$ is the angle by which the current lags the polarising voltage, and $\tau$ is the maximum-torque angle (MTA), also called the relay characteristic angle — a design constant set by adding phase shift inside the relay.

The name says what it is: torque is greatest when $\theta = \tau$, because that is when the cosine is 1.

Key result — the four operating criteria, exactly as your notes state them
$\theta = \tau$maximum torque — the relay's most sensitive condition
$\theta = \tau \pm 90°$zero torque — the boundary. The relay does not operate.
$|\theta - \tau| < 90°$positive torque — operate (forward direction)
$|\theta - \tau| > 90°$negative torque — restrain (reverse direction)

These four lines are the complete answer key to Major Quiz Q4, including the one case the question is actually built around. Memorise the second line.

Why it happens — why the boundary is 90° and not 0°

It is tempting to assume the relay distinguishes "forward" from "reverse" by whether the current is within some narrow angle of $\tau$. It does not. The operating region is a full half-plane, 180° wide, centred on $\tau$.

The reason is in the cosine. $\cos(\theta - \tau)$ is positive for the whole range $|\theta - \tau| < 90°$ and negative for the whole range beyond. So the sign of the torque — which is all the relay's contacts can respond to — flips exactly at 90°, and the characteristic on the R–X plane of §5.8 is a straight line through the origin perpendicular to $\tau$ (the "directional" row of the §10.2 table).

A half-plane is exactly what you want. Reversing the current direction shifts $\theta$ by 180°, which takes any point in the operating half-plane to a point in the restraining half-plane, whatever the power factor. So the relay discriminates direction correctly for every load angle, rather than only for a narrow band of them.

7.3 Choosing the polarising voltage

The phase current on the faulted phase collapses in voltage during a fault, which makes it a poor reference — if the polarising voltage goes to zero, so does the torque, exactly when you need it. So the polarising quantity is deliberately taken from a different phase, which stays healthy.

ConnectionCurrentPolarising voltageTypical MTA
30°$I_a$$V_{ac}$0° or 30°
60°$I_a - I_b$$V_{ac}$0° or 30°
90°$I_a$$V_{bc}$30° or 45° — the usual choice for phase faults
Earth fault$I_{\text{res}} = 3I_0$$-3V_0$ (residual voltage, from an open-delta PT)0° to 45° depending on earthing

The names come from the angle between the current and the voltage under unity-power-factor load. The 90° connection with a 30° or 45° MTA is the common one for phase faults, because $V_{bc}$ barely changes during a fault on phase a, giving a reliable reference, and the MTA then positions the operating half-plane to cover the realistic range of fault angles.

For earth faults the reference is the residual voltage, which is zero under healthy conditions and appears only when the system neutral shifts — i.e. precisely during an earth fault. It is the voltage counterpart of the residual current of §5.7, and the pair of them form the standard directional earth-fault element.

Worked example — directional relay with a transformer phase shift Major Quiz Q4

A directional OC relay is on the 11 kV side of a 33/11 kV star–delta transformer (30° phase shift). CT ratio 200/1, plug setting 120% of secondary, MTA = 30° lag. A fault on the 33 kV side causes the fault current to lag the HV bus voltage by 150°. The primary fault current is 300 A. Find the phase angle seen by the relay, the PSM, and the final action.

1 — the phase angle. The current's angle is quoted relative to the HV bus voltage, but the relay sits on the LV side and is polarised from LV voltage. A star–delta transformer shifts by 30°, so the angle the relay sees is the quoted angle corrected by that shift:

$$\theta = 150° - 30° = \mathbf{120°}$$

2 — the PSM, by the §6.3 procedure. Secondary current:

$$I_{\text{relay}} = \frac{300}{200/1} = 1.5\ \text{A}$$

Pickup, at 120% of the 1 A secondary rating:

$$I_{pu} = 1.20 \times 1 = 1.2\ \text{A} \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \text{PSM} = \frac{1.5}{1.2} = \mathbf{1.25}$$

3 — the direction check. With $\tau = 30°$:

$$|\theta - \tau| = |120° - 30°| = \mathbf{90°}$$

which is the zero-torque boundary. From §7.2, $\theta = \tau \pm 90°$ gives no torque.

4 — the action. The overcurrent element is satisfied ($\text{PSM} = 1.25 > 1$), but the directional element produces zero torque, and the relay is an AND of the two. It will not operate.

This is a deliberately placed boundary case, and it is worth seeing why it is a good question. Every intermediate number is unremarkable — a 30° shift, a straightforward PSM — and then the final comparison lands exactly on 90°. A student who has memorised "the boundary is ±90°" without memorising "and the boundary does not operate" will compute 90°, see that it is not greater than 90°, and guess "operate". The answer is the opposite.

Physically, 90° is the knife edge: the current is at right angles to the direction of maximum torque, so its component along that direction is zero and there is nothing to turn the element. In a real relay this condition is unstable and the trip would be unreliable — which is itself the reason schemes are designed so that realistic fault angles sit well inside the half-plane, not on its edge. If the question had asked you to comment, that is the comment.

7.4 Grading, and the general rules

Now the second half of the chapter: given relays with characteristics, how do you choose their settings so the right one goes first? Your notes state the rules as a box, and they are worth reproducing because the worked example follows them step for step.

Key result — the grading rules, as your notes give them
  1. Start from the relay at the tail end — the one furthest from the source — and work back towards the source.
  2. Plug setting: $I_{L,\max} < \text{PS} < I_{f,\min}$ (§6.2), with the course's 25% overload margin applied to $I_{L,\max}$.
  3. TMS of the tail-end relay is set to its minimum, about 0.1 — it has nobody downstream to wait for, so it should be as fast as it can be.
  4. TMS of each upstream relay from $T_{RA} \ge T_{RB} + T_{CB,B} + T_{OS,A}$ (§5.5), or, in the revision sheet's form, $T_{RA} = T_{RB} + \text{CTI}$ with CTI about 0.3 to 0.4 s (§7.6).
  5. PS controls sensitivity; TMS controls selectivity (§6.2).

Rule 1 is the one that makes the procedure work. The settings form a chain in which each relay depends on the one below it, so there is only one end you can start from.

Why it happens — and why you must check at both fault currents

An inverse characteristic means each relay's operating time depends on the fault current, which depends on where the fault is. So "relay A is 0.77 s slower than relay B" is not a single fact — it is different for every fault position.

The grading margin therefore has to hold across the whole range of fault currents in the graded section, from $I_{f,\max}$ (a fault just past the downstream relay) to $I_{f,\min}$ (a fault at the far end). You set the TMS at one and verify at the other, and the one that produces the larger required TMS governs.

Which one wins is not obvious in advance, which is why the verification step is not optional. In the worked example below the maximum-current case governs — but only by a margin, and the notes do the check explicitly.

Procedure — grading two inverse-time relays
  1. Downstream relay's CT and plug setting. Take its maximum load current, apply the 25% margin, and choose the next sensible CT ratio and plug setting so that the pickup is at or just above that.
  2. Upstream relay's CT and plug setting. Its maximum load is the sum of all loads downstream of it. Same 25% margin.
  3. Downstream TMS: set to the minimum, 0.1.
  4. Downstream operating time at $I_{f,\max}$. Compute PSM from its own CT ratio and plug setting, then $t_{op}$ from the IDMT formula.
  5. Required upstream time. $T_{RA} \ge 1.1\,(T_{RB} + T_{CB,B})$ — the 1.1 being the 10% overshoot of §5.5. If the question gives no breaker time or overshoot, use the CTI of §7.6 instead.
  6. Upstream TMS. Compute the upstream relay's PSM at the same fault current — the fault is in the downstream section, so both relays see the same primary current — and invert the IDMT formula: $\text{TMS} = T_{RA}\bigl(\text{PSM}^{\alpha}-1\bigr)/k$.
  7. Verify at $I_{f,\min}$. Repeat steps 4–6 at the minimum fault current and check the chosen TMS still satisfies the margin. If not, use the larger TMS.
Worked example — the full two-relay coordination notebook p.7 · tablet pp. 63–67

Three buses A–B–C in a radial chain fed from A. Load currents 200 A at A, 160 A at B, 80 A at C. Minimum fault currents 3500 / 2000 / 1000 A and maximum 4000 / 3000 / 1500 A at A / B / C respectively. The breaker at B operates in 0.5 s. Use a 25% overload margin and normal inverse relays. Grade relay A against relay B.

1 — relay B's CT and plug setting. Relay B protects the section B–C, so the load it must not trip on is C's 80 A:

$$I_{L,\max} = 80 \times 1.25 = 100\ \text{A} \;\Longrightarrow\; \text{PS} = 100\%,\quad \text{CT}_{RB} = \mathbf{100{:}1}$$

With a 100:1 CT and a 100% plug setting, the pickup referred to the primary is exactly 100 A.

2 — relay A's CT and plug setting. Relay A must carry everything downstream of it, which is B's load plus C's:

$$I_{L,\max} = (160 + 80) \times 1.25 = 240 \times 1.25 = 300\ \text{A} \;\Longrightarrow\; \text{PS} = 100\%,\quad \text{CT}_{RA} = \mathbf{300{:}1}$$

3 — relay B's TMS. It is the tail-end relay, so

$$\text{TMS}_B = \mathbf{0.1}$$

4 — relay B's operating time at maximum fault current. The worst case for coordination is a fault just beyond B, where $I_{f,\max} = 3000\ \text{A}$:

$$\text{PSM}_B = \frac{3000}{100 \times 1} = 30 \qquad t_{op,B} = \frac{0.14 \times 0.1}{30^{0.02} - 1} = \frac{0.014}{0.070383} = 0.1989 \approx \mathbf{0.2\ s}$$

5 — the required time for relay A.

$$T_{OS} = 0.1 \times (0.2 + 0.5) = 0.07\ \text{s}$$

$$T_{RA} \ge 0.2 + 0.5 + 0.07 = \mathbf{0.77\ s}$$

6 — relay A's TMS. The fault is in B's section, so relay A sees the same 3000 A primary current — but through its own 300:1 CT:

$$\text{PSM}_A = \frac{3000}{300} = 10$$

$$0.77 = \frac{0.14 \times \text{TMS}_A}{10^{0.02}-1} \;\Longrightarrow\; \text{TMS}_A = \frac{0.77 \times 0.047129}{0.14} = 0.2592 \approx \mathbf{0.26}$$

7 — verify at minimum fault current. A fault at C gives $I_{f,\min} = 2000\ \text{A}$. Relay B:

$$\text{PSM}_B = \frac{2000}{100} = 20 \qquad t_{op,B} = \frac{0.014}{20^{0.02}-1} = \frac{0.014}{0.061163} = 0.2290 \approx 0.23\ \text{s}$$

so the required upstream time is

$$T_{RA} \ge 0.23 + 0.5 + 0.1(0.73) = \mathbf{0.803\ s}$$

And relay A's actual time at TMS 0.26, with $\text{PSM}_A = 2000/300 = 6.667$:

$$t_{op,A} = \frac{0.14 \times 0.26}{6.667^{0.02}-1} = \frac{0.0364}{0.038671} = \mathbf{0.941\ s}$$

Since $0.941 \ge 0.803$, the margin holds comfortably — the minimum TMS that would have satisfied this case alone is 0.221, which is less than 0.26. So the maximum-fault-current case governs, and $\text{TMS}_A = 0.26$ is the answer.

Two things to take from this. First, the note written beside this example in your own handwriting — "time increased for lower fault current; this is the characteristic of IDMT" — is the whole reason step 7 exists. Relay A's time rose from 0.772 s to 0.941 s as the fault moved away, and relay B's rose too, but at different rates, because they sit at different points on the same curve. Grading is only safe if you check both ends.

Second, notice how little margin there really is. Relay A clears a fault at the far end of B's section in 0.94 s. Chapter 2's critical clearing time for a terminal fault was 0.22 s. Backup protection is nowhere near fast enough to preserve stability — it exists to limit equipment damage once stability has already been lost. That is the honest reading of 0.94 s, and it is why differential protection (Chapter 9) is bought for anything expensive.

Watch out — one number in the question is not used

The data gives a 200 A load at bus A, and the notes' own working never uses it: relay A's setting comes from $(160 + 80) \times 1.25 = 300\ \text{A}$, not from $(200 + 160 + 80) \times 1.25 = 550\ \text{A}$.

The consistent reading is that the 200 A at A is tapped off upstream of relay A, so it does not flow through it. Relay A only ever carries what is downstream of its own CT, which is B's load and C's.

Whichever way the question intends it, the principle is what to carry away: an overcurrent relay's plug setting is governed by the load downstream of its own CT, and nothing else. If an exam question is ambiguous about which loads are downstream, state your reading in one line and proceed — that is exactly what "state all assumptions" on the rubric is asking for.

7.5 Which relays see which fault
