One more table from your notes, worth having because it is the kind of thing that is quick to ask and easy to get wrong under time pressure. On a three-phase feeder you can fit either three overcurrent relays (one per phase) or the cheaper two-phase-plus-earth scheme — relays on phases a and c, plus one earth relay fed from the residual current of §5.7.

Fault3-relay scheme2φ + 1 earth scheme ($R_a$, $R_c$, $R_g$)
a–g$R_a$$R_a$, $R_g$
b–g$R_b$$R_g$ only
c–g$R_c$$R_c$, $R_g$
a–b$R_a$, $R_b$$R_a$ — no earth current
b–c$R_b$, $R_c$$R_c$ — no earth current
c–a$R_a$, $R_c$$R_a$, $R_c$
a–b–g$R_a$, $R_b$$R_a$, $R_g$
b–c–g$R_b$, $R_c$$R_c$, $R_g$
c–a–g$R_a$, $R_c$$R_a$, $R_c$, $R_g$
a–b–call three$R_a$, $R_c$ — balanced, so no zero-sequence component
a–b–c–gall three$R_a$, $R_c$
Key result — why two phases plus earth is enough

Read down the right-hand column: every fault is detected by at least one relay. The one that looks alarming is b–g, caught by the earth relay alone — but it is caught, and that is all that is required.

The reason it works is that any fault not involving earth must involve at least two phases, and any two phases out of three must include either a or c. So the two phase relays between them cover every phase-to-phase fault, and the earth relay covers everything involving ground. Three relays become two plus a cheap residual element.

Note the last two rows: a balanced three-phase fault produces no zero-sequence current, so $I_{\text{res}} = 0$ and the earth relay stays still — correctly, because there is no earth current. It is the phase relays that catch it.

7.6 One margin, two ways to write it: the 1.1 rule and the CTI revision sheet p. 2

Section 7.4 graded relay A against relay B with the rule $T_{RA} \ge 1.1(T_{RB} + T_{CB,B})$. The professor's revision sheet grades the same network with a single number, the coordination time interval (CTI), the gap between B's operating time and A's:

Key result — the CTI

$$\text{CTI} = t_{\text{breaker}} + t_{\text{overshoot}} + t_{\text{errors}} + \text{margin} \;\approx\; 0.3\text{ to }0.4\ \text{s}$$

and the upstream relay is graded to $T_{RA} = T_{RB} + \text{CTI}$, with $\text{CTI} = 0.35\ \text{s}$ in the sheet's own example. The four terms:

  • $t_{\text{breaker}}$ — time for B's breaker to interrupt;
  • $t_{\text{overshoot}}$ — the time A's moving element keeps travelling after the fault has gone (the 10% of §5.5);
  • $t_{\text{errors}}$ — CT errors and relay timing tolerance;
  • margin — a safety allowance on top.
Why it happens — the two forms are the same idea

The 1.1 rule keeps only the first two terms. Expand it: $1.1(T_{RB} + T_{CB,B}) - T_{RB} = T_{CB,B} + 0.1(T_{RB} + T_{CB,B})$. The first piece is the breaker, the second is the overshoot. The CTI adds the other two terms, errors and margin, as a lump. They agree whenever the breaker is fast enough that the lump of errors and margin is the only thing left to add.

The sheet gives only the total. A typical split for a 5-cycle breaker is 0.10 s breaker, 0.10 s overshoot, 0.05 s errors, 0.10 s margin, which sums to 0.35 s. That split is standard practice and mine, not the course's.

Watch out — two coordination margins, two different TMS values notebook p. 7 / tablet pp. 63–67 vs revision sheet p. 2

The notebook's worked example (§7.4) uses a 0.5 s breaker and the 1.1 rule. The margin it demands is $0.77 - 0.20 = 0.57\ \text{s}$, giving $\text{TMS}_A = 0.26$. The revision sheet grades the same network (B at PSM 30, A at PSM 10) with $\text{CTI} = 0.35\ \text{s}$ and gets $\text{TMS}_A = 0.185$. Both are correct for their stated assumptions. They differ because the first assumes a slow (0.5 s) breaker and the second a modern one.

Which to use: if the question gives a breaker time and an overshoot, use the 1.1 rule, as in §7.4. If it gives neither, use CTI = 0.3 to 0.4 s (0.35 s in the professor's example) and say that you did. The sheet is the more recent source and the professor's own, so a question set from it takes the CTI.

Worked example 3 — the sheet's grading check, expanded revision sheet p. 2

Downstream relay B (normal inverse, TMS 0.10) sees a fault at PSM 30. Upstream relay A, with a larger CT ratio, sees the same fault at PSM 10. Use CTI = 0.35 s. Find the time A must take and its TMS. Then check the minimum-fault case, where B's PSM is 20 and A's is 6.667.

B's time.

$$t_B = \frac{0.14 \times 0.10}{30^{0.02} - 1} = \frac{0.014}{0.070383} = 0.199\ \text{s}$$

A's required time. Add the CTI to an operating time (never to a TMS):

$$T_{RA} = 0.199 + 0.35 = 0.549\ \text{s}$$

A's TMS. Invert the IDMT formula at $\text{PSM}_A = 10$:

$$\text{TMS}_A = \frac{T_{RA}\,(10^{0.02} - 1)}{0.14} = \frac{0.549 \times 0.047129}{0.14} = 0.185$$

Check at the minimum fault current. B at PSM 20: $t_B = 0.014/0.061163 = 0.229\ \text{s}$, so A must take at least $0.229 + 0.35 = 0.579\ \text{s}$. A at TMS 0.185, PSM 6.667:

$$t_A = \frac{0.14 \times 0.185}{6.667^{0.02} - 1} = \frac{0.0259}{0.038671} = 0.670\ \text{s}$$

0.670 s is above the 0.579 s needed, so the setting holds at both ends. The tight case is the high-current one: that is where the two curves are closest, which is why the sheet says to grade at the maximum fault current of the bus being backed up. Compare §7.4: the same network with a 0.5 s breaker needed $\text{TMS}_A = 0.26$ and 0.77 s. The difference, 0.57 s against 0.35 s of margin, is the breaker.

Watch out — the sheet's "Remember" items for grading revision sheet p. 3
  • TMS is a multiplier, not a time. Never add the CTI to a TMS. Add it to an operating time, then invert the curve to get the TMS.
  • Grade at maximum fault current, set plugs from maximum load. Two different currents for two different jobs.
  • The relay nearest the source is the slowest. That is the price of selectivity, and the reason IDMT is preferred to DTOC where it helps (§6.9 says when it does not).

7.7 The 90°–τ connection and the cosine of the departure revision sheet p. 3

Section 7.3 listed the 90° connection as the usual choice for phase faults: relay A is fed with the current $I_a$ and the quadrature voltage $V_{bc}$, which stays healthy for a close-in fault on phase a. The sheet states precisely where the maximum torque lands. Since $V_{bc}$ lags $V_a$ by 90°, in a 90°–τ connection the maximum torque occurs when $I_a$ lags $V_a$ by $90° - \tau$. Written with the angle $\varphi$ by which $I_a$ lags $V_a$:

Key result — torque in a 90°–τ connection

$$T \;\propto\; V I \cos\bigl(\varphi - (90° - \tau)\bigr)$$

The torque is a fraction $\cos(\text{departure})$ of its maximum, where the departure is $|\varphi - (90° - \tau)|$.

Why choose τ? A fault on an inductive line has current lagging the phase voltage by roughly 60° to 80°. Choosing τ = 30° puts the maximum torque at 60° lag, right where phase-a faults typically sit; choosing 45° puts it at 45°, for lines with more resistance.

Worked example 4 — the 90°–60° check, and how τ reshapes the answer revision sheet p. 3

A directional relay is connected 90°–60°. A fault current lags $V_a$ by 85°. Find the angle of maximum torque, the departure from it, and the torque as a fraction of the maximum. Repeat for a 90°–30° connection.

90°–60°: maximum torque at $90° - 60° = 30°$ lag. Departure $85° - 30° = 55°$, so

$$\frac{T}{T_{\max}} = \cos 55° = 0.574 = 57.4\%$$

90°–30°: maximum torque at $90° - 30° = 60°$ lag. Departure $85° - 60° = 25°$, so

$$\frac{T}{T_{\max}} = \cos 25° = 0.906 = 90.6\%$$

The same fault produces 57% of maximum torque on the 90°–60° relay and 91% on the 90°–30° one. Both operate (the departure is under 90°), but the second is far more sensitive to this fault because its maximum-torque axis points nearer to where the current actually is. Directional-relay design is therefore the choice of τ so that expected fault angles sit near the axis, not near the zero-torque edge of §7.2.

Watch out — two ways of writing the angle §7.2–7.3 vs revision sheet p. 3

In §7.2 the angle $\theta$ is measured from the relay's polarising voltage, which in the 90° connection is $V_{bc}$, and $\tau$ is the relay characteristic angle. The sheet measures from the phase voltage $V_a$ instead and says maximum torque is at $90° - \tau$ lag. These describe the same relay: the 90° is the gap between $V_a$ and $V_{bc}$. Use the sheet's form when a question names a "90°–τ connection" and gives the current's lag behind $V_a$. Use §7.2's form when the question gives the angle relative to the polarising voltage directly, as Major Quiz Q4 does.

Exam note — how this chapter has been asked

Major Quiz Q4 is the only direct appearance, and it bundles three things into one question: the transformer phase-shift correction, the PSM arithmetic of Chapter 6, and the zero-torque boundary. Three blanks, the last of which is the action.

Coordination itself has not been asked on either quiz — but the full worked example is in your notes with every number checked, your lecturer's "general rules" are boxed, and this examiner demonstrably re-asks his own worked examples (the midterm's Q1 was a lecture example verbatim). Treat §7.4 as a live end-term candidate. It is the most likely long-form protection question on the paper, because it is the only protection topic in your notes that comes with a complete multi-step worked solution of midterm length.

If it appears, the marks are in the structure: tail-end relay first, both plug settings from downstream load with the 25% margin, minimum TMS downstream, the grading inequality with all three terms named, then the verification at the other fault current. That last step is what separates a complete answer from a half one.

What the revision sheet stresses in this chapter (sheet pp. 2–3): grade backwards from the remotest relay with CTI about 0.3 to 0.4 s (0.35 s in the example, TMS 0.185), checked at the maximum fault current (§7.6); and the 90°–τ connection, with maximum torque at $90° - \tau$ lag and the 57.4% check (§7.7). If a question gives a breaker time, use the 1.1 rule of §7.4; if not, use the CTI.

Checkpoint

In the worked example, suppose the breaker at B were replaced with a modern one operating in 0.06 s instead of 0.5 s. What does $\text{TMS}_A$ become, and what has been gained?

Answer. Step 5 becomes $T_{RA} \ge 1.1\,(0.2 + 0.06) = 1.1 \times 0.26 = 0.286\ \text{s}$. Step 6 then gives $\text{TMS}_A = 0.286 \times 0.047129 / 0.14 = 0.0963$ — which is below the minimum settable TMS of about 0.1, so in practice $\text{TMS}_A = 0.1$ and relay A would operate in $0.14 \times 0.1/0.047129 = 0.297\ \text{s}$ for a fault at B.

What has been gained is a factor of about 2.6 in backup clearance time — 0.30 s instead of 0.77 s — for no change to any relay. The breaker was the dominant term in the grading margin all along, which is why faster breakers improve protection everywhere upstream of them and why §11.7's interrupting media matter beyond the breaker itself. Note too that the chain has now hit the floor: the relay cannot be set faster than its minimum TMS, so further breaker improvement would buy nothing here without changing the characteristic.

Chapter 8 post-midterm · core

Transformer and Generator Protection

This chapter sits before differential protection on purpose, which is backwards from most textbooks. The reason is that percentage bias and harmonic restraint are not features in their own right — they are the cure for a specific disease called magnetising inrush. Meet the disease first and the cure stops looking arbitrary. The chapter also contains the single most persuasive number in the course: a 5,000-ampere internal fault that shows up at the terminals as 0.1 A.

8.1 Why a transformer needs its own kind of protection

A transformer has no moving parts, sits in a tank, and fails in ways that the outside world can barely see. Four of its characteristic faults are either invisible to an overcurrent relay or actively mimicked by normal operation:

ConditionWhat an overcurrent relay seesWhat is needed
Interturn fault — a few turns shorted together Almost nothing. See §8.2 — the number is startling. Buchholz, or gas analysis
Magnetising inrush on energisation A large current into one side and none out of the other — i.e. exactly an internal fault Harmonic restraint (§8.5)
Over-fluxing from low frequency or over-voltage Nothing unusual in the current at all A V/f relay (§8.6)
Incipient faults — a hot spot, a loose connection, slow insulation breakdown Nothing, until it becomes a real fault Dissolved-gas analysis (§8.4)

8.2 The interturn fault, and why it is invisible

Take a short between two adjacent turns of a winding. The shorted loop is a single-turn secondary with a near-zero impedance, so the current circulating in it is enormous. But that current is internal — it does not pass through the terminals.

What the terminals see is only the power the fault consumes, because that power has to be supplied from outside. And that is where the arithmetic becomes shocking.

Worked example — the 48,000-to-one problem tablet p.104 · notebook p.22

A transformer on a 415 kV system develops an interturn fault carrying 5 kA internally and dissipating 25 kW. What current rise appears at the terminals?

1 — the phase voltage.

$$V_{ph} = \frac{415\ \text{kV}}{\sqrt 3} = 239.6\ \text{kV}$$

2 — the voltage across the shorted section. The fault dissipates 25 kW while carrying 5 kA, so

$$V_{\text{fault}} = \frac{P}{I_f} = \frac{25{,}000}{5000} = 5\ \text{V} \qquad R_{\text{fault}} = \frac{P}{I_f^2} = \frac{25{,}000}{25 \times 10^{6}} = 1\ \text{m}\Omega$$

Five volts — which is about one turn's worth of voltage on a large transformer. That is the physical picture: a single shorted turn, carrying thousands of amperes through a milliohm.

3 — the terminal current. The 25 kW must arrive through the terminals at 239.6 kV:

$$\Delta I_{\text{terminal}} = \frac{P}{V_{ph}} = \frac{25{,}000}{239{,}600} = \mathbf{0.104\ A}$$

Read the ratio: 5,000 A inside, 0.104 A outside — a factor of roughly 48,000. A transformer of this rating carries hundreds of amperes at full load, so a 0.1 A rise is far below any conceivable overcurrent setting, and well inside normal load variation. It is also far below the spill current a differential relay would need to see (§9.4).

So the fault is undetectable electrically from outside. Yet 25 kW is being dissipated in a milliohm inside an oil tank, which will boil the oil locally and decompose it. The only way to detect it is to look for the consequence rather than the cause — the gas the decomposing oil gives off. That is exactly what Buchholz and dissolved-gas analysis do, and this one calculation is the complete justification for both of them. If a question asks "why is a Buchholz relay necessary when differential protection is already fitted", this is the answer.

8.3 Magnetising inrush — the mechanism

Now the condition that drives the design of Chapter 9. Start from the transformer EMF equation, which your notes give as

Key result — flux follows voltage over frequency

$$E = 4.44\, f\, \phi_m\, N \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \boxed{\;\phi_m \;\propto\; \frac{E}{f}\;}$$

The peak core flux is set by the applied voltage divided by the frequency, and by nothing else the operator controls. That single proportionality drives both §8.3 (inrush) and §8.6 (over-fluxing), which is why they belong in the same chapter.

Why it happens — inrush, in three steps

Step 1 — flux is the integral of voltage. From Faraday, $v = N\,d\phi/dt$, so $\phi(t) = \frac{1}{N}\int v\,dt$. In normal operation with $v = V_m\sin\omega t$ the flux is $-\phi_m\cos\omega t$ — a cosine, lagging the voltage by 90°, swinging symmetrically between $+\phi_m$ and $-\phi_m$.

Step 2 — the switching instant decides the offset. When you energise a transformer you start that integral from zero at whatever point on the voltage wave the breaker happens to close. Close at the voltage peak and the flux starts where the steady-state cosine would be, and nothing unusual happens. Close at a voltage zero — the worst case — and the integral gives

$$\phi(t) = \phi_m\bigl(1 - \cos\omega t\bigr)$$

which never goes negative and reaches $2\phi_m$ — twice the normal peak.

Step 3 — residual flux makes it worse. A core that has been de-energised retains remanent flux $\phi_r$, typically 0.6 to 0.8 of $\phi_m$, because the iron is magnetically hard. That offset adds on top:

$$\phi_{\text{peak}} = 2\phi_m + \phi_r \;\approx\; 2.8\,\phi_m$$

And the core saturates at perhaps $1.2\phi_m$. So the core is driven more than twice as far as saturation, where the magnetising inductance collapses towards the value it would have with no iron at all — and the current needed to produce the demanded flux becomes enormous.

Key result — the four signatures of inrush current
LargePeaks at roughly 6 to 12 times full-load current on the worst switching instant — comparable with a real fault.
OffsetUnidirectional, with a large DC component, because the flux never reverses.
Harmonic-richStrong second harmonic, typically 15–20% of the fundamental revision sheet p. 5 (see the note in §8.5 on the older 15–30% figure), because the waveform is asymmetric. A symmetric waveform cannot contain even harmonics; this one is not symmetric.
DecayingDies away over 0.1 s to several seconds as winding resistance dissipates the DC offset. Larger transformers decay more slowly.
Watch out — one figure in your notes does not stand up

Page 21 of the protective-relaying notebook records inrush as "≈ 20–40% of $I_{FL}$". Taken as written that is wrong by more than an order of magnitude: inrush is a multiple of full-load current, not a fraction of it. If inrush were only a third of full load it would never trouble any relay, and the entire apparatus of harmonic restraint would be unnecessary.

The most likely reading is that the figure is a fraction of the short-circuit current rather than of full-load current — 20–40% of fault level is a realistic inrush magnitude and is a figure that does appear in the literature. Under that reading the note is fine and only its label slipped.

For an exam, quote 6–12 × $I_{FL}$, and if you want to be safe add "or equivalently a substantial fraction of the short-circuit current". Do not revise from the 20–40% figure attached to $I_{FL}$.

8.4 Buchholz, and reading a gas analysis

From §8.2, some faults can only be found by their consequences. Inside an oil-filled transformer, the consequence of any local overheating is that the oil decomposes and gives off gas. A Buchholz relay sits in the pipe between the tank and the conservator and catches that gas. It has two elements:

Upper float — alarm Gas accumulates slowly in the chamber, displacing oil, and the float sinks. Signals a slow, incipient fault — a hot spot, a developing interturn fault. It raises an alarm rather than tripping, because the transformer is still serviceable and you want the chance to take a gas sample.
Lower vane — trip A violent internal fault generates gas fast enough to produce a surge of oil up the pipe, which deflects a vane. Signals a major fault and trips immediately.
Key result — what makes Buchholz special revision sheet p. 5

A Buchholz relay makes no electrical measurement at all. It reacts to gas and to oil flow, which appear before a fault has become an electrical fault. That makes it the only protection that detects incipient internal faults. Slow gas accumulation gives the alarm; a sudden surge of oil gives the trip. Differential and overcurrent relays see only what has already become a fault current.

The gas collected at the alarm stage can then be analysed, and its composition identifies the fault. Your notes give the mapping, and this table appears in exactly one place in your material — page 23 of the notebook — so it is easy to miss. The tablet notes list the same four gas combinations without the diagnoses.

Key result — dissolved-gas analysis, the course's mapping
Gases foundDiagnosis
$\mathrm{H_2}$, $\mathrm{C_2H_2}$ Arcing in oil between constructional parts
$\mathrm{H_2}$, $\mathrm{C_2H_2}$ and $\mathrm{CH_4}$ Fault at the tap changer
$\mathrm{H_2}$, $\mathrm{CH_4}$, $\mathrm{C_2H_4}$ Hot spot in the core
$\mathrm{H_2}$, $\mathrm{CH_4}$, $\mathrm{CO_2}$, $\mathrm{C_3H_6}$ Hot spot in a winding
Why it happens — the chemistry gives you a thermometer

The table is not arbitrary, and knowing the logic means you can reconstruct it rather than memorise four lists.

Transformer oil is a mixture of hydrocarbons. Heat breaks the molecular bonds, and which bonds break depends on how hot it gets — so the gas mixture is a record of the temperature.

  • $\mathrm{H_2}$ — hydrogen appears at the lowest energies, so it is present in every fault. It tells you something is wrong and nothing about what.
  • $\mathrm{CH_4}$, $\mathrm{C_2H_4}$ — methane then ethylene need progressively more energy. Their presence without acetylene indicates sustained thermal heating rather than an arc.
  • $\mathrm{C_2H_2}$ — acetylene requires the highest temperature of all, several thousand kelvin, which only an arc produces. Acetylene is therefore the arcing marker, and its presence changes the diagnosis from "running hot" to "flashing over".
  • $\mathrm{CO_2}$, CO — these come from cellulose, the paper insulation, not from the oil. So their presence places the fault in a winding, which is where the paper is — and that is why the fourth row is the winding row.

So the two questions the gases answer are: arc or heat? (acetylene present or not) and oil or paper? (carbon oxides present or not). Four combinations, four diagnoses. The tap-changer row is the arcing case with thermal gases alongside, which fits a contact that is arcing in service — exactly what a tap changer does.

8.5 Harmonic restraint — the cure for inrush

Chapter 9 will show that no bias slope can separate inrush from an internal fault, because on a bias characteristic the two look identical. From §8.3 we now know what does separate them.

Key result — how inrush is discriminated

Not by magnitude. By second-harmonic content.

Inrush is asymmetric, so it contains a strong even harmonic — typically 15–20% second harmonic revision sheet p. 5. Fault current is close to symmetric and contains very little. So a transformer differential relay measures the second-harmonic fraction of the spill current and blocks tripping while it exceeds a set threshold, usually 15%.

Two alternatives are also used, and are worth naming if a question asks for methods:

  • Waveform discrimination — detect the dead interval marked in Figure 8.1, which fault current does not have.
  • A short time delay of two or three cycles, long enough for the inrush to decay. Cheap, but it throws away the speed that differential protection was bought for.
Watch out — two figures for second harmonic in inrush revision sheet p. 5

Earlier versions of this chapter (and Chapter 9) quoted inrush as 15–30% second harmonic, the wider range that appears in general transformer-protection practice. The professor's revision sheet (p. 5) says 15–20%. They do not describe different physics: the real figure depends on the core steel and the switching instant, and the sheet quotes the usual band. For the end-term, write 15–20%. The restraint setting (block above about 15%) is the same under both.

Watch out — harmonic restraint has a failure mode of its own

Blocking on second harmonic assumes faults do not produce it. Two situations break that assumption, and both are standard viva material:

Modern core steels saturate more sharply, producing inrush with less second harmonic — sometimes below 15%, which lets the relay trip on energisation.

Sympathetic inrush: energising one transformer onto a bus can drive a neighbouring, already-energised transformer partly into saturation, so a healthy unit also shows inrush signatures.

Hence modern relays use cross-blocking — second harmonic in any one phase blocks all three — and combine harmonic restraint with waveform methods rather than relying on one.

8.6 Over-fluxing and the V/f relay

Back to $\phi_m \propto E/f$. If the flux is set by that ratio, then the way to drive a core into saturation in steady state is to make the ratio too large — and there are exactly two ways to do that.

Key result — the two causes of over-fluxing
  1. Low frequency, at normal voltage. Happens on a generator running up or running down, or on an islanded system that has lost generation.
  2. Over-voltage or over-excitation, at normal frequency. Happens on load rejection, or when an automatic voltage regulator misbehaves.

Either way the core saturates in steady state — not for a few cycles as in inrush, but continuously. Flux then spills out of the core into the tank and clamps, where it induces eddy currents in steel that was never designed to carry flux, and the transformer overheats from the outside in. It is a slow, thermal failure, which is why a V/f relay alarms and then trips on a time delay rather than instantaneously.

Over-fluxing also leaves a signature in the current. A core pushed into steady saturation draws a magnetising current that is symmetric but peaky, so it is rich in fifth harmonic rather than second revision sheet p. 5. A transformer differential relay can use this to block on over-fluxing, so the rule is: second harmonic means inrush, fifth harmonic means over-fluxing (Chapter 9, §9.9).

So the protected quantity is the ratio, and the relay measures it directly:

$$\text{V/f relay operates when}\quad \frac{V}{f} \;\;\text{departs from its nominal value by more than a set margin}$$

Why it happens — why neither a voltage relay nor a frequency relay would do

This is the cleanest answer to "why do we need a V/f relay at all?"

An over-voltage relay would miss cause 1 entirely, because the voltage is normal. A frequency relay would miss cause 2, because the frequency is normal. And neither would recognise the genuinely dangerous combination of a modest over-voltage with a modest under-frequency, which multiply together into a serious over-flux while each individually looks acceptable.

Because the damage depends on the ratio and only on the ratio, the relay must measure the ratio. That is the whole argument, and it is worth one sentence in any answer.

Procedure — V/f relay, trip or no trip
  1. Nominal ratio: $(V/f)_{\text{nom}} = V_{\text{rated}} / f_{\text{rated}}$, in kV/Hz.
  2. Threshold: multiply the nominal ratio by the stated percentage. Read the question carefully for the direction — "trips when V/f falls below 85%" is a lower threshold; "trips when V/f exceeds 110%" is an upper one.
  3. Actual ratio: $V_{\text{measured}} / f_{\text{measured}}$, in the same units.
  4. Compare and state the action.

A note on direction. Over-fluxing is caused by V/f being too high, so a real over-fluxing relay has an upper threshold. The quiz question below is phrased with a lower threshold, which is unusual — answer the question as asked, and §8.7 discusses it.

Worked example — V/f relay decision Major Quiz Q2

A V/f relay on a 132 kV, 50 Hz transformer trips when V/f falls below 85% of nominal. The measured values are 105 kV and 45 Hz. Find the nominal ratio, the threshold, the actual ratio, and the action.

1. $\left(\dfrac{V}{f}\right)_{\text{nom}} = \dfrac{132}{50} = \mathbf{2.64\ kV/Hz}$

2. Threshold $= 0.85 \times 2.64 = \mathbf{2.244\ kV/Hz}$

3. Actual $= \dfrac{105}{45} = \mathbf{2.333\ kV/Hz}$

4. $2.333 > 2.244$, so the ratio has not fallen below the threshold: no trip.

Both the voltage and the frequency are down — 105 kV against 132 kV is 80% of nominal, and 45 Hz against 50 Hz is 90% — so on a bare glance the transformer looks to be in trouble. But they are down together, and the ratio is what matters: 2.333 is 88% of nominal, inside the 85% band. The flux is only slightly below normal and the core is fine.

That is the whole point of the relay, and it is what makes this a good question. A voltage relay set at 85% would have tripped on 80% voltage; a frequency relay set at 90% would have been on the edge. Only the ratio gives the right answer, because only the ratio determines the flux. If a question asks you to comment, that is the comment: voltage and frequency fell together, so the flux barely changed.

Watch out — this question's threshold direction is the unusual one

Over-fluxing happens when V/f is too high, so a protective V/f relay normally has an upper setting — typically trip above 110% of nominal. This question specifies tripping when V/f falls below 85%, which protects against under-fluxing.

Under-fluxing is a real condition but a much less dangerous one: the core is merely under-used, the magnetising current falls, and nothing overheats. It matters mainly on generators, where low flux means low internal EMF and a loss of the ability to hold synchronism — which is the loss-of-field condition rather than a transformer problem.

Answer the question as written — it says "falls below", so compare against the lower threshold, and the master copy confirms "no trip". But if an end-term question asks you to explain V/f protection, explain over-fluxing, because that is what the relay is for and what your notes describe.

8.7 Generator protection: the reverse-power relay

A generator is protected against many things; the quiz asks about one, and it is the one with the most interesting mechanism.

Consider a synchronous machine connected to a stiff grid, delivering power. Now the prime mover fails — a steam valve closes, a fuel supply trips, a turbine shaft shears — but the machine stays connected and its field stays energised.

Why it happens — the machine becomes a motor

A synchronous machine connected to a grid stays locked to grid frequency: the grid holds it there. With no mechanical input it cannot slow down, so it keeps turning at synchronous speed — and the only remaining source of the energy to do that is the grid.

So the machine draws real power in and runs as a synchronous motor, dragging the dead turbine round with it. Electrically this is harmless: the machine is perfectly happy as a motor. Mechanically it is destructive — a steam turbine spun without steam overheats its low-pressure blades, which depend on steam flow to carry heat away, and a hydro turbine churning in still water cavitates.

The condition is detected by the one thing that unambiguously changed: the direction of real power flow. A reverse-power relay is a directional relay (Chapter 7) whose polarising arrangement makes it sensitive to real power, set to operate on a small reverse flow — typically 1–5% of rating, since motoring a turbine needs only its windage and friction losses.

Worked example — loss of prime mover Major Quiz Q5

A 25 MVA, 11 kV, 0.9 pf lagging alternator delivers 20 MW to an infinite bus. The prime mover fails while excitation is held constant. What does the accelerating power become, and which relay is used?

From the swing equation of §2.2, the accelerating power is the mechanical input minus the electrical output:

$$P_a = P_m - P_e$$

With the prime mover failed, $P_m = 0$, and the machine was delivering $P_e = 20\ \text{MW}$:

$$P_a = 0 - 20 = \mathbf{-20\ MW}$$

and the protection is the reverse-power relay.

The sign is the answer. A negative accelerating power means the machine is decelerating — being braked by the electrical load it can no longer supply. It falls back in rotor angle until the angle goes negative, at which point the power flow reverses and it begins drawing power instead. The $-20\ \text{MW}$ is the instantaneous value at the moment the prime mover fails, not the steady motoring load: once settled, the machine draws only enough to cover windage and friction, a few per cent of rating, which is why the relay must be set so sensitively.

Note that 20 MW on a 25 MVA, 0.9 pf machine is 89% of its 22.5 MW rating, so this was a heavily loaded unit — and note that the field stays energised, which is what keeps it synchronised and lets it motor rather than falling out of step. Had the field been lost instead, this would be loss-of-field, a different condition with different protection.

On the master copy, "Directional O.C.R" is written underneath "Reverse Power Relay" and also ticked. Both are accepted, and they are the same device viewed two ways — a reverse-power relay is a directional relay set for real power. If you are unsure in a quiz, "reverse power relay" is the specific answer and the safer one.

The rest of the generator's protection, briefly (full treatment in §8.9)

ConditionWhat it doesProtection
Loss of field Excitation fails; the machine loses synchronising torque, slips poles, and induces heavy currents in the rotor An impedance relay on the R–X plane (§10.2), looking for the characteristic locus in the negative-reactance region
Negative sequence Unbalanced loading or an open phase produces a negative-sequence current, which rotates backwards relative to the rotor and so induces double-frequency currents in it — overheating the rotor body fast A negative-sequence relay with an $I_2^2 t$ characteristic, since the heating is again an energy limit
Stator earth fault Insulation failure to the core Restricted earth fault, or neutral-displacement voltage
Over-speed Load rejection with the governor failing to respond Mechanical and electrical over-speed trips

That table is the short version. §8.9 supersedes it with the revision sheet's full list and the numbers behind the two most examinable rows (negative sequence and loss of field).

8.8 Rotor earth-fault protection notes pp. 1–2

The generator's field winding is a DC circuit that spins. It sits on the rotor, gets its current through two slip rings (bronze rings on the shaft, with carbon brushes pressing on them from the stationary side), and is fed from the exciter, a DC source. One design decision drives this whole section: the field circuit is connected to earth nowhere. It floats. The rotor body, by contrast, is earthed through the shaft and bearings.

Why it happens — the first fault is harmless, and that is the problem

Suppose the insulation fails at one point and the winding touches the rotor body. What current flows? None. For current to flow there has to be a closed loop, and the only connection between the field circuit and earth is the fault itself. One wire to earth, with nothing to return through, carries nothing. The machine runs on exactly as before.

But it is now a different machine. One point of its winding is tied to earth, so a second insulation failure anywhere else closes a loop through the rotor body. The stretch of winding between the two faults is short-circuited, and its share of the field current bypasses it. Some poles are now weaker than others, so the magnetic pull on the rotor is no longer balanced. A rotor of many tonnes spinning at 3000 rpm with an unbalanced pull vibrates violently, and the bearings are wrecked. The fault current itself also burns the rotor iron.

So the protection has to catch the first fault: the one that does no damage, gives no symptom, and turns the next fault into a disaster. (The notes give the detection scheme; this reasoning about why is standard machine practice.)

A fault that draws no current cannot trip anything by itself. So the scheme supplies the current. It injects a small DC voltage between the field circuit and earth. On a healthy rotor, the field insulation keeps that loop open, and nothing flows. The moment any point of the winding touches the rotor body, the loop closes, a direct current $I_{dc}$ flows, and an ordinary overcurrent relay sees it.

Two details in the drawing are there for a reason, and both are worth a line in an answer. (Neither reason is written in the notes; both are standard.)

  • Why an isolation transformer. The 50 Hz supply has its own earthed neutral. Connect a rectifier straight to it and the field circuit would be tied to earth through the injection unit, which is exactly the connection the scheme exists to detect. The isolation transformer lets the injection voltage float, so the only possible path to earth is a real fault.
  • Why DC and not AC. The winding and the rotor body face each other across thin insulation over a large area, which makes a capacitor. AC injection would push current through that capacitance even on a perfectly healthy rotor, and the relay would see a standing current. DC cannot pass a capacitor once it has charged, so a healthy rotor reads a clean zero.

The relay is an ordinary overcurrent relay, set with a plug setting and a time setting like any other (Chapter 6); the notes mark both, as PS and TS. Its two outputs reflect a real choice. Many operators only alarm on the first rotor earth fault and arrange a planned shutdown, since the machine is not yet damaged. Others trip at once, because a second fault gives no warning at all.

Checkpoint

A colleague says the rotor earth-fault relay is pointless: "a single earth fault draws no current, so it can't hurt the machine". Is the premise right? Is the conclusion?

Answer. The premise is right: with the field circuit floating, one fault has no return path, so no current flows and nothing is damaged. The conclusion is wrong. That first fault earths one point of the winding, so a second fault anywhere shorts part of the winding through the rotor body, unbalancing the magnetic pull and wrecking the bearings with no warning. The relay exists to catch the harmless first fault, precisely because it is the last moment anything can be done calmly. It needs its own injected DC to do that, because the fault itself supplies no current to detect.

8.9 The generator's full protection list revision sheet p. 6

Why does a generator need so many relays? Because it is the most valuable single item in a power station, and it is the only one that can be damaged by conditions that are not faults at all. A cable is hurt only by a fault. A generator is also hurt by an unbalanced load, by losing its field current, by its turbine losing steam, by being run at the wrong V/f. In every one of those the stator currents can look perfectly ordinary. So the list is long, and each entry watches for a different way of being hurt.

Each protective function carries a standard number from the ANSI (American National Standards Institute) device-number scheme: 87 is differential, 46 is negative sequence, and so on. A letter after the number says what it is applied to (G for generator). Exam answers and relay drawings both use them.

Key result — the generator protection table revision sheet p. 6
ANSIFunctionWhat it detectsPrinciple
87GDifferentialStator phase faults Percentage bias, instantaneous (Chapter 9)
64REFRestricted earth faultFaults near the neutral High impedance (§9.9)
64GStator earth faultThe first 95% of the winding from the terminals Neutral displacement
100%Stator earth fault, 100%The last 5% at the neutral Third-harmonic or injection
46Negative sequenceUnbalanced load, open phase $I_2^2 t = K$
40Loss of excitationField failure, so asynchronous running Offset mho in $-X$
32Reverse powerPrime-mover failure, motoring Directional power (§8.7)
24OverfluxingHigh V/f at low speed V/f (§8.6)
81Over/under frequencyAbnormal system frequency Frequency
78Out of stepPole slipping Impedance locus

Read the table as three groups. Faults inside the stator: 87G, 64REF, 64G, 100%. Abnormal operation of the machine itself: 46, 40, 24. Abnormal operation of the system or the prime mover: 32, 81, 78. The last group is the reason a generator relay can trip a machine that has nothing wrong with it.

Negative-sequence protection (46): a stator current that heats the rotor

Start with what an unbalanced load does. Any set of three unequal phase currents can be split into three balanced sets. The positive-sequence set is the ordinary one: its field turns forward at synchronous speed, exactly with the rotor, so the rotor sees it as a steady field and nothing is induced. The negative-sequence set, $I_2$, has the phases in the opposite order, so its field turns backwards at synchronous speed. The rotor is turning forwards at the same speed. Relative to the rotor that field passes at twice synchronous speed, so it induces currents in the rotor at double frequency, 100 Hz on a 50 Hz system.

At 100 Hz those currents cannot get into the depth of the iron. They crowd into a thin skin of the rotor body and flow through the wedges and retaining rings, where the resistance is high. The heating is fast and localised: a stator current, harmless to the stator, cooks the rotor surface.

Key result — the negative-sequence withstand revision sheet p. 6

$$I_2 = \tfrac{1}{3}\bigl(I_a + a^2 I_b + a\,I_c\bigr), \qquad a = e^{j120^\circ} \qquad\qquad I_2^2\, t = K$$

$I_2$ is in per unit of rated stator current and $t$ in seconds. $K$ is the machine's withstand constant, typically 10 to 40, lower for larger machines. The form is an energy limit: heating goes as $I_2^2$ and the rotor surface has no time to shed heat, so the permissible time falls as the square of $I_2$. Double $I_2$ and the machine survives a quarter as long.

Worked example 4 — one phase loaded, the other two open revision sheet p. 6

A single-phase load takes $I_a = 500\ \text{A}$ with $I_b = I_c = 0$. Find $I_2$.

Put $I_b = I_c = 0$ into the definition:

$$I_2 = \tfrac{1}{3}\bigl(I_a + 0 + 0\bigr) = \frac{500}{3} = \mathbf{166.7\ A}$$

The machine carries 500 A in one phase, but the negative-sequence part is a third of that, 166.7 A. That is the current the rotor feels. Divide by the rated current to get per unit before using $I_2^2 t = K$. The factor is $1/3$ because one phase current splits equally into positive, negative and zero sequence.

Worked example 5 — single-phasing and how long the machine survives revision sheet p. 6

One line of the generator circuit opens, so one phase is open and the other two carry $I = 0.80\ \text{pu}$ (equal and opposite). With $K = 10$, how long may the machine stay in this state?

With one phase open the two others carry $I$ and $-I$: $I_a = I$, $I_b = -I$, $I_c = 0$. Then

$$I_2 = \tfrac{1}{3}\bigl(I - a^2 I\bigr) = \frac{I}{3}\,\bigl|1 - a^2\bigr| = \frac{I}{3}\sqrt{3} = \frac{I}{\sqrt 3} = \frac{0.80}{\sqrt 3} = \mathbf{0.462\ pu}$$

$$t = \frac{K}{I_2^2} = \frac{10}{0.462^2} = \frac{10}{0.2133} = \mathbf{46.9\ s}$$

Now the mistake the sheet warns about. Take $I_2 = 0.80\ \text{pu}$ (the line current itself) and you get $t = 10/0.80^2 = 15.6\ \text{s}$.

The permissible time is 46.9 s. The wrong figure, 15.6 s, is a third of it, and would trip a healthy machine three times too early (the ratio is $\bigl(\sqrt3\bigr)^2 = 3$). Two different situations, two different factors: single-phasing gives $I_2 = I/\sqrt3$; a single-phase load gives $I_2 = I_a/3$. Decide which one the question describes before you substitute.

Loss of excitation (40): detected by impedance, not by current

Lose the field current and the rotor flux fades. The machine can no longer hold its magnetic grip on the system, speeds up slightly above synchronous speed, and runs as an induction generator: still delivering some real power, but now drawing large reactive power from the system to magnetise itself. That depresses system voltage. At the same time the rotor, sliding past the stator field, carries slip-frequency currents and overheats. The stator current may be near normal, so an overcurrent relay is blind. What changes is the ratio of terminal voltage to current. The apparent impedance $Z = V/I$ at the terminals leaves its normal region (resistive and inductive, a generator exporting Q) and swings into the region of negative reactance. So the relay is an offset mho (a circle on the R–X plane, Chapter 10) placed in the $-X$ half.

Key result — the loss-of-excitation characteristic revision sheet p. 6

$$\text{diameter} = X_d, \qquad \text{offset} = \frac{X_d'}{2}\ \text{into}\ -X, \qquad Z_{\text{base}} = \frac{(\text{kV})^2}{\text{MVA}}$$

Here $X_d$ is the synchronous reactance, $X_d'$ the transient reactance, both in per unit on the machine rating; multiply by $Z_{\text{base}}$ to get ohms. The circle begins $X_d'/2$ below the origin and extends a further $X_d$ downwards. The standard rationale: the impedance seen after field loss settles somewhere between about $X_d'$ and $X_d$ below the origin, depending on the loading, and the circle is placed to enclose that range. (This reasoning is standard practice; the sheet gives only the dimensions.)

Worked example 6 — sizing the loss-of-excitation relay revision sheet p. 6

A 150 MVA, 15 kV generator has $X_d = 1.80\ \text{pu}$ and $X_d' = 0.30\ \text{pu}$. Find the diameter, the offset, and the far tip of the mho circle in ohms.

1 — base impedance.

$$Z_{\text{base}} = \frac{15^2}{150} = \mathbf{1.50\ \Omega}$$

2 — diameter and offset.

$$\text{diameter} = 1.80 \times 1.50 = \mathbf{2.70\ \Omega}, \qquad \text{offset} = \frac{0.30}{2} \times 1.50 = 0.15 \times 1.50 = \mathbf{0.225\ \Omega}$$

3 — far tip. It lies the offset plus the diameter below the origin:

$$(0.15 + 1.80)(1.50) = 1.95 \times 1.50 = \mathbf{2.925\ \Omega}$$

The circle runs from 0.225 Ω to 2.925 Ω below the origin, with a diameter of 2.70 Ω (not 2.925 Ω: the diameter is $X_d$ alone, the offset sits on top of it). The relay is not looking at current at all, so it will pick up whenever the machine's apparent impedance lands in this circle, however modest the current.

Stator earth fault (64G and the last 5%): why a resistor makes the neutral blind

The generator neutral is not solidly earthed. It is earthed through a resistance, or through a distribution transformer with a loading resistor on its secondary, chosen to limit the earth-fault current to about 5 to 15 A. The reason is damage: an earth fault burns the stator core where it flashes, and a few amperes burn very little iron where thousands would burn a lot.

That choice has a price, and it falls on faults near the neutral. A fault at a fraction $x$ of the winding from the neutral is driven only by $x$ times the phase voltage, so its current is about $x$ times the terminal-fault current. Take an 11 kV machine whose resistor limits a terminal fault to 10 A: the phase voltage is 6351 V and the resistor is about 635 Ω. A fault 50% of the way from the neutral drives about 5 A; a fault 5% of the way drives about 0.5 A. (These round numbers are an illustration of mine, not the sheet's.)

A neutral-displacement relay (64G) watches the voltage that appears at the neutral, across that resistor. That voltage is roughly $x V_{ph}$, so the relay, which needs a minimum voltage to pick up (a setting of about 5% of $V_{ph}$ is typical), cannot see a fault closer than about 5% to the neutral. It protects about 95% of the winding from the terminals. The last 5% is covered by a separate function. The usual choices are third-harmonic undervoltage (the machine makes a little third harmonic that shows at the neutral in healthy running; a fault near the neutral short-circuits it, so its disappearance is the signal) or low-frequency injection (a small sub-50 Hz voltage injected into the neutral, whose current changes if the stator insulation to earth is breaking down). Together they make the protection 100%.

Watch out — the "Remember" list for generator protection revision sheet p. 6
  • Negative sequence heats the rotor, at double frequency, even though it is a stator current. That is the whole mechanism.
  • Two different factors. Single-phasing: $I_2 = I/\sqrt3$. A single-phase load: $I_2 = I_a/3$. Do not interchange them.
  • Loss of excitation is not an overcurrent condition. It is detected by impedance, in the negative-reactance region.
  • Reverse power protects the turbine (blade overheating when motoring), not the generator. The machine is perfectly happy as a motor (§8.7).
  • The neutral resistor is a trade. It spares the core and blinds the relay to the last 5%.
Exam note — three quiz blanks from this chapter
QuestionAsks forSection
Major Q2Four blanks: nominal V/f, threshold, actual, action§8.6
Major Q5Two blanks: accelerating power, relay name§8.7
Lab Q6Transformer percentage bias — worked in §9.6Ch. 9

Both Major-quiz questions are one-line substitutions with the action or the device name as a separate blank, which is this examiner's consistent pattern (§5.10). The parts of this chapter that have not been asked on a quiz but are fully written up in your notes — the inrush mechanism, the DGA table, the interturn-fault calculation, and now the rotor earth-fault scheme of §8.8 — are the ones most likely to appear as a longer end-term question, since they come with derivations rather than just numbers. The DGA table in particular is pure recall and exists in only one place in your material, so it is worth copying onto a revision card.

What the revision sheet emphasises here revision sheet pp. 5–6: the generator list of §8.9 (the ANSI table, $I_2^2 t = K$ with both worked checks, the loss-of-excitation offset mho with its three dimensions, and the 95%/100% stator earth fault), plus second harmonic means inrush, fifth harmonic means over-fluxing, and Buchholz alarm versus trip. Its checks are one-line substitutions, so these are likely exam numbers.

Checkpoint

A transformer differential relay trips every time the transformer is energised, but never spuriously in service. A colleague proposes raising the bias slope from 30% to 50%. Will that fix it? If not, what will?

Answer. No, and the reason is the whole point of this chapter's placement. Inrush draws current into one winding and nothing out of the other, so the spill is the full inrush current while the restraint is only half of it — which on Figure 9.2 is the internal fault trajectory, climbing at twice the rate it travels right. Raising the slope from 0.3 to 0.5 shifts the line a little but the trajectory still crosses it, because no slope less than 2 can lie above a trajectory of gradient 2. Raising the slope that far would also destroy the relay's sensitivity to real faults.

The fix is second-harmonic restraint (§8.5): block tripping while the spill current's second-harmonic content exceeds about 15%, which inrush has and fault current does not. If the relay already has harmonic restraint and still mis-trips, the threshold is set too high for this transformer's core steel — lower it, or add waveform discrimination, or as a last resort delay the differential element by two or three cycles on energisation only.

Chapter 9 post-midterm · highest yield

Differential Protection and Percentage Bias

The protection schemes in Chapters 6 and 7 all worked by asking "is the current too big?", and then had to be graded against each other because a big current is visible from a long way off. This chapter asks a completely different question, one whose answer is only ever yes inside one specific zone — which means no grading, and no delay. It is the fastest protection there is, and the reason it needs an entire chapter is that the obvious implementation does not work.

9.1 Kirchhoff as a protection principle

Take any piece of equipment — a transformer winding, a busbar, a generator stator, a short cable. Put a current transformer on every conductor entering or leaving it. Now add up what the CTs say.

Kirchhoff's current law says the sum must be zero. Whatever flows in has to flow out, because the equipment does not store charge. So:

Key result — the differential principle

$$I_{\text{diff}} \;=\; \left|\;\sum_{\text{all terminals}} I \;\right| \;=\; 0 \qquad \text{for a healthy zone}$$

A non-zero sum means current is leaving the zone by a path you did not put a CT on. There is only one such path: through the fault.

So the differential current — also called the spill current, because it is what spills into the relay instead of circulating between the CTs — is a direct measurement of the fault current inside the zone, and it is zero for anything outside. That is the whole idea.

Why it happens — why this needs no grading at all

This is the property that makes differential protection special, and it is worth being able to state in one sentence.

An overcurrent relay at bus A sees a fault at bus C, because the fault current flows through A on its way there. That is unavoidable, and it is why Chapter 7 had to insert deliberate delays so that C's relay goes first.

A differential relay on zone A sees a fault at C as current flowing in one terminal and out another — sum zero, nothing to report. It is inherently selective: it cannot see outside its own zone, so there is nobody to grade against and no reason to wait. Differential protection therefore operates in one to two cycles with no intentional time delay whatsoever, which is why it is the primary protection on every expensive item in a substation.

The price is that it needs CTs on every terminal and a pilot connection between them, so it only pays for equipment worth protecting that thoroughly — which is also the answer to "why not use it everywhere?"

9.2 Why the obvious implementation fails

Connect the two CT secondaries so their currents circulate, put the relay across the midpoint, and set it to trip on anything above a small fixed threshold. For a healthy zone the two secondary currents are equal and opposite, they circulate, and nothing flows through the relay.

That works right up until a fault occurs outside the zone. Then a very large current flows in one terminal and out the other — a through fault. The sum is still zero in principle, so in principle nothing happens. In practice the two CTs disagree slightly, and 1% of 2,000 A is 20 A.

9.3 The four sources of spill current

A real scheme produces non-zero spill on a healthy zone for four distinct reasons, and a question asking you to "list the causes of spill current" wants these.

SourceMechanismDoes it grow with fault current?
CT ratio error Two CTs of the same nominal ratio are never identical. Each has its own magnetising current, which is the part of the primary current that magnetises the core instead of appearing in the secondary. Yes — roughly in proportion.
CT saturation From §5.6: past the knee the secondary current stops following the primary. The two CTs will not saturate identically, because their burdens and remanent flux differ. Yes, and worse than proportionally — this is the dominant source at high fault current, and it is what Figure 9.1 shows.
Tap changer position A transformer with an on-load tap changer has a variable turns ratio. The CTs were matched for the nominal tap, so moving the tap by ±10% creates a genuine 10% mismatch between the two sides. Yes — it is a fixed percentage, so the absolute spill grows with load.
Magnetising inrush From Chapter 8: energising a transformer draws a large current into one side and nothing out of the other, because it is all going into magnetising the core. To a differential relay that is indistinguishable from an internal fault. Not applicable — it is a transient, and it is why §9.7 exists.
Key result — the pattern in that last column

Three of the four sources produce a spill current that grows with the through current. That single observation is the entire design of percentage bias: if the spurious spill grows with the through current, then make the trip threshold grow with it too, and faster.

9.4 The bias characteristic

Replace the fixed threshold with one that rises as the through current rises. Measure the through current by the average of what the two CTs say — call that the restraint or bias current — and set the threshold proportional to it.

Key result — the biased differential characteristic

The relay operates when

$$\underbrace{|I_1 - I_2|}_{\text{spill, } I_{\text{diff}}} \;>\; \underbrace{k_0}_{\substack{\text{minimum}\\ \text{pickup}}} \;+\; k\,\underbrace{\frac{I_1 + I_2}{2}}_{\text{restraint, } I_{\text{bias}}}$$

which is the form written and boxed on page 16 of your protective-relaying notebook, there with $k = N_r/N_0$ — the turns ratio of the restraint winding to the operating winding, which is how the bias slope is physically set in an electromechanical relay.

$k$ is the bias slope or percentage bias, usually quoted as a percentage: $k = 0.5$ is "50% bias". $k_0$ is the minimum pickup, which stops the relay operating on tiny spills at no load.

Why it happens — what the slope actually buys

Plot spill against restraint, as in Figure 9.2. The characteristic is a straight line of intercept $k_0$ and slope $k$, and it divides the plane into two regions.

A through fault drives the restraint current up hard — it is the average of two large currents — while the spill rises only as the CT error does. So the operating point moves along the horizontal axis much faster than it climbs, and it stays comfortably in the restrain region no matter how large the through fault becomes.

An internal fault does the opposite. Current flows in one terminal and out through the fault, so one CT reads large and the other reads little or nothing. The spill is almost the whole of $I_1$, while the restraint is only half of it — so the point climbs at twice the rate it moves right, and crosses the line almost immediately.

That is the mechanism in one sentence: a through fault doubles the restraint for every unit of spill; an internal fault halves it. The slope $k$ is chosen to sit between the two.

And what the slope costs: sensitivity. A steeper bias tolerates more CT error but needs a bigger internal fault before it will act, so a high-resistance internal fault at high load can hide under a steep characteristic. Typical settings are 20% to 40% for a generator and 20% to 50% for a transformer with a tap changer — the tap changer buys the larger number.

9.5 Restraint conventions — and which one this course uses

Watch out — there are three definitions of restraint current in circulation

Textbooks differ, and so do relay manufacturers. The three you will meet:

Convention$I_{\text{bias}}$Effect on the threshold
Average — this course$\dfrac{I_1 + I_2}{2}$ The standard. A 50% slope means the spill must exceed half the average.
Sum$I_1 + I_2$ Doubles the restraint, so the same physical characteristic needs half the quoted $k$. A 50% slope here is a 100% slope above.
Maximum$\max(I_1, I_2)$ Common in numerical relays; close to the average for a through fault, twice it for an internal one.

Use the average. It is what your notes write, and it is what reproduces the master-copy answer: Major Quiz Q1 gives restraint $= (10+8+6)/2 = 12\ \text{A}$, which is the sum of the three terminal currents divided by two. The sum convention would have given 24 A and the wrong threshold.

Note how the convention generalises to more than two terminals: it is $\tfrac12\sum |I_n|$ over all terminals, not "the average of two numbers". For a three-feeder busbar that is $(10+8+6)/2$, not $(10+8+6)/3$. Getting that wrong on a busbar question is the most likely way to lose Q1.

9.6 The procedure, and both quiz questions worked

Procedure — biased differential relay, trip or no trip
  1. Get every terminal current onto the secondary side. Divide each primary current by its own CT ratio — the ratios may differ between terminals, and on a transformer they will (§9.7).
  2. Compute the spill. $I_{\text{diff}} = \bigl|\sum_n I_n\bigr|$, respecting direction: currents into the zone are positive, currents out are negative. If the question hands you "the differential current", this step is done.
  3. Compute the restraint. $I_{\text{bias}} = \tfrac12 \sum_n |I_n|$ — the sum of the magnitudes, halved. Magnitudes, so direction does not cancel here.
  4. Compute the threshold. $I_{\text{threshold}} = k_0 + k\,I_{\text{bias}}$. Convert a percentage bias to a fraction first: "50% bias" is $k = 0.5$.
  5. Compare, and state the action. Spill greater than threshold $\Rightarrow$ trip. Write the inequality out.
Worked example 1 — busbar differential Major Quiz Q1

A busbar differential scheme has three feeders with secondary currents 10 A, 8 A and 6 A. The relay has a 50% bias and a pickup setting $k_0 = 0.5\ \text{A}$. The measured differential (spill) current is 5.2 A. Find the restraint current and the operating threshold.

1. The currents are already secondary values — no CT division needed.

2. The spill is given: $I_{\text{diff}} = 5.2\ \text{A}$.

3. Restraint, summing all three magnitudes and halving:

$$I_{\text{bias}} = \frac{10 + 8 + 6}{2} = \frac{24}{2} = \mathbf{12\ A}$$

4. Threshold, with $k = 0.50$:

$$I_{\text{threshold}} = 0.5 + 0.50 \times 12 = 0.5 + 6.0 = \mathbf{6.5\ A}$$

5. $5.2\ \text{A} < 6.5\ \text{A}$, so the relay does not operate.

The quiz asked only for the two numbers, but the action is the point and it is worth one line. A spill of 5.2 A on a busbar carrying 24 A of secondary current is 22% mismatch — far too much for healthy CTs, but entirely plausible for CTs partly saturated by a heavy through fault, which is precisely the condition the bias exists to ride through. Had this been a fixed-threshold relay set at 1 A, it would have tripped the whole busbar and taken all three feeders with it. Note also that the answer turns on which restraint convention you use: the sum convention gives 24 A, a threshold of 12.5 A, and the same verdict — but on a question where the spill sat between 6.5 and 12.5, the two conventions would disagree, so state which one you are using.

Worked example 2 — percentage bias from a measurement Lab Quiz Q6

An 11/132 kV transformer has CTs of 600/5 on the LV side and 40/1 on the HV side. During a through fault the differential current is 0.1 A and the average restraining current is 1.0 A. Find the percentage bias.

Here the question runs the characteristic backwards: it gives you a point and asks for the slope through it. Ignoring $k_0$, which is not given,

$$k = \frac{I_{\text{diff}}}{I_{\text{bias}}} = \frac{0.1}{1.0} = 0.10 \;\Longrightarrow\; \mathbf{10\%}$$

The two CT ratios are a distractor — they appear in the question, they look essential, and they are not used at all. Both currents are already given on the secondary side and the ratio of two secondary currents is what the percentage bias is. Spotting a distractor quickly is worth real time on a quiz with two minutes per question.

As for what 10% means: this is the bias the relay would need just to ride through the mismatch observed on this particular through fault. A real setting must exceed the worst case — the largest through fault, with the tap changer at its extreme and the CTs at their most saturated — which is why practical settings are 20% to 50% rather than 10%. If this question had asked you to comment, that is the comment: 10% is the measured requirement, so the setting must be above it with margin.

9.7 Transformer differential — the three extra problems

Applying this to a transformer rather than a busbar introduces three complications that do not exist anywhere else, and transformer differential questions are built out of them.

1. The CT ratios must undo the transformer ratio

An 11/132 kV transformer carries twelve times more current on the LV side than the HV side. The two CTs therefore cannot have the same ratio — they must be chosen so that their secondary currents match under healthy conditions.

Key result — matching CTs across a transformer

$$\frac{\text{CT ratio}_{LV}}{\text{CT ratio}_{HV}} \;=\; \frac{I_{LV}}{I_{HV}} \;=\; \frac{V_{HV}}{V_{LV}}$$

Check this against Lab Q6's numbers: the voltage ratio is $132/11 = 12$. The CT ratios are $600/5 = 120$ and $40/1 = 40$, whose ratio is $120/40 = 3$ — which is not 12. So those CTs are not matched, and some of the mismatch the question reports is built into the scheme rather than caused by the fault. Real schemes correct the residue with interposing CTs or, in a numerical relay, with a software ratio-correction factor.

2. A star–delta transformer introduces a 30° phase shift

From §7.3: a star–delta winding shifts the line currents by 30°. Two currents equal in magnitude but 30° apart do not sum to zero — their difference has magnitude $2\sin(15°) = 0.518$ times the current, so a 52% spurious spill. The fix is to connect the CTs on the star side in delta, which applies the matching 30° shift in the secondary circuit; a numerical relay does the same thing arithmetically. The sheet's example is the Dy11 transformer (delta on the HV side, star on the LV side, vector group number 11, a 30° shift): star-side CTs in delta and delta-side CTs in star revision sheet p. 4. A fourth problem, zero-sequence current, is added in §9.9 below.

3. Magnetising inrush looks exactly like an internal fault

This is Chapter 8's material arriving where it matters. Energising a transformer draws a large current in one side and nothing out of the other — spill equal to the whole inrush current, restraint only half of it. On Figure 9.2 that is the internal fault trajectory exactly, and no bias slope can separate them, because the two conditions are genuinely identical as far as a bias characteristic can see.

Key result — how inrush is actually discriminated

Not by magnitude, because magnitude cannot do it. By harmonic content. From §8.3, inrush current is a half-cycle-saturated waveform, rich in second harmonic — typically 15% to 20% of the fundamental revision sheet p. 5 (the older 15–30% figure is discussed in §8.5). Fault current is close to sinusoidal and has very little.

So a transformer differential relay includes a second-harmonic restraint: it measures the second-harmonic content of the spill current and blocks tripping while that fraction exceeds a set figure, typically 15%. Hence the ordering of these chapters — the cure only makes sense once you have met the disease.

Two alternatives are also used and are worth naming: waveform discrimination, which looks for the characteristic dead interval in each cycle of an inrush waveform, and a simple time delay of two or three cycles, which is cheap but gives away the one thing differential protection was bought for.

9.8 The dual-slope characteristic and the stability ratio revision sheet p. 4

One straight line forces a compromise. A gentle slope (say 25%) makes the relay sensitive to small internal faults, but at very high through current the CTs saturate and their error stops being a fixed percentage; it grows faster than the current. A gentle slope then lets a saturating CT trip a healthy zone. A steep slope (say 50%) is safe at high current, but it is also steep at low current, where it throws away sensitivity for no reason.

The fix is to use both. Keep the gentle slope where the CTs are still behaving, and steepen it only once the through current is large enough that saturation is a real danger. The current at which the slope changes is the breakpoint (BP).

Key result — the dual-slope threshold revision sheet p. 4

Write the restraint as $I_{rt}$ (the same average of Section 9.4). The relay trips when $I_{op} > I_{\text{threshold}}$, where

$$I_{\text{threshold}} = \begin{cases} \max\bigl(I_{\text{pickup}},\ \text{SLP1}\cdot I_{rt}\bigr) & I_{rt} \le \text{BP}\\[4pt] \text{SLP1}\cdot \text{BP} \;+\; \text{SLP2}\,\bigl(I_{rt} - \text{BP}\bigr) & I_{rt} > \text{BP} \end{cases}$$

Typical values: SLP1 = 20–30%, SLP2 = 50–80%. The second slope applies only to the part of the restraint beyond the breakpoint. The first slope is not discarded: the first term is the threshold already earned at the breakpoint, and the second slope adds to it. The two segments meet at the breakpoint, so the characteristic has no jump.

Watch out — two ways of writing the same floor Major Quiz Q1 revision sheet p. 4

§9.4 writes the characteristic as $k_0 + k\,I_{\text{bias}}$ (a line with an intercept, the form in the notebook and in Major Quiz Q1). The sheet writes $\max(I_{\text{pickup}},\, K I_{rt})$ (a flat floor, then a sloped line). Both give the relay a minimum pickup and a percentage bias; they differ only at small restraint, where the first adds the floor to the slope and the second takes whichever is larger. They are not in conflict: use the form the question gives. For quiz-style problems that state a "pickup setting" and a "bias" together, use §9.4 as before. For a breakpoint question, use the box above. Also, the sheet allows $I_{rt}$ as the average or the sum "by convention", which is the §9.5 caution again: state your convention.

Worked example 3 — dual slope, and the error it prevents revision sheet p. 4

(a) A relay sees $I_1 = 1.20\ \text{pu}$ and $I_2 = 0.80\ \text{pu}$. Find $I_{op}$, $I_{rt}$ and the bias. (b) The relay has SLP1 = 25% up to a breakpoint of 2.0 pu and SLP2 = 50% beyond. What is the threshold at $I_{rt} = 2.2\ \text{pu}$?

(a)

$$I_{op} = |1.20 - 0.80| = 0.40\ \text{pu}, \qquad I_{rt} = \frac{1.20 + 0.80}{2} = 1.00\ \text{pu}, \qquad \frac{I_{op}}{I_{rt}} = 40\%$$

Against the first slope, the threshold at $I_{rt} = 1.00$ is $0.25 \times 1.00 = 0.25$ (taking the pickup floor to be below that). $0.40 > 0.25$, so this reading is in the operate region.

(b) $I_{rt} = 2.2$ is beyond the breakpoint of 2.0, by 0.2:

$$I_{\text{threshold}} = 0.25(2.0) + 0.50(0.2) = 0.50 + 0.10 = \mathbf{0.60\ pu}$$

The tempting wrong answer applies the second slope to the whole restraint: $0.50 \times 2.2 = 1.10\ \text{pu}$.

The true threshold is 0.60 pu, not 1.10 pu. The wrong method nearly doubles the threshold, so the relay would need an internal fault almost twice as big before it acted. That is the price of misapplying the steep slope: you pay in sensitivity for restraint you never needed at that part of the curve. Check yourself at the breakpoint: both formulas give 0.50 pu there, and the threshold should rise smoothly from it.

The stability ratio

How good is the stability of a differential scheme? One number summarises it: how much through-fault current the scheme withstands, compared with the smallest fault current it will operate on.

Key result — stability ratio revision sheet p. 4

$$\text{Stability ratio} = \frac{\text{maximum through-fault current without maloperation}} {\text{minimum operating current}}$$

It is always much greater than 1. Both currents must be referred to the same side of the transformer before dividing, because the same fault gives a different number of amperes on each side.

Worked example 4 — stability ratio across a 132/33 kV transformer revision sheet p. 5

A 132/33 kV transformer differential scheme remains stable for a through fault of 12 kA on the 33 kV side. The relay's minimum operating current is 50 A on the 132 kV side. Find the stability ratio. (Values chosen to show the trap; the sheet states the fourfold effect, not these numbers.)

Refer the operating current to the 33 kV side. The turns ratio is $132/33 = 4$, so the 33 kV current is four times the 132 kV current:

$$I_{\text{op,min}} = 50 \times 4 = 200\ \text{A on the 33 kV side}$$

$$\text{Stability ratio} = \frac{12\,000}{200} = \mathbf{60}$$

Dividing the raw numbers, 12 000 A (33 kV) by 50 A (132 kV), gives 240.

The scheme withstands a through fault 60 times its minimum operating current. The raw division overstates this by a factor of 4, the voltage ratio, and the error looks plausible (240 is still "much greater than 1"). That is why the sheet calls it out: refer first, then divide.

9.9 The transformer differential in full: four corrections, two harmonics and REF revision sheet pp. 4–5

§9.7 introduced three problems. The sheet lists four corrections a transformer differential needs. They are not alternatives; a working scheme applies all of them.

Key result — the four corrections revision sheet pp. 4–5
CorrectionWhat goes wrong without itThe fix
1. Magnitude The two sides carry different currents (12 times different in the 11/132 kV case), so the secondaries do not match. Choose CT ratios so both secondaries are equal at rated load. The residual mismatch is absorbed by the bias slope. (§9.7)
2. Phase A Dy11 transformer shifts the windings 30°, so equal-magnitude currents do not cancel (52% spurious spill, §9.7). Star-side CTs connected in delta, delta-side CTs in star. A numerical relay uses a software vector-group setting.
3. Zero sequence An external earth fault drives zero-sequence current on the star side, with no counterpart on the delta side. It would appear as spill. The delta CT connection (or a numerical zero-sequence filter) traps it.
4. Tap changer An on-load tap changer alters the ratio, creating a real mismatch (§9.3). The slope must accommodate the full tap range.
Why it happens — where the zero-sequence current goes

Zero-sequence current is the part of the line currents that is identical in all three phases. It can only flow if it has a return path through the neutral to earth. On the earthed-star side of the transformer it has one. On the delta side it does not: a delta winding has no neutral, so the in-phase current can only circulate round inside the delta, and never reaches the delta's line terminals.

So for an external earth fault on the star side, the star-side CTs measure zero-sequence current and the delta-side CTs measure none. The relay sees one side carrying current that the other does not, and calls it an internal fault.

Now connect the star-side CTs in delta. The relay is fed with the difference of two phase currents (for instance $i_a - i_b$). Zero-sequence current is identical in both, so it cancels in the difference. It circulates inside the CT delta and never reaches the relay. That is what "trapped" means, and the same arrangement is what produces the 30° phase correction: one trick does two jobs.

Which harmonic blocks what

Key result — second harmonic means inrush, fifth means over-fluxing revision sheet p. 5
ConditionHarmonic that marks itTypical levelWhy
Magnetising inrush on energisationSecond 15–20% of fundamental Asymmetric, one-sided saturation (§8.3)
Over-excitation (high V/f)Fifth blocked separately Steady, symmetric saturation (§8.6)

Sympathetic inrush is the case where an already-energised parallel transformer is pushed partly into saturation when its neighbour is switched on, so a healthy unit also shows the second-harmonic signature (§8.5). Inrush is restrained or blocked; it is never a reason to trip. Over-fluxing is blocked in the differential element and handled by the separate V/f relay (§8.6).

Restricted earth fault (REF)

An earth fault near the neutral of a star winding is the transformer's version of the generator problem in §8.9. Only a fraction $x$ of the phase voltage drives the fault, so the fault current is small, and the current the line terminals see is smaller still (roughly $x^2$ of the full value, a standard result, not on the sheet). The ordinary differential relay, set to ignore CT mismatch and tap changer error, cannot see it.

Restricted earth fault protection fixes this by shrinking the zone to one winding and comparing only the earth-fault currents. It is a high-impedance differential around that winding and its neutral CT: the three line CTs of the winding are paralleled (their sum is the residual $3I_0$), and compared against the CT in the neutral earth connection. For a fault outside the winding the two currents are equal and opposite and circulate. For a fault inside the winding they are not, and the relay operates. It is "restricted" because it responds only to earth faults inside that one winding. Because it compares the earth-fault current itself against a very small setting, it can see the low-current faults that the phase differential cannot. The stabilisation against CT saturation is the same high-impedance idea as for the busbar (§9.10).

The other transformer protections

The sheet lists, besides differential and REF: the Buchholz relay (alarm on slow gas, trip on a sudden surge; the only detector of faults that are not yet electrical, §8.4), pressure relief, winding and oil temperature, V/f over-fluxing (§8.6), and back-up overcurrent and earth fault (Chapters 6 and 7).

Watch out — "Remember" list for transformer differential revision sheet p. 5
  • CT secondaries, not primary currents, are compared. Convert first, then subtract.
  • Second harmonic means inrush. Fifth harmonic means over-fluxing. Do not interchange them.
  • Buchholz has no electrical measurement at all, and is the only device that reacts to a fault that has not yet become an electrical fault.
  • Stability ratio: refer both currents across the turns ratio first. Dividing raw numbers from two voltage levels overstates the scheme fourfold in a typical 132/33 kV case (Worked example 4).

9.10 Busbar protection revision sheet p. 5

The busbar is the hardest zone in a substation to protect, for two reasons that pull in the same direction. A busbar fault is the most severe fault there is, because every source feeds it. And tripping the bus disconnects every circuit connected to it, so a wrong trip is almost as bad as a missed one. The scheme must therefore be very fast and extremely secure.

Key result — the design constraint is the external fault

A busbar scheme is designed from stability on an external fault, not from sensitivity to an internal one. The internal fault is easy: all the current has nowhere to go but the relay. The danger is a heavy through fault on a feeder: one CT (the faulted feeder's) carries the whole fault current, saturates while the others do not, and produces a large spill that looks like an internal fault. Everything in the scheme below is arranged to survive exactly that.

The high-impedance scheme

The idea: parallel all the CT secondaries and give the relay a high impedance. The spill from a saturated CT then prefers to circulate through the low-resistance path (the saturated CT's own winding and leads) rather than force current through the relay.

To size it, assume the worst case: one CT completely saturated. Its magnetising branch has collapsed to a short circuit, so the other CTs' secondary current, $I_{f,\text{sec}}$, drives straight through that CT's winding resistance $R_{CT}$ and the two leads. The voltage this develops across the parallel connection, which is the voltage the relay branch sees, is

Key result — high-impedance busbar scheme revision sheet p. 5

$$V_s = I_{f,\text{sec}}\bigl(R_{CT} + 2R_L\bigr), \qquad V_k \ge 2V_s, \qquad R_{\text{stab}} = \frac{V_s}{I_{\text{relay}}}$$

$$I_{\text{pri,pickup}} = \text{CTR}\Bigl(I_{\text{relay}} + \sum I_{\text{mag}}\Bigr)$$

$V_s$ is the stability voltage: the largest voltage a healthy external fault can put across the relay branch. $V_k$ is the CT knee-point voltage. $R_{\text{stab}}$ is the total resistance of the relay branch (the relay plus its series stabilising resistor), chosen so that the relay current is $I_{\text{relay}}$, its setting, at exactly $V_s$. $\sum I_{\text{mag}}$ is the sum of every paralleled CT's magnetising current at that voltage.

Why it happens — the lead resistance counts twice, and what the Metrosil is for

The current from the other CTs flows out along one lead to the saturated CT, through its winding, and back along the other lead. It crosses the lead resistance twice, once each way. So the loop contains $R_{CT} + 2R_L$, not $R_{CT} + R_L$. If you count one lead you halve the lead term and compute a stability voltage that is too low, and the real voltage on an external fault would then exceed the relay setting and trip it.

Why $V_k \ge 2V_s$? On an internal fault the relay branch is the only path, so the CTs have to develop enough voltage to push the relay's operating current round it, quickly. A knee-point of at least twice the setting keeps the CTs well inside their linear region while they do so, and leaves margin for the pessimism of the "completely saturated" assumption. (This rationale is standard practice; the sheet gives the rule.)
