And the Metrosil (a non-linear resistor: its resistance falls sharply as voltage rises) is connected across the relay for the opposite case. On an internal fault the CTs try to push their full secondary current through a 3000 Ω branch. By $V = IR$ that would be kilovolts, enough to break down insulation. The Metrosil, high resistance at low voltage and low at high, clips the peak.

Worked example 5 — sizing a high-impedance busbar scheme revision sheet p. 5

A 20 kA through fault; CTs are 2000/1 with $R_{CT} = 3.0\ \Omega$; each lead is 1.5 Ω one way; the relay setting is 20 mA; there are six CTs, each drawing 15 mA of magnetising current at the setting voltage. Find $V_s$, the knee-point requirement, $R_{\text{stab}}$ and the primary pickup.

1 — secondary fault current.

$$I_{f,\text{sec}} = \frac{20\,000}{2000} = 10\ \text{A}$$

2 — stability voltage, with the lead counted twice:

$$V_s = 10\,(3.0 + 2\times 1.5) = 10 \times 6.0 = \mathbf{60\ V}$$

3 — knee-point and stabilising resistance.

$$V_k \ge 2V_s = \mathbf{120\ V}, \qquad R_{\text{stab}} = \frac{60}{0.020} = \mathbf{3000\ \Omega}$$

4 — primary pickup. Six CTs each take 15 mA, so $\sum I_{\text{mag}} = 6 \times 0.015 = 0.090\ \text{A}$:

$$I_{\text{pri,pickup}} = 2000\,(0.020 + 0.090) = 2000 \times 0.110 = \mathbf{220\ A}$$

The scheme stays quiet for the whole 20 kA external fault and operates for an internal fault above 220 A of primary current. Notice where the 220 comes from: 40 A for the relay itself and 180 A for the six CTs' magnetising currents. The magnetising currents dominate, which is why the sheet warns that in the primary pickup they all add. Counting only one lead in step 2 would have given $V_s = 10(3.0 + 1.5) = 45\ \text{V}$, a stability voltage 25% too low, and a trip on the external fault.

The other busbar schemes

  • Low-impedance biased differential. The percentage-bias scheme of §9.4, extended to many feeders (restraint is half the sum of all the currents, as in Worked example 1). It tolerates CT mismatch by its slope, so it needs no matched CTs, and it is used with a check zone: a second differential measurement over the whole switchyard, which must also pick up before tripping is allowed. A failed CT or a wiring error in one bus zone creates a spill that looks like an internal fault, but the check zone, measuring independently, sees no fault. This is dependability traded for security, on purpose.
  • Frame leakage. The whole switchgear frame is insulated from earth and given a single earth connection, monitored by a CT. Any insulation fault to the frame sends current through that connection. Simple, suited to metal-clad indoor gear.
  • Directional comparison. Compare the direction of the current in each feeder. For an external fault, current leaves the bus in the faulted feeder while it enters in the others. For an internal fault every feeder's current points into the bus. (The sheet names this scheme; the description is the standard one.)
Watch out — "Remember" list for busbar protection revision sheet p. 6
  • $R_{CT} + 2R_L$. Counting one lead halves the lead term, and the scheme then maloperates on an external fault.
  • In the primary pickup, the magnetising currents of all the paralleled CTs are added to the relay current. With six CTs they usually dominate.
  • Busbar schemes are designed from the external fault, not the internal one.
Exam note — how this topic has been asked

Twice across the two post-midterm quizzes, and in two different directions:

  • Major Quiz Q1 — forwards. Given the bias and pickup, compute the restraint and the threshold. Two blanks.
  • Lab Quiz Q6 — backwards. Given a spill and a restraint, compute the percentage bias. One blank, with the CT ratios as a distractor.

So be ready to run the characteristic in either direction, and do not assume every number in the question is needed. The likely third variant, given this examiner's habit of re-asking the same shape with different numbers, is to give you the spill, the restraint and the bias and ask for the action — which is step 5 of the procedure and one line.

What the revision sheet emphasises here revision sheet pp. 4–6: the dual slope with its breakpoint (0.60 pu, not 1.10 pu, in §9.8), the four transformer corrections and the second-versus-fifth harmonic rule (§9.9), and the whole of busbar protection (§9.10): $V_s = I_{f,\text{sec}}(R_{CT} + 2R_L)$, the 60 V, 120 V, 3000 Ω and 220 A check, and the reason the design starts from the external fault.

Checkpoint

A two-winding transformer differential relay has 30% bias and $k_0 = 0.2\ \text{A}$. The CT secondaries read 8 A into the zone and 7 A out. Does it trip? And what would you check before believing the answer?

Answer. Spill $= |8 - 7| = 1\ \text{A}$. Restraint $= (8+7)/2 = 7.5\ \text{A}$. Threshold $= 0.2 + 0.30 \times 7.5 = 0.2 + 2.25 = 2.45\ \text{A}$. Since $1 < 2.45$, it does not trip.

What to check first: whether the transformer has just been energised. A 1 A spill on a 7.5 A restraint is a 13% mismatch, which is well within what a tap changer plus ordinary CT error can produce — so the no-trip verdict is sound for a steady-state condition. But if this is an energisation transient, the numbers would be far larger and the bias characteristic would not save you; the second-harmonic restraint would have to. Saying so is the kind of qualification this examiner's "justify your decision fully" asks for.

Chapter 10 post-midterm · highest yield

Distance Protection and the MHO Relay

Overcurrent protection has one incurable weakness: it measures the symptom, and the symptom depends on the whole network rather than on the line you are protecting. Change the generation pattern and every setting in Chapter 7 is wrong. Distance protection measures something that belongs to the line itself and cannot be changed by anybody else — and the price is that it needs geometry, which is why §5.8 set up the R–X plane in advance.

10.1 The problem that current cannot solve

Chapter 7 set a relay's pickup between the maximum load current and the minimum fault current, and graded relays by time. Both steps assume you know the fault current. You do not, reliably:

  • Fault current depends on the source. Two generators running gives roughly twice the fault current of one. So the same fault produces different currents at different times of day, and a setting that discriminates at peak generation may not discriminate at minimum.
  • On a long line it barely changes with position. If the source impedance is large compared with the line impedance, a fault at 50% along the line and one at 100% along it draw almost the same current — so no current setting can tell them apart.
  • Grading accumulates. Each relay upstream waits for the one below, so on a long radial feeder the relay nearest the source — protecting the most valuable equipment, seeing the largest fault — is the slowest of all.
Key result — the quantity to measure instead

The relay has a CT and a PT, so it can compute

$$Z_{\text{app}} \;=\; \frac{V_{\text{relay}}}{I_{\text{relay}}}$$

For a fault at a fraction $p$ along a line of impedance $Z_L$, the voltage at the relay is the drop across the faulted section, $V = I \cdot p Z_L$, so

$$Z_{\text{app}} = \frac{I \cdot p Z_L}{I} = p\,Z_L$$

The fault current cancels. The apparent impedance is proportional to the distance to the fault and is completely independent of how much current the source happens to supply. Hence the name: measuring impedance measures distance.

Why it happens — and what this buys that nothing else can

$Z_L$ is a property of the conductor: its length, its spacing, its material. It does not change when a generator is switched in, when the load pattern shifts, or when the network is reconfigured. So a setting expressed in ohms stays correct.

And because the measurement is of position, the relay can be given a reach: operate for anything closer than 80% of the line, do nothing for anything beyond. That is instantaneous, correct protection for most of the line with no grading delay at all — which is what Chapter 7's instantaneous unit was reaching for in §6.7 and could not achieve reliably, because its threshold was in amperes.

Where it breaks: $Z_{\text{app}} = pZ_L$ assumed the only impedance between relay and fault is the line. Any extra impedance — an arc, a tower footing, a current infeed from a parallel path — breaks it, and §10.4 and §10.8 are about exactly that.

10.2 One torque equation, three relays

Your lecturer derived all three distance-relay types from a single equation, and this is worth following because it makes three apparently unrelated characteristics into one family with three settings.

An electromechanical relay's moving element experiences a torque with contributions from current, from voltage, and from their interaction:

Key result — the universal relay torque equation

$$T \;=\; K_1 I^2 \;+\; K_2 V^2 \;+\; K_3\,V I \cos(\theta - \tau) \;+\; K_4$$

where $\theta$ is the angle by which the current lags the voltage, $\tau$ is the relay's design angle (the maximum-torque angle of §7.2), and $K_4$ is the restraining torque of the control spring. The relay operates when $T > 0$.

Each term is produced by a different winding, so a designer chooses the $K$s by choosing which windings to fit. Set all but one or two to zero and a named relay falls out.

KeepOperating conditionRelayShape on the R–X plane
$K_1$ only$I^2 > \text{const}$Overcurrent Not a region on this plane at all — it ignores voltage (Chapter 6)
$K_3$ only$\cos(\theta-\tau) > 0$Directional A half-plane through the origin, perpendicular to $\tau$ (Chapter 7)
$K_1$ operating,
$K_2$ restraining		$K_1 I^2 > |K_2| V^2$ Impedance A circle centred on the origin
$K_3$ operating,
$K_2$ restraining		$K_3 VI\cos(\theta-\tau) > |K_2|V^2$ MHO (admittance) A circle passing through the origin
$K_1$ operating,
$K_3$ restraining		$K_1 I^2 > |K_3|VI\sin(\theta-\tau)$ Reactance A straight line parallel to the $R$ axis
Proof — where the impedance circle comes from

Operate when $K_1 I^2 > |K_2| V^2$. Divide both sides by $I^2$, which is positive:

$$K_1 > |K_2|\frac{V^2}{I^2} = |K_2|\,|Z|^2 \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad |Z| < \sqrt{\frac{K_1}{|K_2|}}$$

A fixed bound on $|Z|$ and nothing else. On the R–X plane that is a disc centred on the origin — and critically, it contains points in every direction, so the relay operates for a fault behind it just as readily as in front. An impedance relay is not directional and needs a separate directional unit. That is its main drawback and the reason the MHO replaced it. HP.

Proof — where the MHO circle comes from, and why it is directional for free

Operate when $K_3 V I \cos(\theta - \tau) > |K_2| V^2$. Divide by $V^2$:

$$K_3 \frac{I}{V}\cos(\theta-\tau) > |K_2| \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \frac{\cos(\theta-\tau)}{|Z|} > \frac{|K_2|}{K_3}$$

and rearranging, with $D = K_3/|K_2|$:

$$\boxed{\;|Z| \;<\; D\,\cos(\theta - \tau)\;}$$

In polar coordinates $r = D\cos(\phi - \tau)$ is the equation of a circle of diameter $D$ whose diameter lies along the direction $\tau$ and which passes through the origin. Two consequences, both important:

It passes through the origin because $r = 0$ when $\phi - \tau = \pm 90°$. So a zero-impedance fault right at the relay is on the boundary — which is also the MHO's one weakness, since a close-up fault with any arc resistance at all can fall outside.

It is inherently directional. $\cos(\phi-\tau)$ is negative whenever $|\phi - \tau| > 90°$, and $|Z|$ cannot be negative, so the condition is unsatisfiable for any impedance more than 90° away from $\tau$. The whole circle lies on one side of the origin. A fault behind the relay appears in the opposite quadrant (§5.8) and therefore cannot be inside the circle. No separate directional element is needed, which is the MHO's decisive advantage. HP.

Key result — the three settings, compared on the four parameters your notes list
ImpedanceMHOReactance
Directional on its own?NoYesNo
Tolerance of arc resistancemoderatepoor — the circle is narrow in the $R$ directionexcellent — it ignores $R$ entirely
Immunity to power swings
and load encroachment		poor — the disc is wide in every directiongood — the circle is narrow and tilted along the line anglevery poor — the region is unbounded in $R$
Typical useZone 2 and 3 backup on older schemes the standard choice for transmission lines Short lines and arcing earth faults, always with an MHO starter to make it directional

The pattern: arc tolerance and swing immunity pull in opposite directions. Tolerating arc resistance means extending the region in $R$, which is exactly the direction normal load lies in. The MHO is the compromise everybody settled on.

The revision sheet revision sheet p. 3 lists a fourth characteristic, the quadrilateral, which numerical relays use to escape that trade-off by setting the $R$ reach and the $X$ reach separately. It does not come out of the torque equation (it needs a computer, not a coil), so it has its own section, §10.7. The sheet's one-line verdict on the three above: the impedance relay has the largest arc coverage of the circles but the worst load encroachment; the mho relay is the workhorse; the reactance relay is immune to arc resistance but "will operate on load, so it must be supervised by a starter (mho or offset)".

10.3 Three zones, and what each is for

One reach cannot do everything. A reach set to exactly 100% of the line would, given measurement error and CT/PT error, sometimes reach into the next line and trip for somebody else's fault. So the reach is deliberately set short, and two further zones are added behind it.

ZoneReachTimeJob
Zone 180% of the protected line instantaneous — no intentional delay Primary protection. Set short so it can never over-reach into the next line, which is what lets it be instantaneous.
Zone 2120% — the whole line plus 50% of the shortest adjacent line0.3–0.4 s Covers the last 20% that Zone 1 deliberately missed, and backs up the adjacent line's Zone 1. The delay is the grading margin of §5.5.
Zone 3own line + 100–120% of the longest adjacent line 0.8–1.0 s Remote backup: it acts if the adjacent line's own protection has failed completely.
Watch out — the zone table changed revision sheet p. 3

The earlier version of this chapter, built from the notebook and tablet notes, taught Zone 2 at 0.3–0.5 s and Zone 3 at "~180%" of the line with a delay of 1–2 s. The professor's revision sheet (p. 3) gives Zone 2 at 0.3–0.4 s and Zone 3 as own line + 100–120% of the longest adjacent line, at 0.8–1.0 s. The table above now carries the sheet's values, because the sheet is the professor's own statement of what the end-term wants. If an older note of yours says 0.5 s or 1–2 s, that is the superseded figure.

Two further cautions. The Zone 2 reach is written two ways on the sheet, "120%" and "line + 50% of the shortest next line". They are the same number only when the shortest adjacent line happens to be 40% of the protected one. If a question gives you the adjacent line, build the reach from "line + 50%"; if it gives nothing, use 120%. That is my reading of the two phrasings, not something the sheet states. And Zone 3 is no longer a fixed percentage at all: it depends on the longest neighbour, so you need that length before you can write a reach.

Key result — Zone 1 under-reaches and Zone 2 over-reaches, both on purpose revision sheet p. 4

Zone 1 stops at 80% so that errors can never push it into the next line: it under-reaches by design. Zone 2 goes to 120% so that errors can never leave a gap in the protected line: it over-reaches by design, and the 0.3–0.4 s delay is what makes that harmless. Neither is a mistake in the setting. If an exam question asks why Zone 1 misses a fault at 90% of the line, "because it is supposed to" is the correct first sentence.

Why it happens — why 80% and not 100%

The 20% shortfall is not caution for its own sake. It is the sum of the things that could make the relay over-reach: CT and PT ratio errors, the accuracy of the relay's own comparator, uncertainty in the published line impedance, and the effect of any intermediate infeed. Add those and 15–20% is a realistic worst case.

If Zone 1 over-reached even slightly it would trip instantaneously for a fault on the next line — a loss of selectivity, and the one failure mode that cannot be corrected by a delay, because there is no delay. So Zone 1 is set short and the shortfall is covered by a timed zone, where over-reach is harmless because the adjacent relay has already been given its chance. The 80/120 split is the trade between speed and selectivity, resolved by doing both in turn.

The sheet puts the error budget at "roughly 10–20%", which matches the 15–20% above. It adds one more route to the last 20%: with a communication channel, a carrier-aided scheme covers it instantaneously instead of waiting for Zone 2. In outline (standard practice, not developed in the course material), each end tells the other what it sees; a fault that one end sees in Zone 1 and the other end sees in forward Zone 2 must be inside the line, so both ends may trip at once.

10.4 Arc resistance, under-reach, and Major Quiz Q3

A real fault is rarely a bolt. It is an arc, with resistance. From §10.1, $Z_{\text{app}} = pZ_L$ assumed nothing but line between relay and fault; an arc adds a purely resistive term:

$$Z_{\text{app}} \;=\; p\,Z_L \;+\; R_{\text{arc}}$$

Arc resistance adds no reactance, so on the R–X plane it pushes the measured point horizontally to the right — away from the line impedance vector and towards the edge of the MHO circle. If it pushes far enough, the relay sees a fault outside its zone and fails to operate. That is under-reach, and it is the MHO's characteristic weakness.

Your notes give Warrington's empirical formula for how large the arc resistance is:

Key result — Warrington's arc resistance formula

$$R_{\text{arc}} \;=\; \frac{28{,}710\;L}{I^{1.4}}\ \ \Omega \qquad L \text{ in metres}, \quad I \text{ in amperes}$$

Read the exponent. $R_{\text{arc}}$ falls steeply with current, so arc resistance matters most for low-current faults — which are the distant ones, where the relay has least margin anyway. And it grows with arc length $L$, which grows as the arc is blown along by wind and by its own magnetic field, so the resistance increases while the fault persists.

Sample values, worth a sanity check: a 2 m arc at 2 kA gives $1.37\ \Omega$; the same arc at 500 A gives $9.56\ \Omega$; a 5 m arc at 2 kA gives $3.43\ \Omega$.

Key result — how much arc the zone can actually tolerate

Sweeping the figure's two sliders gives the largest arc resistance Zone 1 can absorb at each fault position, for this line and a 70% reach:

Fault at10%20%30%40%50%60%70%+
tolerates up to12.5 Ω14.5 Ω14.5 Ω 13.5 Ω11.5 Ω7.5 Ω0 Ω

Two things to take from that row, and the second is not obvious.

Tolerance collapses near the reach boundary. A fault at 70% of the line is at the 70% reach, so there is no margin left for anything — any arc at all puts it outside. This is why Zone 1 is normally set to 80% and not to 100% (§10.3): the shortfall is margin for exactly this.

But tolerance also falls for a very close fault. It peaks around 20–30% of the line, not at 0%. The reason is the circle's geometry: it passes through the origin, so it is pinched to nothing there and widens as you move out along the diameter. A fault close to the relay therefore has less of the circle to its right than a mid-line fault does. That is the concrete cost of the MHO characteristic passing through the origin, which §10.2 flagged as its one structural weakness.

Procedure — MHO relay, trip or no trip
  1. Write the line impedance in rectangular form, $Z_L = R_L + jX_L$, and compute $|Z_L| = \sqrt{R_L^2 + X_L^2}$.
  2. Compute the reach setting: $\text{reach} = (\text{percentage reach}) \times |Z_L|$. This is a magnitude, in ohms.
  3. Compute the apparent impedance, in rectangular form: $Z_{\text{app}} = p\,Z_L + R_{\text{arc}}$ — multiply the complex $Z_L$ by the fault position, then add the arc resistance to the real part only.
  4. Take its magnitude: $|Z_{\text{app}}|$.
  5. Compare and state the action. $|Z_{\text{app}}|$ less than the reach $\Rightarrow$ inside the zone $\Rightarrow$ trip.

Keep the three numbers separate. Step 3 gives a complex number, step 2 and step 4 give magnitudes. Writing any of them into the wrong blank is the single most common error here — and the examiner made it himself, see below.

Worked example 1 — the full MHO decision Major Quiz Q3

A transmission line has $Z_L = 10 + j40\ \Omega$. An MHO relay is set to reach 70% of the line impedance. A fault occurs at 60% of the line length with an arc resistance of 20 $\Omega$ in series. Find the apparent impedance, its magnitude and the reach setting, and say what the relay does.

1. $|Z_L| = \sqrt{10^2 + 40^2} = \sqrt{1700} = 41.231\ \Omega$, at an angle $\theta_L = \arctan(40/10) = 75.96°$.

2. Reach setting:

$$\text{reach} = 0.70 \times 41.231 = \mathbf{28.86\ \Omega}$$

3. Apparent impedance — multiply the complex $Z_L$ by 0.6, then add the arc resistance to the real part:

$$Z_{\text{app}} = 0.6(10 + j40) + 20 = (6 + j24) + 20 = \mathbf{26 + j24\ \Omega}$$

4. Magnitude:

$$|Z_{\text{app}}| = \sqrt{26^2 + 24^2} = \sqrt{676 + 576} = \sqrt{1252} = \mathbf{35.39\ \Omega}$$

5. $35.39\ \Omega > 28.86\ \Omega$, so the fault lies outside the Zone 1 reach: the relay does not trip.

The fault is at 60% of a line whose Zone 1 covers 70%, so it is inside the protected section and the relay should operate. It does not, and the arc resistance is the entire reason: without it the apparent impedance would be $6 + j24$ with magnitude $24.74\ \Omega$, comfortably inside 28.86. Twenty ohms of arc has turned a correct instantaneous trip into a failure to operate — textbook under-reach.

What actually happens on a real system is that Zone 2 picks it up after its 0.3–0.4 s delay, so the fault is cleared, just slowly. Check that here: Zone 2 at 120% reaches $1.2 \times 41.231 = 49.48\ \Omega$, and $35.39 < 49.48$, so Zone 2 does see it. That is precisely what the timed backup zone is for, and saying so is the complete answer to "comment on the result".

Watch out — the examiner made an error here, and it tells you what the trap is

On the master copy of this question, the answers were filled in, struck through and corrected. He first wrote the magnitude (35.39) into the blank asking for the complex apparent impedance, then wrote the reach (28.86) into the blank asking for the magnitude, then corrected both. The final answer "$26 + j24$" is written in the left margin rather than in the blank itself.

So the difficulty in this question is not the physics — it is keeping three numbers in their right places: a rectangular impedance, its modulus, and a setting. All three are in ohms, all three are plausible-looking answers, and there is no step marking. Label them explicitly as you work:

$Z_{\text{app}} = 26 + j24$  ·  $|Z_{\text{app}}| = 35.39$  ·  $\text{reach} = 28.86$  ·  compare the second with the third.

Worked example 2 — reach from a line's per-kilometre impedance Lab Quiz Q4

An MHO relay is set to cover 80% of a 200 km line of impedance $0.2 + j0.8\ \Omega/\text{km}$. Find the magnitude of the reach setting.

1. Total line impedance — multiply the per-kilometre figure by the length, keeping it complex:

$$Z_L = 200 \times (0.2 + j0.8) = 40 + j160\ \Omega$$

2. Take 80% of it, still complex:

$$0.8 \times (40 + j160) = 32 + j128\ \Omega$$

3. Magnitude:

$$|32 + j128| = \sqrt{1024 + 16384} = \sqrt{17408} = 131.94 \approx \mathbf{132\ \Omega}$$

Note the order of operations: scale the complex impedance and then take the magnitude. Doing it the other way round — taking $|Z_L| = 164.9$ and then 80% of that — gives 131.94 as well, because scaling by a positive real number commutes with taking the modulus. So here both routes agree, and that is worth knowing because it saves a step. But it does not work in Q3 above, where 20 Ω was added to the real part only: once you add anything, you must be in rectangular form first. The habit to build is stay rectangular until the last step.

The examiner's own working for this question is written in the bonus box at the bottom of the page — "$(40 + j160) \times 0.8 = 32 + j128$" — which is easy to misattribute to the bonus question.

10.5 Six measuring units, and residual compensation

Everything above assumed one voltage and one current. A three-phase line can fault in ten different ways, and a single relay element cannot measure the right impedance for all of them. Your notes build the full scheme from symmetrical components; here is what it amounts to.

Key result — the six elements

A full distance scheme per zone needs six measuring units, with their outputs combined through an OR gate so that any one of them can trip:

TypeElementsMeasures
Three phase-to-phasea–b, b–c, c–a $Z = \dfrac{V_a - V_b}{I_a - I_b}$ and its two rotations
Three phase-to-grounda–g, b–g, c–g $Z = \dfrac{V_a}{I_a + k\,I_{\text{res}}}$ and its two rotations

The phase-to-phase elements use a line voltage over a difference of currents, which is the correct ratio for a fault between two phases. The phase-to-ground elements need a correction, and that correction is the interesting part.

Why it happens — where the residual compensation factor $k$ comes from

For a phase-to-ground fault the current returns through the earth, not through another phase. The earth path has a different impedance from the phase conductor, so the naive ratio $V_a/I_a$ does not give $pZ_L$ — it gives something larger, and the relay under-reaches on every earth fault.

Working it through in symmetrical components, the voltage on the faulted phase is

$$V_a = p\bigl(Z_1 I_1 + Z_2 I_2 + Z_0 I_0\bigr)$$

and since for a line $Z_1 = Z_2$, and $I_a = I_1 + I_2 + I_0$, this rearranges to

$$V_a = p\,Z_1\Bigl(I_a + \frac{Z_0 - Z_1}{Z_1}\,I_0\Bigr)$$

With $I_{\text{res}} = I_a + I_b + I_c = 3I_0$ from §5.7, define

$$\boxed{\;k = \frac{Z_0 - Z_1}{3\,Z_1}\;} \qquad\text{so that}\qquad \frac{V_a}{I_a + k\,I_{\text{res}}} = p\,Z_1$$

and the relay now reads the positive-sequence line impedance times the fault position — exactly the same quantity the phase-to-phase elements read, so one reach setting serves both. That is the entire purpose of $k$: to make an earth-fault measurement comparable with a phase-fault measurement.

Typical values: an overhead line has $Z_0 \approx 3 Z_1$, giving $k \approx (3-1)/3 = 0.67$. A cable has a lower $Z_0/Z_1$ ratio and therefore a smaller $k$. If a question gives you $Z_0$ and $Z_1$ and asks for the residual compensation factor, this one line is the answer.

The revision sheet revision sheet p. 3 writes the same factor as $k_0$ and the compensated reading as $Z_{\text{relay}} = V_a / (I_a + k_0 I_{\text{res}})$. $k$ and $k_0$ are one quantity. In general it is a complex number, because $Z_0$ and $Z_1$ have different angles. It comes out real, as in the example below, only when $Z_0$ is a real multiple of $Z_1$.

Key result — in a single-line-to-ground fault, $I_a = 3I_0 = I_{\text{res}}$ revision sheet p. 3

For a solid single-line-to-ground (SLG) fault on phase $a$ of a radially fed line, phases $b$ and $c$ carry no fault current. Then the three sequence currents are forced to be equal:

$$I_1 = I_2 = I_0 \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad I_a = I_1 + I_2 + I_0 = 3I_0 = I_{\text{res}}$$

The faulted phase carries the entire residual current: $I_a$ and $I_{\text{res}}$ are the same number. So the compensated denominator collapses to $I_a + k_0 I_a = (1 + k_0)\,I_a$, and the relay reads

$$Z_{\text{relay}} = \frac{p\,Z_1\,(1 + k_0)\,I_a}{(1 + k_0)\,I_a} = p\,Z_1 \qquad\text{but with } k_0 = 0:\quad Z_{\text{relay}} = \frac{V_a}{I_a} = p\,Z_1\,(1 + k_0)$$

Setting $k_0$ to zero therefore leaves the factor $(1+k_0)$ uncancelled. The relay reads too much impedance, so it sees every earth fault as further away than it is: it under-reaches.

Worked example 3 — what a wrong $k_0$ does at the Zone-1 boundary revision sheet p. 3

A 100 km overhead line has $Z_1 = 0.10 + j0.50\ \Omega/\text{km}$ and $Z_0 = 0.30 + j1.50\ \Omega/\text{km}$. Zone 1 is set to 80%. Find $k_0$. Then, for a solid SLG fault at 80 km, find what the earth-fault element reads with the correct $k_0$ and with $k_0 = 0$, and say whether it trips.

1. Compare the two impedances. $Z_0 = 3Z_1$ exactly, because $3(0.10 + j0.50) = 0.30 + j1.50$. So the ratio $Z_0/Z_1 = 3$ and

$$k_0 = \frac{Z_0 - Z_1}{3Z_1} = \frac{3Z_1 - Z_1}{3Z_1} = \frac{2}{3} = 0.667$$

2. Line and setting. $Z_{1L} = 100(0.10 + j0.50) = 10 + j50\ \Omega$, so $|Z_{1L}| = \sqrt{100 + 2500} = 50.99\ \Omega$. Zone 1 is 80% of that:

$$Z_{\text{set}} = 0.80 \times 50.99 = \mathbf{40.79\ \Omega}$$

3. The fault at 80 km is at $p = 0.80$, so its true positive-sequence impedance is $0.80 \times 50.99 = 40.79\ \Omega$. It sits exactly on the Zone-1 boundary, which is the hardest place to be right.

4. With the correct $k_0 = 0.667$ the relay reads $p\,|Z_{1L}| = 40.79\ \Omega$. That equals the setting: the fault is at the boundary and the element operates.

5. With $k_0 = 0$ (the compensation left out) the relay reads

$$|Z_{\text{relay}}| = 40.79\,(1 + 0.667) = 40.79 \times 1.667 = \mathbf{68.0\ \Omega}$$

68.0 Ω is far outside a 40.79 Ω setting, so with $k_0 = 0$ the relay refuses to trip at its own Zone-1 boundary. The error is not small: a factor of $1 + k_0 = 1.667$, so the apparent reach on earth faults shrinks to $40.79/1.667 = 24.5\ \Omega$, only 48% of the line instead of 80%. A fault at 50 km (true impedance 25.5 Ω) would already read 42.5 Ω and fall outside Zone 1 as well. That is why $k_0$ exists: the earth loop contains $Z_0$, and without $k_0$ the relay prices every kilometre of an earth fault at $1.667$ times the true rate.

10.6 The two things that make a healthy line look faulty

Load encroachment

From §5.8, normal load appears on the R–X plane far from the origin at a small angle. "Far" is relative to the reach, though. A heavily loaded long line can present an apparent impedance of a few hundred ohms at perhaps 25°, and a Zone 3 element reaching 180% of a long line may have a characteristic large enough to contain it. The relay then trips a fully healthy, merely busy line.

Key result — why the MHO shape helps here

Normal load sits at a small impedance angle, typically 10°–30°, because load is near unity power factor. A fault sits at the line angle, typically 70°–85°, because a line is mostly reactance.

The MHO circle has its diameter along $\tau \approx \theta_L$, so it is long in the fault direction and narrow in the load direction. It therefore discriminates between them by angle as well as by magnitude — which an origin-centred impedance circle cannot do at all, since it is equally wide in every direction.

Where that is not enough, a load-encroachment blinder is added: a pair of straight lines that carve the low-angle region out of the characteristic, so the relay cannot operate there whatever the magnitude.

Watch out — one mark in your notes is ambiguous, and the physics settles it

Page 125 of the tablet notes shows a worked case annotated "Normal load → Trip (✓)". Taken at face value that says an MHO relay trips on normal load, which would make the relay useless.

Work it from the geometry instead. An MHO circle passes through the origin and has diameter equal to the reach — a few tens of ohms, tilted to ~76°. A normal-load point sits at a few hundred ohms at ~25°. It is nowhere near inside. So physically it does not trip, and the tick most likely marks "this case handled correctly" rather than "trips".

Trust the construction, not the annotation, and if a question asks, answer from the geometry: compute the load's apparent impedance, compare its magnitude and angle with the circle, and state that it lies outside.

Power swings

After a disturbance the machines swing against each other — which is Chapter 2's subject seen from the protection side. During a swing the apparent impedance seen by a relay moves, tracing a locus across the R–X plane, and that locus can pass straight through a distance characteristic. The relay then trips a healthy line in the middle of a stability event, which is the worst possible moment to remove a transmission path.

Key result — how a swing is told from a fault

By speed. A fault appears in under a cycle: the impedance jumps from the load point to the fault point essentially instantaneously. A power swing develops over hundreds of milliseconds to seconds, because it is governed by machine inertia.

So a power-swing blocking scheme places a second, larger characteristic outside the tripping zones and times how long the impedance takes to cross between them. Slow crossing $\Rightarrow$ swing $\Rightarrow$ block the trip. Fast crossing $\Rightarrow$ fault $\Rightarrow$ allow it.

That is also the cleanest possible illustration of the "three clocks" frame from Chapter 1: the only thing distinguishing these two conditions is which clock they happen on.

10.7 The fourth characteristic: the quadrilateral revision sheet p. 3

Look at what each of the three characteristics got wrong. The mho circle is narrow in the $R$ direction, which keeps load out and also keeps arcs out (§10.4). The reactance line ignores $R$ completely, which welcomes arcs in and welcomes load in too. Both problems come from one fact: a circle, or a line, has only one dial. You cannot widen the $R$ reach without also changing the $X$ reach, because the shape ties them together.

A quadrilateral characteristic has four straight edges and so four independent dials. It is the numerical-relay default, because a computer can test four inequalities as easily as one, whereas a coil and a spring could not.

Key result — what each edge does revision sheet p. 3

The relay operates when the measured $Z = R + jX$ satisfies all four conditions at once:

  • Top edge: $X < X_{\text{reach}}$. This is a reactance line. It sets how far along the line the zone reaches, and, because it ignores $R$, it is immune to arc resistance.
  • Right edge: $R < R_{\text{reach}}$. This sets how much resistance (arc, tower footing) the zone will tolerate, independently of the reactive reach. Setting it too large lets heavy load in, so it is chosen a little above the largest expected fault resistance.
  • Bottom and left edges. These stop the zone at, or slightly behind, the origin so that a fault in the reverse direction is excluded. That is how the quadrilateral becomes directional, as the sheet puts it: it combines "the arc coverage of reactance with the directionality of mho".

The sheet's phrase for the whole idea is independent $R$ and $X$ reach. (Real relays often tilt the top edge down by a few degrees so that load flow cannot make the zone over-reach. That is standard practice, not in the course material.)

ImpedanceMhoReactanceQuadrilateral
Shapecircle on origincircle through originline $X$ = const four straight edges
Directional?no, needs a separate elementyes, inherently no, needs a starteryes, by its left and bottom edges
Arc resistancelargest cover of the circlespoor immuneset by $R_{\text{reach}}$
Loadworst encroachmentleast affected operates on loadset by $R_{\text{reach}}$
Where usedolder Zone 2 and 3the workhorse short lines, with a starternumerical relays
Worked example 4 — the Q3 fault again, on a quadrilateral revision sheet p. 3

The line of Worked example 1 ($Z_L = 10 + j40\ \Omega$, fault at 60% with 20 Ω arc, so $Z_{\text{app}} = 26 + j24\ \Omega$) is now protected by a quadrilateral Zone 1 with $X_{\text{reach}} = 0.70 \times 40 = 28\ \Omega$ and $R_{\text{reach}} = 40\ \Omega$. Does it trip? What does it do with a heavy load of $60\ \Omega$ at $25°$?

1. Fault. $X = 24\ \Omega < 28\ \Omega$ and $R = 26\ \Omega < 40\ \Omega$: inside both edges, and in front of the relay. Trip.

2. Load. $R = 60\cos 25° = 54.4\ \Omega$ and $X = 60\sin 25° = 25.4\ \Omega$. The reactance test passes ($25.4 < 28$), but $R = 54.4\ \Omega > 40\ \Omega$ fails the right edge. No trip.

The mho relay of Worked example 1 failed to trip on this fault because its single circle has no spare width in $R$. The quadrilateral trips because the $R$ edge was set independently, to 40 Ω against a 20 Ω arc. A pure reactance relay with the same 28 Ω reach would also have tripped on the fault, and would also have tripped on the load, because $25.4 < 28$. The right edge is the only thing standing between the load and a false trip, which is why it is not made arbitrarily large.

10.8 What a distance relay gets wrong revision sheet p. 4

The idea of §10.1 is that $V/I$ measures distance. Every error in this section is a way in which $V/I$ at the relay stops being $p\,Z_L$. The sheet asks you to know each one, so here they are with their mechanisms. Arc resistance, load encroachment and power swings have been met already; the rest are new.

Arc resistance: worst on short lines

§10.4 showed that an arc adds to the real part only, so a mho relay under-reaches and a reactance relay is unaffected. The sheet's addition is where it hurts. What matters is the arc compared with the line it sits on. Take Warrington's 1.37 Ω (a 2 m arc at 2 kA) on the line of §10.5, with $|Z_L| = 50.99\ \Omega$ at 100 km: that is 2.7% of the line impedance. On a 10 km line of the same type, $|Z_L| = 5.10\ \Omega$, and the same arc is 27%. The mho circle for the short line is ten times smaller, while the arc has not shrunk at all. Hence reactance and quadrilateral characteristics on short lines.

Infeed and outfeed

Suppose relay A protects line AB, and a second line feeds a source into the bus B. A fault sits on the line beyond B, a distance $Z_{BF}$ from B. The current in line AB is $I_A$. The current in the faulted section beyond B is larger, $I_A + I_B$, because the second source adds $I_B$ there. Relay A sees only $I_A$, but the voltage at A includes the drop across both sections:

$$V_A = I_A Z_{AB} + (I_A + I_B)\,Z_{BF} \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad Z_{\text{app}} = \frac{V_A}{I_A} = Z_{AB} + \Bigl(1 + \frac{I_B}{I_A}\Bigr) Z_{BF}$$

The second section's impedance is multiplied by $1 + I_B/I_A$, which exceeds 1 when $I_B$ flows into the fault. The relay sees the fault further away than it is: infeed causes under-reach. If the intermediate current flows the other way (outfeed, a load or tap drawing current away from the faulted path, so that $I_B$ is negative) the factor is below 1 and the relay over-reaches.

Worked example 5 — infeed pushes a Zone-2 fault out of the zone revision sheet p. 4

Line AB has $Z_{AB} = 6 + j24\ \Omega$ and the next line BC has $Z_{BC} = 4 + j16\ \Omega$. Relay A's Zone 2 is set to the whole of AB plus 50% of BC. A fault occurs 40% of the way along BC, and a source at B contributes $I_B = 0.5\,I_A$. Does Zone 2 see it? Repeat with $I_B = -0.5\,I_A$ (outfeed).

1. Zone-2 reach. $Z_{AB} + 0.5 Z_{BC} = 6 + j24 + 2 + j8 = 8 + j32$, so the setting is $\sqrt{64 + 1024} = 32.99\ \Omega$.

2. Fault impedance beyond B: $Z_{BF} = 0.4(4 + j16) = 1.6 + j6.4\ \Omega$.

3. True impedance (no infeed): $7.6 + j30.4$, magnitude $\sqrt{57.76 + 924.16} = 31.34\ \Omega$. That is less than 32.99, so with no infeed Zone 2 would see the fault.

4. With infeed, $1 + I_B/I_A = 1.5$:

$$Z_{\text{app}} = 6 + j24 + 1.5(1.6 + j6.4) = 8.4 + j33.6\ \Omega, \qquad |Z_{\text{app}}| = \sqrt{70.56 + 1128.96} = 34.63\ \Omega$$

5. $34.63 > 32.99$, so the fault is outside Zone 2: under-reach.

6. With outfeed, $1 + I_B/I_A = 0.5$: $Z_{\text{app}} = 6.8 + j27.2$, magnitude $\sqrt{46.24 + 739.84} = 28.04\ \Omega$.

A real fault, 31.34 Ω from A, reads 34.63 Ω with infeed (11% too far) and 28.04 Ω with outfeed (11% too near). The infeed case is the dangerous one: the relay does not see a fault it is meant to back up. The remedy is to set the Zone-2 and Zone-3 reaches with the infeed in mind, which means using a larger $1 + I_B/I_A$ than the everyday value.

Source-to-line impedance ratio (SIR)

Define $\text{SIR} = Z_S / Z_L$, where $Z_S$ is the impedance of the source behind the relay. A weak source has a large $Z_S$ and so a high SIR. In a three-phase fault at a fraction $p$ of the line, the fault current is limited by $Z_S + pZ_L$, and the relay sees only the drop across the line part:

$$\frac{V_{\text{relay}}}{V_{\text{source}}} = \frac{pZ_L}{Z_S + pZ_L} = \frac{p}{\text{SIR} + p}$$

At the Zone-1 reach, $p = 0.8$. With a strong source, SIR = 0.5, the relay retains $0.8/1.3 = 61.5\%$ of the source voltage. With a weak source, SIR = 10, it retains $0.8/10.8 = 7.4\%$. A voltage error of 1% of nominal (noise, CT and VT inaccuracy) is then 1/7.4, about 13%, of what the relay is trying to measure, and 13% eats most of the 20% margin between Zone 1's 80% and the end of the line. The relay has not changed. Its signal has shrunk, and its errors have not.

The CVT transient

A capacitor voltage transformer (CVT) steps voltage down using a capacitive divider followed by a small tuned transformer, which is cheaper than an ordinary voltage transformer at transmission voltage. Both the capacitors and the tuning inductor store energy. When the line voltage collapses in a close-in fault, that energy does not vanish at once: the secondary voltage decays over a few cycles instead of dropping to zero. The relay is shown more voltage than the fault allows, reads $V/I$ too high, and momentarily under-reaches for close-in faults (the sheet's phrase on p. 1). Standard practice (not in the course material) is to filter this out or to add a short delay to Zone 1 for the highest-risk faults.

Power swing and load encroachment, with the sheet's framing

§10.6 timed the crossing between two characteristics. The sheet states the same test as a rate of change of $Z$: a fault is a step, a swing is a drift. As an illustration (the timer values are settings, not course constants), a fault takes the impedance point across the gap in under a cycle (20 ms at 50 Hz), while a swing needs 150 ms or more. Slow means swing: block the trip.

For load encroachment, the sheet's wording is "heavy load at low power factor". Both words matter. Heavy makes $|Z|$ small, bringing the load point towards the Zone 3 boundary; low power factor makes the load angle large, towards the line angle, which removes the angle discrimination §10.6 relied on. The cures are the load-encroachment blinder of §10.6 or a quadrilateral whose $R$ edge stops short of the load, as in Worked example 4.

10.9 Primary ohms to secondary ohms revision sheet p. 4

The relay does not sit on the line. It sits behind a CT and a VT, so it sees a small current and a small voltage, and the impedance it calculates is a different number from the line's. Settings in the relay are entered in secondary ohms. Settings calculated from line data are in primary ohms. The conversion is where many marks go.

Key result — the conversion revision sheet p. 4

Let the CT ratio be $N_{CT}$ and the VT ratio be $N_{VT}$ (each as primary divided by secondary, so a 1000/5 CT has $N_{CT} = 200$). The relay sees $V_{\text{sec}} = V_{\text{pri}}/N_{VT}$ and $I_{\text{sec}} = I_{\text{pri}}/N_{CT}$, so

$$Z_{\text{sec}} = \frac{V_{\text{sec}}}{I_{\text{sec}}} = \frac{V_{\text{pri}}/N_{VT}}{I_{\text{pri}}/N_{CT}} = Z_{\text{pri}}\;\frac{N_{CT}}{N_{VT}}$$

Multiply by the CT ratio, divide by the VT ratio. The memory aid: the VT shrinks the voltage, which would shrink the impedance, and the CT shrinks the current, which makes the impedance look larger. The CT term goes on top.

Worked example 6 — setting Zone 1 in secondary ohms, and the inversion error revision sheet p. 4

The Zone-1 setting of Worked example 3 is 40.79 Ω primary. The CT is 1000/5 and the VT is 220 kV/110 V. Find the secondary setting, and show what the inverted formula would give.

1. Ratios: $N_{CT} = 1000/5 = 200$ and $N_{VT} = 220\,000/110 = 2000$.

2. Correct conversion:

$$Z_{\text{sec}} = 40.79 \times \frac{200}{2000} = \mathbf{4.079\ \Omega}$$

3. Inverted (wrong): $40.79 \times 2000/200 = 407.9\ \Omega$.

The correct and the inverted answers differ by a factor $(N_{VT}/N_{CT})^2 = 100$. The wrong answer is a perfectly believable resistor-sized number, so nothing about it looks broken. A relay set to 407.9 Ω would never see a fault on the line, because the real fault looks like 4 Ω on the secondary side. Check the direction before you write the answer: secondary impedance values for a transmission line are typically a few ohms, not hundreds (a rule of thumb, not a course result).

Remember — the sheet's list for distance protection revision sheet p. 4
  • A reactance relay takes only the imaginary part. Write $X$ for it, never $|Z|$. Using $|Z|$ lets the arc resistance back into the answer and defeats the only reason to choose the characteristic. In Worked example 4 the reactance test used $X = 24\ \Omega$, not $35.39\ \Omega$.
  • Primary to secondary: multiply by the CT ratio, divide by the VT ratio. The inverted version looks plausible and is wrong by $(N_{VT}/N_{CT})^2$.
  • Infeed causes under-reach, never over-reach. (Outfeed is the reverse, so over-reach comes from outfeed.)
  • Z1 under-reaches by design and Z2 over-reaches by design. Neither is a fault in the setting (§10.3).
  • $k_0$ is not optional on earth faults. Without it the relay reads $1 + k_0$ times too much (§10.5).
Exam note — how this topic has been asked

Revision sheet, Module 4(c): the professor's end-term sheet revision sheet pp. 3–4 stresses the zone table (Z2 120% at 0.3–0.4 s, Z3 at 0.8–1.0 s), the four characteristics with the quadrilateral, the $k_0 = 0.667$ check (40.79 Ω reading as 68.0 Ω), the list of what a distance relay gets wrong, and the CT/VT conversion. Expect a "which direction does the error go" question: infeed under-reaches, arc under-reaches a mho, a missing $k_0$ under-reaches.

On the quizzes, twice, and both times the arithmetic was the same five steps:

  • Major Quiz Q3 — four blanks: apparent impedance, its magnitude, the reach setting, the action. With arc resistance, and with a deliberate trap in keeping the three numbers apart.
  • Lab Quiz Q4 — one blank: the reach setting magnitude from a per-kilometre impedance and a length.

Given the depth your notes go to on the derivations — one torque equation for all three relay types, the four performance parameters, the six-element scheme from symmetrical components — a derivation question is plausible on the end-term even though the quizzes only asked for numbers. The three most likely are: derive the MHO characteristic from the universal torque equation (§10.2), explain why the MHO is inherently directional and the impedance relay is not (§10.2), and derive the residual compensation factor (§10.5). All three are short, and all three are in this chapter.

Checkpoint

A 100 km line has $Z_L = 0.1 + j0.5\ \Omega/\text{km}$. Zone 1 is set to 80%. A phase-to-ground fault occurs at 70% of the line with a 15 Ω arc. Does Zone 1 operate? And would a reactance relay with the same reach have operated?

Answer. $Z_L = 100(0.1 + j0.5) = 10 + j50$, so $|Z_L| = \sqrt{100+2500} = 50.99\ \Omega$ and the reach is $0.8 \times 50.99 = 40.79\ \Omega$. The apparent impedance is $0.7(10+j50) + 15 = (7 + j35) + 15 = 22 + j35$, magnitude $\sqrt{484+1225} = \sqrt{1709} = 41.34\ \Omega$. Since $41.34 > 40.79$, Zone 1 just fails to operate — under-reach again, and by only half an ohm.

A reactance relay would have operated. It measures only the reactive part, and the fault's reactance is $35\ \Omega$ against a reach of $0.8 \times 50 = 40\ \Omega$ in reactance terms — comfortably inside, because arc resistance contributes nothing to $X$. That is exactly the "excellent arc tolerance" row of the §10.2 table, and it is why reactance relays are used on short lines where arc resistance is large compared with line impedance. The catch, from the same table, is that a reactance relay is not directional and has no load immunity at all, so it must be used with an MHO starter — which is the standard arrangement.

Chapter 11 post-midterm · highest yield

Interrupting the Fault: Circuit Breakers, RRRV and Fuses

Every relay in Chapters 6 to 10 ends by closing a contact and asking somebody else to do the actual work. This chapter is that somebody else. It carries the single highest-value blank on either quiz — three marks in one box — and it contains the one place in this course where your lecture notes and the examiner's answer key disagree. Read §11.4 before you revise anything else here.

Watch out — where this chapter's material comes from

Your notes now reach this chapter. Tablet-notes_rotor-fault-and-switchgear.pdf (pp. 2–8, added 27 Oct) covers the ideal breaker, restriking versus recovery voltage, the breakdown strengths of the interrupting media, current chopping, the full derivation of the restriking voltage and RRRV, and resistance switching. Those parts of this chapter are now taught from the lecture, page by page.

And the end-term revision sheet (Prof. Kumar, for the 14 November paper, pp. 6–7) covers this chapter as Module 4(h): arc interruption theories, the restriking-voltage model, resistance switching, chopping, ratings, breaker types and auto-reclosing. Its worked example is Worked example 3, and its ratings check is Worked example 6. It does not mention fuses.

Still unnotated: fuses (§11.8) and most of the breaker ratings table. Those are built backwards from the master-copy answers plus standard switchgear theory, as before. The notes also record "1 class missed" immediately before switchgear begins, so something was taught there that no source captures.

And one conflict. The lecture derives $\text{RRRV} = \omega_n V_m \sin\omega_n t$. The quiz master copy's numbers only come out of $\omega_n V_m \cos\omega_n t$. Both are shown in §11.4, with which to write where. Treat the physics as reliable and the conventions as calibrated to this examiner specifically.

11.1 Interrupting a current is not opening a switch

Pull a plug out of the wall and the current stops. That intuition is the problem, because it is wrong about anything carrying real power.

Start with what a perfect breaker would be, because the lecture does. A breaker is two contacts in a sealed chamber full of an insulating medium — air, oil, vacuum or a gas — one fixed and one that a mechanism pulls away. Its normal position is closed: it spends almost its entire life carrying load current and doing nothing at all.

Key result — the ideal breaker dissipates nothing in either state notes p. 2
StateCurrent through itVoltage across itPower lost in it
Closed (making, carrying)rated or load current $\approx 0$$\approx 0$
Open (broken)$0$ the full supply voltage$0$

Current with no voltage, or voltage with no current: either way $P = VI = 0$. The trouble is entirely in the transition, the few milliseconds when there is current and voltage at the same time. Everything in this chapter is about getting through that transition.

When a breaker's contacts begin to part, the current does not stop. The last point of contact carries the entire current through a vanishing area, so the metal there vaporises, and the vapour between the contacts is hot enough to be ionised — full of free electrons and ions. An ionised gas conducts. So the moment the contacts separate, a plasma arc bridges the gap and the current keeps flowing, now through an arc rather than through metal.

Pulling the contacts further apart does not help much on its own: the arc merely lengthens, and the energy it dissipates keeps the channel hot and conducting. A 40 kA arc across a 132 kV breaker dissipates energy in the megawatts, and left alone it destroys the breaker.

Key result — the only moment interruption is possible

In an AC system, the current passes through zero twice per cycle. At that instant there is no current to maintain the arc, the plasma stops being fed, and the gap has a chance to cool and de-ionise.

So an AC breaker does not stop the current. It waits for the natural current zero and then tries to prevent the current from restarting. Everything a breaker is designed to do — blast compressed air through the gap, flow $\mathrm{SF}_6$, draw the arc into a vacuum, split it across arcing contacts — is in service of removing heat and charge carriers from the gap in the few hundred microseconds around that zero.

Why it happens — the race, and both ways to lose it

At the current zero, two quantities start changing in opposition.

The gap's dielectric strength rises as the plasma cools and the charge carriers recombine. How fast depends entirely on the interrupting medium — this is what the breaker designer controls.

The voltage across the gap also rises, because the system is still energised and the gap is now the only thing standing between the source and the fault. This is the restriking voltage, and §11.3 is about its shape.

Interruption succeeds if the dielectric strength stays ahead of the voltage. It fails in two distinct ways, and the distinction is examinable:

  • Restrike — the voltage wins after a quarter-cycle or more, when the gap had already partly recovered. The arc re-establishes and the breaker must wait for the next current zero.
  • Re-ignition (thermal failure) — the voltage wins within microseconds of the zero, because the gap was still too hot to recover at all. The current never really stopped.

So the design question is not "how strong is the gap?" but "how fast does the gap recover compared with how fast the voltage rises?" — and that rate of rise is the quantity this chapter computes.

The two named theories (revision sheet p. 7) are two ways of keeping score in that race. The recovery-rate (dielectric) theory compares voltages: the gap's dielectric strength must out-run the restriking voltage. The energy-balance theory compares powers: the rate at which heat is removed from the residual arc column must exceed the rate at which the current still trickling through it generates heat. Lose the energy race and the column re-heats, which is re-ignition. Lose the voltage race and the cooled gap breaks down, which is a restrike.

Watch out — a "3-cycle breaker" still waits for a current zero revision sheet p. 7

A breaker's quoted cycles are a mechanism rating: how long the contacts take to part. The arc then burns until the next natural current zero, which comes every half cycle, so at worst 10 ms later at 50 Hz. Interruption always happens at a current zero; no rating changes that.

11.2 Where the oscillation comes from

Why does the voltage across the gap oscillate rather than just rise to the system voltage? Because of what is left in the circuit.

Looking back from the breaker into the system you see the inductance of the source and the lines, and the capacitance of the busbars, bushings, CTs and the lines themselves. That is a series L–C circuit. Before interruption, the inductance is carrying the full fault current and the capacitance is nearly discharged — the fault held the voltage near zero.

At the current zero the fault is disconnected, and the energy stored in $L$ has nowhere to go but into $C$. An L–C circuit handed energy does one thing: it rings.

Key result — the natural frequency of the restriking voltage

$$f_n = \frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{LC}} \qquad\text{and}\qquad \omega_n = 2\pi f_n = \frac{1}{\sqrt{LC}}$$

Note the scale. System frequency is 50 Hz; this natural frequency is typically a few hundred hertz to tens of kilohertz, because $L$ and $C$ are small. At $f_n = 1\ \text{kHz}$ the ringing period is 1 ms, so the voltage is rising fastest 250 µs after the current zero and peaks at 500 µs. That is why everything in this chapter is measured in microseconds while Chapter 7 was measured in seconds.

A larger $L$ or $C$ gives a lower natural frequency and therefore a gentler voltage rise, which is easier to interrupt. That is the physical basis for fitting a capacitor across a breaker's contacts, which the checkpoint at the end of this chapter returns to.

Deriving the restriking voltage notes pp. 4–6

The lecture derives the shape of that ringing exactly, and it is short enough to reproduce under exam conditions. Figure 11.1 is the circuit, taken from the notes.

Why the source can be frozen. The notes write "Approximation" over panel (b) and move on. Here is the reason. The fault current lags the voltage by nearly 90°, so the current zero arrives when the supply voltage is at its peak, $V_m$. With $f_n = 1$ kHz, the restriking voltage reaches its first peak 500 µs later. In 500 µs the 50 Hz supply turns through $360° \times 50 \times 500\times10^{-6} = 9°$, and $\cos 9° = 0.988$. So the source stays at $V_m$ to within about 1%. The approximation is good whenever $f_n \gg 50$ Hz. It degrades for a natural frequency of only a few hundred hertz.

The starting conditions are both zero, and both for physical reasons. $i(0) = 0$, because $t = 0$ is the current zero; that is the only instant the breaker can interrupt. $v_C(0) = 0$, because the fault was shorting the capacitor a moment ago.

Derivation — the restriking voltage notes pp. 5–6

1. Kirchhoff around the loop of panel (b). The source voltage is shared between the inductor and the capacitor:

$$V_m = L\frac{di}{dt} + v_C = L\frac{di}{dt} + \frac1C\int_0^t i\,dt$$

2. Laplace transform, with both initial conditions zero. A constant $V_m$ transforms to $V_m/s$:

$$\frac{V_m}{s} = Ls\,I(s) + \frac{1}{Cs}\,I(s) \quad\Longrightarrow\quad I(s) = \frac{V_m}{L\left(s^2 + \frac{1}{LC}\right)}$$

3. Recognise a sine. Write $\omega_n^2 = 1/LC$ and multiply top and bottom by $\omega_n$ to match the standard pair $\omega/(s^2+\omega^2) \leftrightarrow \sin\omega t$:

$$I(s) = \frac{V_m}{L\omega_n}\cdot\frac{\omega_n}{s^2+\omega_n^2} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad i(t) = \frac{V_m}{L\omega_n}\sin\omega_n t$$

4. Integrate for the capacitor voltage, which is the voltage across the open breaker:

$$v_C(t) = \frac1C\int_0^t i\,dt = \frac{V_m}{LC\,\omega_n^2}\Bigl[-\cos\omega_n t\Bigr]_0^t = \frac{V_m}{LC\,\omega_n^2}\bigl(1-\cos\omega_n t\bigr)$$

and since $LC\,\omega_n^2 = 1$ by definition,

$$\boxed{\;v_C(t) = V_m\bigl(1 - \cos\omega_n t\bigr)\;}$$

5. Differentiate for the rate of rise:

$$\boxed{\;\text{RRRV}(t) = \frac{dv_C}{dt} = V_m\,\omega_n \sin\omega_n t\;} \qquad \text{RRRV}_{\max} = \omega_n V_m \;\text{ at }\; \omega_n t = 90°$$

The voltage peaks at $\omega_n t = \pi$, half a natural period after the current zero, at $v_C = 2V_m$. That is the curve the lecture sketches on p. 6: it starts flat, rises, and tops out at $2V_m$.

Why it happens — reading the result without the algebra

Think of the capacitor as a bucket filled through a long, heavy hose (the inductor). The water in the hose starts at rest, so at first the level barely moves: that is the flat start, zero slope at $t = 0$. The water speeds up, and the level rises fastest a quarter-period in, when the hose is flowing hardest. By the time the level reaches the source's height $V_m$, the water in the hose is still moving at full speed and cannot stop at once, so it overfills the bucket, all the way to $2V_m$. Then it slows, stops, and drains back. With nothing to absorb energy, it would do that forever.

Where the picture breaks: a real hose has friction, and the real circuit has resistance, so the swing decays and $2V_m$ is an upper bound. §11.6 adds that resistance on purpose.

Watch out — two different omegas in your notes

The notes write the source as $V_m\sin\omega_n t$ on p. 4 and as $V_m\cos\omega_n t$ on p. 7, reusing the subscript $n$. Those are two different frequencies. $\omega = 2\pi\times 50 = 314$ rad/s is the supply. $\omega_n = 1/\sqrt{LC}$, thousands of rad/s, is the ringing. Keep the subscript for the ringing only. Mixing them up is how a correct method gets a wrong number.

11.3 Restriking voltage, recovery voltage, and RRRV

Three terms, routinely confused, and the confusion is worth clearing because questions use all three. The lecture's one-line version (notes p. 3) is the one to remember: restriking voltage is the transient; recovery voltage is the steady state.

TermWhat it isTimescale
Restriking voltage
(transient recovery voltage, TRV)		 The high-frequency transient that appears across the contacts immediately after the current zero — the L–C ringing of §11.2. It overshoots, typically to about twice the peak system voltage. Microseconds. This is what decides whether interruption succeeds.
Recovery voltage The steady-state power-frequency voltage that settles across the open contacts once the transient has died away. Its peak is just the peak system voltage. Milliseconds onward. It decides whether the gap can hold off indefinitely.
RRRV
(rate of rise of restriking voltage)		 $dv/dt$ of the restriking voltage. Units kV/µs. This is the number that is actually compared against the gap's recovery rate. Microseconds.
Key result — the peak voltage the breaker must withstand

The transient rings about the recovery voltage and overshoots by as much again, so with no damping the first peak reaches twice the peak recovery voltage. §11.2 derived exactly this: $v_C = V_m(1-\cos\omega_n t)$ tops out at $2V_m$.

$$v_{\text{peak}} \approx 2 V_m \qquad\text{where}\qquad V_m = V_{LL}\,\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{3}}$$

$V_m$ is the peak phase-to-neutral voltage, and getting this conversion right is the first step of every RRRV calculation. For a 132 kV system: $V_m = 132 \times \sqrt{2}/\sqrt{3} = 107.78\ \text{kV}$.

Two reasons that factor matters. The $\sqrt{3}$ converts line-to-line to phase-to-neutral, because the voltage appears across one pole of the breaker. The $\sqrt{2}$ converts RMS to peak, because the transient is driven by the instantaneous value and the worst case is interruption at the voltage peak — which is exactly what happens, since a fault current lagging 90° reaches zero when the voltage is at its maximum.

Watch out — "effectively earthed" is the phrase that tells you to use $\sqrt{3}$

Major Quiz Q9 specifies "an effectively earthed system", and that phrase is doing work. On an effectively earthed system the healthy phases stay near their normal voltage during an earth fault, so the voltage across one pole of the breaker is the phase-to-neutral value and $V_m = V_{LL}\sqrt{2}/\sqrt{3}$.

The first pole of a three-phase breaker to clear sees a higher recovery voltage than the other two, because until the other poles clear, the neutral point shifts. The revision sheet (p. 7) puts the first-pole-to-clear factor at 1.5 for an ungrounded three-phase fault and about 1.3 for an effectively grounded system.

But neither of his worked numbers applies it. The quiz key for Q9 and the revision sheet's own worked check both take $V_m = \sqrt{2/3}\,V_{LL}$ and stop there. So compute $V_m$ that way by default. Multiply by 1.5 or 1.3 only when a question explicitly asks about the first pole to clear, and quote the factor as a rating fact when asked to explain it.

11.4 RRRV — the lecture, the answer key, and the gap between them

This is the one place in the course where your examiner's own documents disagree. The lecture (notes p. 6) derives $\text{RRRV} = \omega_n V_m\sin\omega_n t$, exactly as §11.2 does. His end-term revision sheet (p. 7), written for the 14 November paper, states the same model: $e(t) = E_m(1-\cos\omega_n t)$, peak $2E_m$ at $t = \pi\sqrt{LC}$, $\text{RRRV}_{\max} = E_m\omega_n$. Only the quiz master copy gives an answer that $\omega_n V_m\cos\omega_n t$ reproduces. So it is now two documents against one, and the two are the more recent and the more deliberate. Both models are below, along with what to write in each kind of question.

Key result — the maximum RRRV, which both models agree on

$$\boxed{\;\text{RRRV}_{\max} \;=\; \omega_n V_m \;=\; 2\pi f_n V_m\;}$$

This is the single most valuable formula in the chapter, because it answers the first and larger half of a 3-mark blank and both models give it. Units: with $V_m$ in volts and $\omega_n$ in rad/s you get V/s; divide by $10^9$ for kV/µs.

Lecture notes (pp. 5–6), revision sheet (p. 7), textbooksQuiz master copy (Major Q9)
Voltage$v(t) = V_m\bigl(1 - \cos \omega_n t\bigr)$ $v(t) = V_m \sin \omega_n t$ (implied)
RRRV$\dfrac{dv}{dt} = \omega_n V_m \sin \omega_n t$ $\dfrac{dv}{dt} = \omega_n V_m \cos \omega_n t$
Maximum RRRV$\omega_n V_m$, at $\omega_n t = 90°$ $\omega_n V_m$, at $t = 0$
Peak voltage$2V_m$, drawn on notes p. 6 $V_m$
RRRV at 100 µs (Q9's numbers)0.398 kV/µs 0.549 kV/µs, the master copy's answer
Watch out — what to write, question by question

The two models agree on the maximum: both give $\text{RRRV}_{\max} = \omega_n V_m$. They split only on "RRRV at a given $t$". Three cases follow from that.

  • Maximum RRRV, any format. $\omega_n V_m$. No conflict, nothing to decide.
  • Derive the restriking voltage / RRRV. Write the lecture's derivation from §11.2, ending at $V_m(1-\cos\omega_n t)$ and $\omega_n V_m\sin\omega_n t$. That is what he taught, and it is the physically correct result.
  • Fill-in "RRRV at $t$ = …", no step marking. The quiz key used $\cos$; the lecture and the end-term revision sheet use the $(1-\cos)$ model, whose rate is $\sin$. The end-term key will be written by the person who wrote that sheet for that paper, so lead with the lecture model. If there is any room to write, put both, labelled: "0.398 kV/µs from $v = V_m(1-\cos\omega_n t)$ [0.549 kV/µs if RRRV $=\omega_n V_m\cos\omega_n t$]". A marker holding either key then finds their number next to the reason for it.

This advice changed on 10 November. Before the revision sheet arrived, the quiz key was the only evidence of marking and this box led with its number. The sheet tips the balance; the reasoning is the same.

Being honest about which is "right". The lecture's model is the physically correct one for the circuit in Figure 11.1(b). The capacitor starts uncharged and the inductor current starts at zero, so the voltage must start with zero slope, which $\sin$ does not do. The $\cos$-RRRV in the answer key is best read as a worst-case envelope: it puts the maximum rate at the current zero itself, the most demanding assumption for a breaker. It is meaningless past the first quarter-cycle. It is also possible the key is simply a slip that was never corrected; the revision sheet makes that more likely, but nothing settles it. If you get the chance to ask him which he wants for "RRRV at $t$", ask. That one answer is worth more than this whole box.

Procedure — RRRV, maximum and at a given instant
  1. Peak phase-to-neutral voltage. $V_m = V_{LL} \times \sqrt{2}/\sqrt{3} = 0.8165\,V_{LL}$. Use the system voltage, not the fault current, and not the breaker's rated voltage if a different system voltage is given.
  2. Natural angular frequency. $\omega_n = 2\pi f_n$. If the question gives $L$ and $C$ instead, $\omega_n = 1/\sqrt{LC}$.
  3. Maximum RRRV. $\omega_n V_m$. Convert: V/s divided by $10^9$ gives kV/µs.
  4. RRRV at a given $t$. Compute $\omega_n t$ in radians. Lecture model: multiply the maximum by $\sin(\omega_n t)$. Answer-key model: multiply by $\cos(\omega_n t)$. Which to write is decided by the question format, per the watch-out above; where there is room, write both, each labelled.
  5. State both with units. kV/µs. The interrupting current is almost always a distractor — it does not enter either formula.
Worked example 1 — the highest-value blank on the paper Major Quiz Q9 · 3 marks

A 132 kV, three-phase circuit breaker interrupts 40 kA at 50 Hz on an effectively earthed system. The restriking voltage has a natural frequency of 1 kHz. Find the maximum RRRV and the RRRV at $t = 100\ \mu\text{s}$.

1. Peak phase-to-neutral voltage:

$$V_m = 132\ \text{kV} \times \frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{3}} = 132 \times 0.8165 = \mathbf{107.78\ kV}$$

2. Natural angular frequency:

$$\omega_n = 2\pi \times 1000 = \mathbf{6283.2\ rad/s}$$

3. Maximum RRRV:

$$\text{RRRV}_{\max} = \omega_n V_m = 6283.2 \times 107.78 \times 10^{3} = 6.772 \times 10^{8}\ \text{V/s} = \mathbf{0.677\ kV/\mu s}$$

4. At $t = 100\ \mu\text{s}$, first the angle:

$$\omega_n t = 6283.2 \times 100 \times 10^{-6} = 0.6283\ \text{rad} = 36.0°$$

Answer-key model: $$\text{RRRV}(100\ \mu\text{s}) = \omega_n V_m \cos(36.0°) = 0.677 \times 0.8090 = \mathbf{0.548\ kV/\mu s}$$

Lecture model: $$\text{RRRV}(100\ \mu\text{s}) = \omega_n V_m \sin(36.0°) = 0.677 \times 0.5878 = \mathbf{0.398\ kV/\mu s}$$

5. 0.677 kV/µs maximum. At 100 µs, 0.398 kV/µs by the lecture and revision-sheet model, or 0.548 kV/µs by the quiz key's convention.

The master copy records 0.678 and 0.549. That differs from the key-model recomputation by one in the third significant figure, which is rounding, so the key's method is confirmed. It does not match the lecture model's 0.398 at all.

Reading the result: 0.677 kV/µs is the rate the breaker's gap must out-recover. Modern $\mathrm{SF}_6$ breakers manage several kV/µs, so this duty is comfortable; an old air-break breaker would not. Note what the number does not depend on: the 40 kA appears nowhere in the calculation. It is a distractor — and a reasonable-looking one, since a breaker's rating is normally quoted in kA. RRRV is set by the system voltage and by the circuit's natural frequency, which is to say by $L$ and $C$, not by how much current you were interrupting. Spotting that in the first ten seconds is worth the three marks.

Worked example 2 — the same formula, different system Lab Quiz Q8

A 220 kV air-blast circuit breaker interrupts 40 kA with a natural frequency of 1 kHz. Find the maximum RRRV across one pole.

$$V_m = 220 \times \frac{\sqrt 2}{\sqrt 3} = 179.63\ \text{kV}$$

$$\text{RRRV}_{\max} = 6283.2 \times 179.63 \times 10^{3} = 1.1286 \times 10^{9}\ \text{V/s} = \mathbf{1.128\ kV/\mu s}$$

Matching the master copy exactly, including the correction written over a struck-through first attempt. This is the same question as Q9 with the voltage changed, which independently confirms the formula $\text{RRRV}_{\max} = \omega_n \times V_{\text{peak, phase-neutral}}$ — and the phrase "across one pole" in the question is the examiner confirming the $\sqrt{3}$.

Note the ratio: $220/132 = 1.667$, and $1.128/0.677 = 1.667$. RRRV is directly proportional to system voltage at fixed $f_n$, which is why higher-voltage breakers are harder to build and is a one-line answer to "why does RRRV matter more at EHV?"

Worked example 3 — starting from $L$ and $C$ revision sheet p. 7

A 132 kV system has $L = 5$ mH and $C = 0.02\ \mu$F looking back from the breaker. Find the natural frequency, the peak restriking voltage and when it occurs, the maximum RRRV, and the resistance across the contacts that makes the recovery non-oscillatory.

1. Natural frequency: $$\omega_n = \frac{1}{\sqrt{LC}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{5\times10^{-3}\times 2\times10^{-8}}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{10^{-10}}} = \mathbf{10^5\ rad/s}, \qquad f_n = \frac{\omega_n}{2\pi} = \mathbf{15.9\ kHz}$$

2. Peak phase-to-neutral voltage: $E_m = \sqrt{2/3}\times 132 = \mathbf{107.8\ kV}$.

3. Peak restriking voltage, at half a natural period: $$e_{\text{peak}} = 2E_m = \mathbf{215.6\ kV} \quad\text{at}\quad t = \pi\sqrt{LC} = \frac{\pi}{10^5} = \mathbf{31.4\ \mu s}$$

4. Maximum RRRV: $$\text{RRRV}_{\max} = E_m\,\omega_n = 107.8\times10^{3}\times10^{5} = 1.078\times10^{10}\ \text{V/s} = \mathbf{10.78\ kV/\mu s}$$

5. Critical resistance (§11.6): $$R = \tfrac12\sqrt{\frac{L}{C}} = 0.5\sqrt{\frac{5\times10^{-3}}{2\times10^{-8}}} = 0.5\sqrt{2.5\times10^{5}} = 0.5\times 500 = \mathbf{250\ \Omega}$$

Compare Worked example 1. Same 132 kV system, but a natural frequency of 15.9 kHz instead of 1 kHz, so the RRRV is about 16 times larger: 10.78 against 0.677 kV/µs. The system voltage sets how high the restriking voltage goes; $L$ and $C$ set how fast it gets there. Note also how short "fast" is: the gap must hold off 215.6 kV just 31.4 µs after the current zero. A 250 Ω resistor across the contacts would remove the overshoot entirely. This is the revision sheet's own example, so expect its shape on the paper.

11.5 Current chopping and capacitive switching

Two special duties worth a paragraph each, because they are the cases where a breaker can be defeated by a small current rather than a large one.

Current chopping

Interrupting a small inductive current — energising current of an unloaded transformer, or a shunt reactor — is harder than interrupting a large one. An air-blast breaker's blast is sized for fault current; against a small current the arc is extinguished prematurely, before the natural current zero. The inductance is then forced to change its current abruptly, and $v = L\,di/dt$ with a near-infinite $di/dt$ produces a severe voltage spike — often far worse than anything a real fault causes. The revision sheet (p. 7) names the risky cases: vacuum and air-blast breakers switching unloaded transformers and reactors, both media strong enough to snap a small arc out early.

How big a spike? The lecture answers with an energy balance (notes p. 4), and it is the cleanest argument in the chapter. At the instant of chopping the inductor is carrying $I_{\text{chop}}$, so it holds $\tfrac12 L I_{\text{chop}}^2$ of magnetic energy. The current path is gone, and the only place that energy can go is into the stray capacitance $C$. It keeps pouring in until the inductor current reaches zero, by which time all of it is stored as electric field.

Key result — the voltage produced by current chopping notes p. 4

$$\tfrac12 L I_{\text{chop}}^2 = \tfrac12 C V_{\text{restrike}}^2 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \boxed{\;V_{\text{restrike}} = I_{\text{chop}}\sqrt{\frac{L}{C}}\;}$$

$\sqrt{L/C}$ has units of ohms and is called the circuit's surge impedance: the ratio of voltage to current in an L–C circuit swapping energy back and forth. A transformer winding has a large $L$ and a tiny stray $C$, so its $\sqrt{L/C}$ is tens of kilohms, and even a few amperes chopped becomes a large voltage. That is the whole reason the small current is the dangerous one.

An upper bound, and it says so. The balance ignores the energy already in $C$ at the chopping instant and every loss in the circuit. Both make the real spike smaller, so read the formula as the worst case.

Worked example 4 — chopping the magnetising current of an unloaded transformer

An 11 kV breaker chops the 5 A magnetising current of an unloaded transformer. The transformer's inductance is 1 H and the stray capacitance at its terminals is 2 nF. Estimate the voltage produced.

$$\sqrt{\frac{L}{C}} = \sqrt{\frac{1}{2\times10^{-9}}} = \sqrt{5\times10^{8}} = 22{,}360\ \Omega$$

$$V_{\text{restrike}} = 5 \times 22{,}360 = 1.12\times10^{5}\ \text{V} = \mathbf{112\ kV}$$

The system's own peak phase-to-neutral voltage is $11 \times 0.8165 = 8.98$ kV. Chopping five amperes produced a spike of roughly twelve times that, from a current smaller than a kettle draws. No insulation on an 11 kV transformer is built for 112 kV, which is why a breaker that "cleanly" interrupted a tiny current can still destroy the thing it was switching. The real spike is lower (see the upper-bound note), but not by a factor of twelve.

The remedy is a resistance across the contacts, which gives the trapped energy a path to dissipate into instead of forcing it all into the capacitance. §11.6 works out how large that resistance should be.

Watch out — the sketch on notes p. 3 is labelled "current chopping" but is not

The graph under the heading "Current chopping (arcing in C.B.)" shows the current running to its natural zero, then a restriking transient that settles to the recovery voltage. That is the normal interruption of §11.3, and it is a good sketch of it. True chopping would show the current snapped to zero before its natural zero. If asked to draw chopping, draw the current cut off mid-slope, then the spike.

Capacitive switching

Interrupting a capacitive current — a cable, a capacitor bank, a lightly loaded line — leaves the capacitor charged to the peak system voltage at the current zero. Half a cycle later the source has reversed, so the voltage across the open contacts is twice the peak, and a restrike at that moment doubles it again. Repeated restrikes can escalate. This is why capacitor banks are switched with breakers specifically rated for the duty, often with pre-insertion resistors.

11.6 Resistance switching — damping the restriking voltage notes pp. 7–8

§11.2 left the circuit ringing forever between $0$ and $2V_m$, because nothing in it could absorb energy. The cure is to give it something that can. The lecture connects a resistor $R$ across the breaker contacts (Figure 11.1c). Since the fault holds the far contact at the return rail, $R$ ends up in parallel with $C$, and every swing of the ringing now drives current through it and turns some of the energy into heat.

The notes draw the resistor through a second, normally open contact, beside the normally closed main one. So the resistor is in circuit only while the breaker is opening. Once the transient has died, that second contact breaks the resistor's own current, which is small and nearly in phase with the voltage, an easy job. (The notes show the two contacts without the sequence; the sequence here is standard practice.)

Derivation — the damped restriking voltage notes pp. 7–8

1. Two equations from the circuit. Kirchhoff around the loop, with the source frozen at $V_m$ as in §11.2 (the notes keep the full $V_m\cos\omega t$, but the damping depends only on the left-hand side):

$$L\frac{di}{dt} + v_C = V_m$$

and at the node, the inductor current splits between the capacitor and the resistor:

$$i = i_C + i_R = C\frac{dv_C}{dt} + \frac{v_C}{R} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \frac{di}{dt} = C\frac{d^2v_C}{dt^2} + \frac1R\frac{dv_C}{dt}$$

2. Substitute and divide by $LC$:

$$\frac{d^2 v_C}{dt^2} + \frac{1}{RC}\frac{dv_C}{dt} + \frac{1}{LC}\,v_C = \frac{V_m}{LC}$$

3. Characteristic roots. The homogeneous equation $s^2 + s/RC + 1/LC = 0$ gives

$$s_{1,2} = -\frac{1}{2RC} \pm j\sqrt{\frac{1}{LC} - \left(\frac{1}{2RC}\right)^2}$$

4. Read off the three regimes. The voltage oscillates only while the quantity under the root is positive:

$$\frac{1}{LC} > \left(\frac{1}{2RC}\right)^2 \;\Longleftrightarrow\; R > \frac12\sqrt{\frac{L}{C}}$$

and the boundary is critical damping:

$$\boxed{\;R_c = \frac12\sqrt{\frac{L}{C}}\;}$$

Watch out — two slips on notes p. 8, and one counter-intuitive direction
  • The notes write the root with $(1/LC)^2$ under the square root. It is $1/LC$, not squared. Check the units: $(1/2RC)^2$ is in s⁻², and so is $1/LC$, but $(1/LC)^2$ is in s⁻⁴.
  • The condition is written "$1/LC > 1/2R$", which has the wrong units. The condition is $1/LC > (1/2RC)^2$, as above. The final answer $R = \tfrac12\sqrt{L/C}$ on the page is correct.
  • In parallel, a larger resistor damps less. An infinite $R$ is no resistor at all, and the circuit rings undamped. The damping ratio is $\zeta = R_c/R$: halve $R$ and you double the damping. A resistor in series behaves the other way round, which is why this one catches people.
Key result — what damping does to the two numbers that matter
$R$$\zeta = R_c/R$Peak voltageMaximum RRRV
none ($R\to\infty$)0$2V_m$$\omega_n V_m$
$3R_c$0.33$1.33V_m$$0.65\,\omega_n V_m$
$R_c$ (critical)1$V_m$, no overshoot$0.37\,\omega_n V_m$

For the underdamped rows, the peak follows the standard second-order overshoot $v_{\text{peak}} = V_m\bigl(1 + e^{-\zeta\pi/\sqrt{1-\zeta^2}}\bigr)$, quoted here from control theory rather than derived. The critical row can be derived in one line. There $v_C = V_m\bigl[1 - e^{-\omega_n t}(1+\omega_n t)\bigr]$, so $dv_C/dt = \omega_n V_m\,(\omega_n t)\,e^{-\omega_n t}$, which is largest at $\omega_n t = 1$, giving $\omega_n V_m/e$.
