Worked example 5 — sizing the resistor for the end-of-chapter checkpoint breaker

The 400 kV breaker of the checkpoint at the end of this chapter sees $L = 5$ mH and $C = 5$ nF. Find the critical resistance, and the peak voltage and maximum RRRV with no resistor, with $R = 1500\ \Omega$, and with $R = R_c$.

1. Critical resistance: $$R_c = \tfrac12\sqrt{\frac{5\times10^{-3}}{5\times10^{-9}}} = \tfrac12\sqrt{10^6} = \mathbf{500\ \Omega}$$

2. The undamped reference: $V_m = 326.6$ kV, $\omega_n = 1/\sqrt{LC} = 2\times10^5$ rad/s, so peak $= 2V_m = 653$ kV and $\text{RRRV}_{\max} = \omega_n V_m = 65.3$ kV/µs.

3. $R = 1500\ \Omega = 3R_c$, so $\zeta = 1/3$. From the table, the peak is $1.33 \times 326.6 = \mathbf{434\ kV}$ and the maximum RRRV is $0.65 \times 65.3 = \mathbf{42\ kV/\mu s}$.

4. $R = 500\ \Omega$: peak $= V_m = \mathbf{327\ kV}$ and maximum RRRV $= 65.3/e = \mathbf{24\ kV/\mu s}$.

A resistor at the critical value removes the overshoot completely and cuts the RRRV to about a third, with no change to the system voltage, $L$ or $C$. Compare the checkpoint's other remedy, a grading capacitor, which halved the RRRV by slowing the oscillation but left the $2V_m$ overshoot untouched. The resistor attacks both numbers, the capacitor only one. That is why the lecture introduces resistance switching straight after the RRRV derivation.

11.7 The interrupting media, compared

The medium between the contacts decides how fast the gap recovers after the current zero, so it decides which side wins the §11.1 race. The lecture ranks the media by breakdown strength: the electric field each will withstand before it breaks down and conducts.

Key result — breakdown strength of the media notes p. 3
AirOilVacuum$\mathrm{SF}_6$ (at 5 bar)
30 kV/cm70 kV/cm170 kV/cm210 kV/cm

These are the lecture's figures, and the ranking is what matters: each step to the right lets a shorter gap hold off the same voltage, so the contacts need to travel less and the breaker opens faster. Real values depend heavily on gap length, pressure, temperature and purity, so use these for ranking and recall, not for design. Air at 30 kV/cm is the classic figure worth knowing cold.

TypeHow it clears the gapWhere it is used, and why
Air-break Lengthens and cools the arc in air using arc runners and splitter plates. LV, up to about 1 kV (revision sheet p. 7). Air's dielectric recovery is slow, so it loses the §11.1 race at high voltage.
Air-blast Blasts compressed air at 20–30 bar through the gap, physically removing the ionised gas. EHV, historically. Very fast recovery and no fire risk, but noisy and needs a compressed-air plant, and prone to current chopping (§11.5). This is the type in Lab Quiz Q8.
Oil (bulk and minimum) The arc decomposes the oil into hydrogen, which has high thermal conductivity and cools the arc; the gas pressure also forces fresh oil in. Older MV and HV installations. Self-generating — the arc makes its own extinguishing medium — but the oil degrades and is a fire risk.
$\mathrm{SF}_6$ Sulphur hexafluoride is strongly electronegative: it captures free electrons, so the gap de-ionises very fast. Puffer or self-blast designs. The modern standard for HV and EHV. Excellent recovery rate, compact. The drawback is that $\mathrm{SF}_6$ is a potent greenhouse gas, which is driving a move to alternatives.
Vacuum With almost no gas there is almost nothing to ionise, and the metal vapour from the contacts disperses in microseconds. The modern standard for MV, 11–33 kV. Extremely fast recovery, long contact life, maintenance-free. Limited at EHV because the gap cannot be made arbitrarily long in a sealed bottle.
Key result — the breaker ratings you may be asked to name
Rated voltageThe system voltage it may be used on, line-to-line RMS.
Rated normal currentThe load current it carries continuously without overheating.
Rated breaking capacityThe symmetrical RMS fault current it can interrupt. Quoted in kA, or as MVA $= \sqrt{3}\,V_{LL}\,I_{\text{break}}$.
Rated making capacityThe peak current it can close onto. Larger than the breaking capacity — about $2.55 \times$ the symmetrical RMS value — because closing onto a fault catches the DC offset at its worst. Where 2.55 comes from: $2.55 \approx 1.8\sqrt2$. The $\sqrt2$ turns RMS into peak; the 1.8 is the doubling effect of the DC offset, a little short of 2 because the offset has already decayed slightly by the first peak.
Short-time ratingThe fault current it can carry without interrupting, for a stated time (1 or 3 s), while a downstream relay is given its grading delay.
Operating duty / sequenceThe standard open–close cycle it can perform, e.g. $O - 0.3\ \text{s} - CO - 3\ \text{min} - CO$, which is what makes auto-reclosing possible.
Worked example 6 — breaking and making capacity revision sheet p. 7

A 132 kV breaker is rated to interrupt 20 kA symmetrical. Find its breaking capacity in MVA and its making capacity.

$$\text{BC} = \sqrt3\,V_{LL}\,I_b = \sqrt3 \times 132 \times 20 = \mathbf{4573\ MVA}$$

$$\text{MC} = 2.55 \times I_{\text{sym}} = 2.55 \times 20 = \mathbf{51\ kA\ peak}$$

The making capacity is a peak current and the breaking capacity is an RMS one, so always state which. The making rating is larger because a breaker closed onto a fault meets it at full asymmetrical value at once, while by the time it interrupts, the DC offset has had a few cycles to decay.

Auto-reclosing revision sheet p. 7

Most faults on overhead lines are transient: a lightning flashover across an insulator, a branch blown onto a conductor. Once the breaker opens and the arc path cools and de-ionises, the line is perfectly healthy again. So the breaker is reclosed automatically after a dead time long enough for that de-ionisation, and supply comes back with nobody touching anything. If the fault was permanent, the relay trips again and the breaker locks out. Distribution circuits use single-shot, three-phase reclosing. EHV lines use single-pole reclosing: only the faulted phase opens, the other two keep carrying power, and so synchronising torque survives the dead time. That is the link back to Chapter 2: single-pole reclosing keeps the post-fault power curve far higher than a three-phase trip would, which enlarges the decelerating area.

11.8 Fuses — the same problem solved by melting

A fuse does the same job with no moving parts, no relay and no CT: a calibrated element melts, an arc forms, and the arc is quenched by the filler packed around it.

Its operating time has two distinct parts, and questions turn on keeping them separate:

Key result — the two phases of fuse operation
Pre-arcing
(melting) time		 From the fault starting to the element melting. The element is still conducting throughout.
Arcing time From the melt to the arc being extinguished and the current reaching zero.
Total operating time The sum of the two.

Each phase is characterised by an $I^2t$ value — the energy let through per ohm — and for a given prospective fault current:

$$t = \frac{I^2 t \text{ value}}{I^2}$$

So a fuse specified by its $I^2t$ can have its timing computed for any fault current, which is the whole point of quoting it that way.

Why it happens — why $I^2t$ and not just a time

Melting the element needs a fixed quantity of heat, and the heat delivered is $\int i^2 R\,dt$. For a fixed element, $R$ is fixed, so the melting condition is $\int i^2\,dt = \text{constant}$ — a constant characteristic of the fuse, independent of what current happens to flow.

That is why the $I^2t$ value, not the time, is what gets printed on the datasheet. It makes the fuse a single number instead of a curve. And it is the same argument that made equipment damage curves inverse in §6.1, which is why fuses and extremely inverse relays grade well against each other (the checkpoint in §6.5).

And it explains the fuse's great advantage: a fuse can interrupt before the first current peak, limiting the $I^2t$ that reaches the protected equipment to far less than a breaker would. The current at which it melts before the peak is the cut-off current, and this current-limiting behaviour is the reason fuses survive alongside breakers.

Worked example 7 — fuse timing Major Quiz Q8

A 10 A cartridge fuse has a pre-arcing $I^2t$ of $1.8 \times 10^{4}\ \text{A}^2\text{s}$ and an arcing value of $0.6 \times 10^{4}\ \text{A}^2\text{s}$. The prospective fault current is 400 A. Find the pre-arcing time and the total operating time.

1. Square the current once and reuse it:

$$I^2 = 400^2 = 1.6 \times 10^{5}\ \text{A}^2$$

2. Pre-arcing time:

$$t_{\text{pre}} = \frac{1.8 \times 10^{4}}{1.6 \times 10^{5}} = \mathbf{0.1125\ s}$$

3. Total — add the two $I^2t$ values first, then divide once:

$$t_{\text{total}} = \frac{(1.8 + 0.6) \times 10^{4}}{1.6 \times 10^{5}} = \frac{2.4 \times 10^{4}}{1.6 \times 10^{5}} = \mathbf{0.15\ s}$$

The arcing phase accounts for $0.15 - 0.1125 = 0.0375\ \text{s}$ — a quarter of the total, which is typical: melting dominates and arcing is the shorter tail. Both figures are comfortably under one power-frequency cycle plus a bit (20 ms per cycle, so 0.15 s is about 7.5 cycles), which makes this fuse slower than a modern breaker and places it as protection for a small feeder rather than for a transmission line.

The 10 A rating is a distractor for this calculation — it tells you the fuse's continuous current, not its timing. But it is not irrelevant: the fault current is 40 times the rating, which is why the fuse operates in a fraction of a second rather than the many seconds it would take at a modest overload. That is the same inverse behaviour as §6.6's worked example 2, for the same $I^2t$ reason.

Discriminating between two fuses

Two fuses in series — an upstream and a downstream — discriminate correctly if the downstream fuse has finished arcing before the upstream one has even melted:

$$\bigl(I^2 t\bigr)_{\text{total, downstream}} \;<\; \bigl(I^2 t\bigr)_{\text{pre-arcing, upstream}}$$

Note that the comparison is between different phases of the two fuses — total for the one that should operate, pre-arcing for the one that should not. A rule of thumb is a 2:1 ratio of current ratings, but the $I^2t$ comparison is the real test.

Exam note — four blanks from this chapter, and where the lecture now stands behind them
QuestionAsks forSection
Major Q9 [3 marks]Max RRRV and RRRV at $t$ — the largest single blank on either paper§11.4
Lab Q8Max RRRV for an air-blast breaker §11.4
Major Q8Fuse pre-arcing and total time §11.8
Major Q7Numerical relay samples per cycle §5.9

Three things follow. These are the cheapest marks on the paper — each is one substitution into one formula, and between them they are about 6 of the 20 Part-I marks. The distractors are consistent: the interrupting current in both RRRV questions and the fuse rating in the $I^2t$ question are all unused. And the lecture now covers the RRRV half with a full derivation (§11.2) and two new results that no quiz has asked yet: the chopping voltage $I\sqrt{L/C}$ (§11.5) and the critical resistance $\tfrac12\sqrt{L/C}$ (§11.6). Freshly taught derivations with short closed-form answers are exactly what a longer end-term question is built from. Derive with the lecture's $(1-\cos)$ model; for a bare "RRRV at $t$" blank, follow the §11.4 watch-out.

What the end-term revision sheet adds. Its single worked check (Worked example 3) goes from $L$ and $C$ to $f_n$, the peak restriking voltage and its time, $\text{RRRV}_{\max}$ and the critical resistance, all in one question. Expect that shape. Its "Remember" list targets four slips: interruption is always at a current zero; restriking voltage (transient) is not recovery voltage (steady state); making capacity exceeds breaking because of the DC offset; and chopping is dangerous because the current was small.

Checkpoint

A 400 kV breaker on an effectively earthed system interrupts a fault, and the circuit has $L = 5\ \text{mH}$ and $C = 5\ \text{nF}$ looking back from the breaker. Find the natural frequency and the maximum RRRV. Then say what one cheap modification would reduce the RRRV, and by how much.

Answer. $f_n = 1/(2\pi\sqrt{LC}) = 1/(2\pi\sqrt{5 \times 10^{-3} \times 5 \times 10^{-9}}) = 1/(2\pi \times 5 \times 10^{-6}) = 31.8\ \text{kHz}$, so $\omega_n = 2 \times 10^{5}\ \text{rad/s}$. $V_m = 400 \times 0.8165 = 326.6\ \text{kV}$. Hence $\text{RRRV}_{\max} = 2 \times 10^{5} \times 326.6 \times 10^{3} = 6.53 \times 10^{10}\ \text{V/s} = \mathbf{65.3\ kV/\mu s}$ — a hundred times the duty in Q9, and a severe one.

The modification: fit a capacitor across the breaker contacts, which increases $C$ and therefore lowers $\omega_n$ as $1/\sqrt{C}$. Since $\text{RRRV}_{\max} = \omega_n V_m$ and $V_m$ is unchanged, quadrupling $C$ from 5 nF to 20 nF halves the RRRV to 32.7 kV/µs. That is the §11.2 result put to work, and it is why grading capacitors are standard on EHV breakers. A resistance across the contacts does the same job by damping the oscillation rather than slowing it, and is the remedy for current chopping too. §11.6 sizes it for this exact breaker: $R_c = 500\ \Omega$ removes the overshoot and cuts the RRRV to 24 kV/µs.

Chapter 12 revision machine

Synthesis, Worked Quizzes, Formula Sheet and Self-Test

Everything from here on is designed to be re-entered in twenty minutes. Four sections: what the whole course actually says, a decision tree that identifies any protection question in four steps, both quizzes to sit cold, and the formula sheet. Then the self-test, the lab write-up template, and a triage list for the night before.

12.1 The whole course, in one page

Three clocks, from Chapter 1 — and now with the content filled in.

MillisecondsSeconds–minutesMinutes–hours
QuestionWill it survive?Is it at 50 Hz? What does it cost?
Governing equation $\dfrac{2H}{\omega_s}\ddot\delta = P_m - P_e$ $\Delta f = -\dfrac{\Delta P_D}{\beta}$ $\dfrac{dC_i}{dP_i} = \lambda$
The key number $t_{cr}$ — the deadline$\beta$ — the stiffness $\lambda$ — the marginal cost
What actsRelays, then breakers. No human. Governors, then AGC.An optimisation, reviewed by people.
If you get it wrongYou lose the system Frequency drifts; equipment complainsIt costs more
Key result — the four sentences worth memorising
  1. Stability sets the deadline; protection meets it. $t_{\text{relay}} + t_{\text{breaker}} < t_{cr}$. This is the only link between the two halves of the course and it is worth a sentence in any answer about why speed matters.
  2. A quadratic's discriminant decides feasibility. In voltage stability, a negative discriminant means the load cannot be delivered at any voltage. In economic operation, the cost quadratics plus the constraint set decide whether a dispatch exists.
  3. One shared scalar coordinates many machines. $\lambda$ in dispatch, $f$ in load-frequency control. Each unit needs to know only the shared number and its own curve — never anything about any other unit.
  4. A deliberate steady-state error is the coordination mechanism. Governor droop. An isochronous governor would fight its neighbours; a drooping one shares load stably and leaves a residual error for the next clock down to clean up.

Items 2–4 are your lecturer's own, from the closing paragraph of his revision sheet. His comment: "If you can explain those three sentences, you understand the course."

12.2 Identifying any protection question in four steps

Seven chapters of protection, and under exam pressure the first problem is working out which of them a question belongs to. The tell is always the same: what the relay is given to measure.

12.3 Sit both quizzes

These are the two post-midterm papers, reproduced as questions. Do them cold, before revising anything, in about 35 minutes for the pair — that is the real time allowance. Then open the reveals.

Every answer below is from the examiner's own filled master copy, and every one has been independently recomputed. Where he and the standard textbook disagree, the reveal says so.

Major Quiz — Part I · 20 marks · no step marking

  1. [Differential] A busbar differential scheme has three feeders with secondary currents 10 A, 8 A and 6 A. The relay has 50% bias and a pickup setting $k_0 = 0.5\ \text{A}$. The measured spill current is 5.2 A. Restraint = ______ A. Operating threshold = ______ A.
    Answer

    12 A and 6.5 A. Restraint $= (10+8+6)/2 = 12$; threshold $= 0.5 + 0.5(12) = 6.5$. Since $5.2 < 6.5$, no trip. Worked in full at §9.6. Trap: the restraint is half the sum of all three, not an average of two.

  2. [V/f] A V/f relay on a 132 kV, 50 Hz transformer trips when V/f falls below 85% of nominal. Measured: 105 kV, 45 Hz. Nominal = ______ · Threshold = ______ · Actual = ______ · Action = ______
    Answer

    2.64, 2.244, 2.333 kV/Hz, no trip. Both V and f fell together, so the ratio barely moved. §8.6.

  3. [Distance] A line has $Z_L = 10 + j40\ \Omega$. An MHO relay reaches 70% of it. A fault at 60% of the line has 20 Ω of arc resistance in series. Apparent impedance = ______ · its magnitude = ______ · reach = ______ · the relay will ______
    Answer

    26 + j24 Ω, 35.39 Ω, 28.86 Ω, not trip. Under-reach caused entirely by the arc: without it the apparent impedance would be 24.74 Ω, comfortably inside. §10.4.

    The real trap is keeping the three numbers in the right blanks — the examiner himself put them in the wrong order on the master copy and corrected it.

  4. [Directional] A directional OC relay on the 11 kV side of a 33/11 kV star–delta transformer (30° shift). CT 200/1, plug setting 120% of secondary, MTA = 30° lag. A 33 kV-side fault makes the current lag the HV bus voltage by 150°. Primary fault current 300 A. Angle seen = ______ ° · PSM = ______ · Final action = ______
    Answer

    120°, 1.25, will not operate. $|\theta - \tau| = |120 - 30| = 90°$ exactly — the zero-torque boundary, which does not operate. A deliberately placed boundary case. §7.3.

  5. [Generator] A 25 MVA, 11 kV, 0.9 pf lagging alternator delivers 20 MW to an infinite bus. The prime mover fails, excitation held constant. Accelerating power = ______ MW · Relay used = ______
    Answer

    −20 MW and reverse power relay. $P_a = P_m - P_e = 0 - 20$. The master copy also ticks "directional OCR" — the same device viewed differently. §8.7.

  6. [Dispatch] $dC_1/dP_1 = 20 + 0.02P_1$, $dC_2/dP_2 = 15 + 0.03P_2$, $P_L = 0.0002P_1^2 + 0.0001P_2^2$, $\lambda = 25$. $L_1$ = ______ · $L_2$ = ______
    Answer

    1.11 and 1.07, by the examiner's route: find the lossless dispatch first ($P_1 = 250$, $P_2 = 333.3$), then evaluate the penalty factors there. The rigorous simultaneous solution gives 1.071 and 1.061 and scores zero. §3.5 — read it if you have not.

  7. [Numerical relay] A relay samples at 1.92 kHz on a 50 Hz system. Samples per cycle = ______
    Answer

    38.4. Do not round — a non-integer means the sampling clock is not locked to the power frequency. §5.9.

  8. [Fuse] A 10 A fuse has pre-arcing $I^2t = 1.8 \times 10^4\ \text{A}^2\text{s}$ and arcing $0.6 \times 10^4$. Prospective fault current 400 A. Pre-arcing time = ______ s · Total operating time = ______ s
    Answer

    0.1125 s and 0.15 s. $I^2 = 1.6\times10^5$; add the two $I^2t$ values before dividing for the total. The 10 A rating is a distractor. §11.8.

  9. [Circuit breaker — 3 marks] A 132 kV, three-phase breaker interrupts 40 kA at 50 Hz on an effectively earthed system. Restriking voltage natural frequency 1 kHz. Maximum RRRV = ______ kV/µs · RRRV at $t = 100\ \mu\text{s}$ = ______ kV/µs
    Answer

    0.678 and 0.549 kV/µs (recomputation gives 0.677 and 0.548 — rounding). $V_m = 132\sqrt2/\sqrt3 = 107.78\ \text{kV}$; $\omega_n = 6283.2$; $\text{RRRV}_{\max} = \omega_n V_m$.

    The second blank is the one that matters. The master copy's 0.549 comes from $\text{RRRV}(t) = \omega_n V_m \cos\omega_n t$. Your lecture notes (pp. 5–6) and the end-term revision sheet (p. 7) use $V_m(1-\cos\omega_n t)$, whose rate is $\omega_n V_m \sin\omega_n t$, giving 0.398. For the end-term, lead with 0.398 and add 0.549 with its formula where there is room. §11.4. The 40 kA is a distractor.

  10. [Droop] Two generators share a 200 MW load. G1: 100 MW, 5% droop. G2: 150 MW, 4% droop. Frequency falls 50 → 49.5 Hz. $\Delta G_1$ = ______ MW · $\Delta G_2$ = ______ MW
    Answer

    20 MW and 37.5 MW. $\Delta P = (\Delta f/f)(P_{\text{rated}}/R_\%) = 0.01 \times 100/0.05$ and $0.01 \times 150/0.04$. The 200 MW is a distractor. §4.4.

Lab Quiz · 8 fill-ins + a 3-mark write-up · 11 marks

  1. [EAC] A 50 Hz, 2-pole generator operates at 1.0 pu before a fault. After clearing, $P_{\max}$ reduces to 0.8 pu. The critical clearing angle is found to be 60°. The system is stable only if the fault is cleared within ______ °
    Answer

    60°. The answer is restated in the question — this examiner does plant trivial items, so read before calculating.

  2. [Dispatch] With $\lambda = 25$ and the cost data of Major Q6, determine the optimal generation. $P_1$ = ______ MW · $P_2$ = ______ MW
    Answer

    250 and 333.3 MW. The lossless $\lambda$-dispatch of §3.5 step 1 — the same data as Major Q6, split across two papers.

  3. [Two-area AGC] $B_1 = 20$, $B_2 = 25\ \text{MW/Hz}$. The steady-state $\Delta f$ after a 100 MW load increase in Area 1 is 0.1 Hz. Tie-line power deviation from Area 1 to Area 2 = ______ MW
    Answer

    2 MW, from $\text{ACE}_1 = 0 \Rightarrow |\Delta P_{\text{tie}}| = B_1|\Delta f| = 20 \times 0.1$.

    The question's data is internally inconsistent — the textbook steady state from a 100 MW step would be $-2.22\ \text{Hz}$, not 0.1. Take the 0.1 Hz as given and apply ACE = 0. §4.5.

  4. [Distance] An MHO relay covers 80% of a 200 km line of $0.2 + j0.8\ \Omega/\text{km}$. Reach setting magnitude = ______ Ω
    Answer

    132 Ω. $200(0.2+j0.8) = 40+j160$; $\times 0.8 = 32+j128$; $|32+j128| = 131.94$. §10.4.

  5. [Extremely inverse] A 5 A relay at plug setting 125% with TMS = 0.2 carries 8 A. Operating time = ______ s
    Answer

    25.06 s. $\text{PSM} = 8/6.25 = 1.28$; $t = 80(0.2)/(1.28^2 - 1) = 16/0.6384$. Twenty-five seconds is correct — PSM is barely above 1, so this is an overload, not a fault. §6.6.

  6. [Percentage bias] An 11/132 kV transformer has CTs 600/5 on LV and 40/1 on HV. During a through fault the differential current is 0.1 A and the average restraining current 1.0 A. Percentage bias = ______ %
    Answer

    10%. $(0.1/1.0) \times 100$. Both CT ratios are distractors. §9.6.

  7. [Very inverse] PSM = 5, TMS = 0.1, very inverse. Operating time = ______ s
    Answer

    0.3375 s. $t = 13.5(0.1)/(5-1) = 1.35/4$. §6.6.

  8. [Air-blast breaker] A 220 kV air-blast breaker interrupts 40 kA with a natural frequency of 1 kHz. Maximum RRRV across one pole ≈ ______ kV/µs
    Answer

    1.128 kV/µs. $V_m = 220\sqrt2/\sqrt3 = 179.63$; $\times 6283.2$. §11.4.

12.4 The lab write-up template — 3 marks for a recallable page

Exam note — the highest marks-per-minute item on either paper

The Lab Quiz bonus, verbatim: "Write a short experimental report on any one hardware experiment, including a schematic diagram with the measurement equipment connected, objectives, learning outcomes and conclusion." Three marks, no calculation, and the four required headings are named in the question.

Prepare one experiment in this shape before the exam and you have three marks in five minutes.

Template — hardware experiment write-up
  1. Title and aim, one line. "To determine the operating-time characteristic of an IDMT overcurrent relay and verify its inverse time/current relationship."
  2. Schematic diagram with the instruments shown connected. The question asks for this explicitly, so draw it and label every instrument. For the relay experiment: a single-phase variable AC supply → an ammeter in series → the relay's current coil → a timer started by the supply contactor and stopped by the relay's trip contact. Mark the CT ratio, the plug setting and the TMS dial.
  3. Procedure, three or four numbered steps. Set the plug setting and TMS; inject a known current and record it on the ammeter; record the operating time; repeat for a range of currents giving PSM from about 2 to 20.
  4. Observations — a table with columns: injected current, PSM, measured time, time calculated from $t = k\,\text{TMS}/(\text{PSM}^{\alpha}-1)$, and percentage error.
  5. Learning outcomes, two or three bullets. That operating time falls as current rises, and the shape is set by $(k, \alpha)$. That TMS scales the whole curve without changing its shape, while the plug setting moves the pickup. That the measurement confirms the IEC constants used in the settings calculation.
  6. Conclusion, two sentences. The measured characteristic matched the IEC normal-inverse curve within the instrument tolerance, confirming the $(k, \alpha)$ pair. Discrepancies at low PSM are expected because the relay's own mechanical overshoot becomes a larger fraction of a long operating time.

Any of the course's experiments fits the same six headings — percentage-bias differential, MHO reach, or the equal-area/stability simulation. The schematic is the part that carries the marks, because it is the part the question names.

12.5 Formula sheet

Clock 1 — milliseconds: stability

Swing equation$\dfrac{2H}{\omega_s}\dfrac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_m - P_e$, $\;P_e = P_{\max}\sin\delta$, $\;P_{\max} = E'V/X$
Equilibria$\delta_0 = \sin^{-1}(P_m/P_{\max})$ stable; $\pi - \delta_0$ unstable
Equal-area criterion$\displaystyle\int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_{\max}} (P_m - P_e)\,d\delta = 0 \iff A_1 = A_2$
Max step (Case A)$(\pi - \delta_0 - \delta_1)\sin\delta_1 = \cos\delta_0 + \cos\delta_1$
Terminal fault (Case B) $\cos\delta_{cr} = (\pi - 2\delta_0)\sin\delta_0 - \cos\delta_0$; $\;t_{cr} = \sqrt{4H(\delta_{cr}-\delta_0)/(\omega_s P_m)}$
Parallel lines (Case C) $\cos\delta_{cr} = \dfrac{(P_m/P^{pre}_{\max})(\delta_{\max}-\delta_0) + r_2\cos\delta_{\max} - r_1\cos\delta_0}{r_2 - r_1}$; $\;\delta_{\max} = \pi - \sin^{-1}\!\bigl(P_m/(r_2 P^{pre}_{\max})\bigr)$
Point-by-point$\Delta\delta_n = \Delta\delta_{n-1} + k P_{a(n-1)}$, $\;k = 180 f_0 (\Delta t)^2 / H$ deg/pu; average $P_a$ at a switching instant
Voltage stability $V^2 = \tfrac{E^2}{2} - QX \pm \sqrt{\tfrac{E^4}{4} - X^2P^2 - XQE^2}$; UPF: $P_{\max} = E^2/2X$, $V_{crit} = E/\sqrt2$

Clock 2 — seconds: load-frequency control

Droop$\Delta P_m = -\Delta f / R$
Steady-state error $\Delta f = -\Delta P_D/(B + 1/R) = -\Delta P_D/\beta \approx -R\,\Delta P_D$
Area frequency response$\beta = 1/R + B$ pu MW/Hz
With speed changer$\Delta f = (\Delta P_C - \Delta P_D)/(B + 1/R)$; $\;\Delta P_C = \Delta P_D \Rightarrow \Delta f = 0$
Plant constants$K_{ps} = 1/B$, $\;T_{ps} = 2H/(Bf^0)$; typical $T_{sg}=0.4$, $T_t=0.5$, $T_{ps}=20$ s
Dynamic response $\Delta f(t) = -\Bigl[\tfrac{RK_{ps}}{R+K_{ps}}\Bigr] \bigl\{1 - e^{-(t/T_{ps})(R+K_{ps})/R}\bigr\}\Delta P_D$; $\;T_{\text{closed}} = T_{ps}R/(R+K_{ps})$
Load sharing$\Delta f / f = R_\% \times (\text{MW carried} / \text{own rating})$; $\;R_{sys} = R_{mach}S_{sys}/S_{mach}$
Tie line$\Delta P_{tie,1} = T_{12}(\Delta\delta_1 - \Delta\delta_2)$; $\;T_{12} = \dfrac{|V_1||V_2|}{P_{r1}X_{12}}\cos(\delta^0_1 - \delta^0_2)$; $\;\Delta P_{tie,1}(s) = \tfrac{2\pi T_{12}}{s}[\Delta F_1 - \Delta F_2]$
Area ratio$a_{12} = P_{r1}/P_{r2}$ (positive); $\;T_{21} = a_{12}T_{12}$; $\;\Delta P_{tie,2} = -a_{12}\Delta P_{tie,1}$
ACE$\text{ACE}_i = \Delta P_{tie,i} + b_i\Delta f_i$, $\;b_i \approx \beta_i$
Two-area steady state $\Delta f = -\Delta P_{D1}/(\beta_1 + \beta_2/a_{12})$; $\;\Delta P_{tie,1} = \beta_2\Delta f/a_{12}$; $\;\text{ACE}_1 = -\Delta P_{D1}$, $\text{ACE}_2 = 0$

Clock 3 — hours: economic operation

Cost and incremental cost $C_i = a_iP_i^2 + b_iP_i + c_i$; $\;\text{IC}_i = 2a_iP_i + b_i$
Lossless optimum$dC_i/dP_i = \lambda$ for every free unit; $\;\sum P_i = P_D$
KKT at limitsat $P^{\max}$: $\text{IC} \le \lambda$; at $P^{\min}$: $\text{IC} \ge \lambda$
With losses$L_i\,\text{IC}_i = \lambda$; $\;L_i = 1/(1 - \partial P_L/\partial P_i)$; $\;P_L = \sum_i\sum_j P_iB_{ij}P_j$; $\;\sum P_i = P_D + P_L$
Unit commitment $\min\sum_t\sum_i[u_{it}C_i(P_{it}) + SU_{it}]$ s.t. $\sum u_{it}P_{it} = P_{D,t}$ and $\sum u_{it}P_i^{\max} \ge P_{D,t} + R_t$
Priority listrank by $C_i(P_i^{\max})/P_i^{\max}$; average cost ranks, incremental cost dispatches

Clock 1 — milliseconds: protection and switchgear

CT referral$I_{\text{sec}} = I_{\text{pri}}/\text{CT ratio}$
Pickup window$I_{L,\max} < I_{pu} < I_{f,\min}$, with a 25% overload margin
PSM$\text{PSM} = I_{\text{relay}}/ (\text{PS}\times I_{\text{rated}})$
IDMT (IEC) $t_{op} = \dfrac{k\,\text{TMS}}{\text{PSM}^{\alpha}-1}$;   normal 0.14 / 0.02 · very 13.5 / 1 · extremely 80 / 2 · long-time 120 / 1 (machine thermal, §6.8)
Grading$T_{RA} \ge T_{RB} + T_{CB,B} + T_{OS,A}$, with $T_{OS} = 0.1(T_{RB}+T_{CB,B})$, so $T_{RA} \ge 1.1(T_{RB}+T_{CB,B})$ when breaker time is given; otherwise CTI $\approx$ 0.3–0.4 s (revision sheet uses 0.35). Grade at max fault current, set plugs from max load. §7.6
90°–τ connectionmax torque when $I_a$ lags $V_a$ by $(90°-\tau)$; $T/T_{\max} = \cos(\text{departure})$. §7.7
Clearing time$t_{\text{clear}} = t_{\text{relay}} + t_{\text{breaker}} < t_{cr}$. §5.12
Directional$T \propto VI\cos(\theta-\tau)$; $|\theta-\tau| < 90°$ operate; $= 90°$ zero torque, no operation; $> 90°$ restrain
Residual current$I_{res} = I_a + I_b + I_c = 3I_0$
Differential trip if $|I_1 - I_2| > k_0 + k\bigl(\tfrac{I_1+I_2}{2}\bigr)$; restraint $= \tfrac12\sum|I_n|$
Dual slopebeyond breakpoint BP: threshold $= \text{SLP1}\times\text{BP} + \text{SLP2}(I_{rt}-\text{BP})$, never $\text{SLP2}\times I_{rt}$. Stability ratio $= I_{\text{through,max}}/I_{op,\min}$, both referred to the same side. §9.8
Busbar (high-Z)$V_s = I_{f,\text{sec}}(R_{CT} + 2R_L)$; $V_k \ge 2V_s$; $R_{\text{stab}} = V_s/I_{\text{relay}}$; $I_{\text{pri}} = \text{CTR}\bigl(I_{\text{relay}} + \sum I_{\text{mag}}\bigr)$. §9.10
Generator$I_2^2 t = K$ (10–40); 1-ph load $I_2 = I_a/3$; single-phasing $I_2 = I/\sqrt3$. LOE offset mho: diameter $X_d$, offset $X_d'/2$ into $-X$, $Z_{\text{base}} = \text{kV}^2/\text{MVA}$. §8.9
Transformer CT matching$\text{CT}_{LV}/\text{CT}_{HV} = V_{HV}/V_{LV}$; inrush blocked on 2nd harmonic (15–20%); overfluxing on 5th. §9.9
Flux$E = 4.44 f\phi_m N \Rightarrow \phi_m \propto E/f$; inrush peak $\approx 2\phi_m + \phi_r \approx 2.8\phi_m$
Distance $Z_{app} = pZ_L + R_{arc}$; reach $= (\%)\times|Z_L|$; MHO: $|Z| < D\cos(\theta-\tau)$, circle of diameter $D$ through the origin
ZonesZ1 80% instantaneous · Z2 120% (line + 50% of shortest next) 0.3–0.4 s · Z3 own line + 100–120% of longest next, 0.8–1.0 s (revision sheet values, §10.3)
Secondary ohms$Z_{\text{sec}} = Z_{\text{pri}} \times \text{CT ratio}/\text{VT ratio}$. Reactance relay reads $\text{Im}(Z)$ only; infeed $\Rightarrow$ under-reach. §10.8–10.9
Residual compensation$Z = \dfrac{V_a}{I_a + kI_{res}}$, $\;k = \dfrac{Z_0 - Z_1}{3Z_1}$ ($\approx 0.67$ for an overhead line)
Arc resistance$R_{arc} = 28{,}710\,L/I^{1.4}\ \Omega$ ($L$ in m, $I$ in A)
Breaker natural frequency$f_n = 1/(2\pi\sqrt{LC})$, $\;\omega_n = 1/\sqrt{LC}$
RRRV $V_m = V_{LL}\sqrt2/\sqrt3$;   $\text{RRRV}_{\max} = \omega_n V_m$;   at $t$: lecture $\omega_n V_m\sin\omega_n t$, quiz key $\omega_n V_m\cos\omega_n t$ (§11.4 says which to write)
Restriking voltage$v_C = V_m(1-\cos\omega_n t)$, peak $2V_m$ at $\omega_n t = \pi$ (source frozen at $V_m$)
Current chopping$\tfrac12 L I^2 = \tfrac12 C V^2 \Rightarrow V = I_{\text{chop}}\sqrt{L/C}$ (upper bound)
Resistance switching$R$ across contacts $\parallel C$; $s = -\tfrac{1}{2RC} \pm j\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{LC} - \left(\tfrac{1}{2RC}\right)^2}$; critical $R_c = \tfrac12\sqrt{L/C}$; $\zeta = R_c/R$; at $R_c$: RRRV$_{\max} = \omega_n V_m/e$
Breakdown strengthair 30 · oil 70 · vacuum 170 · $\mathrm{SF}_6$ 210 kV/cm (5 bar), lecture figures
Breaker ratingsBC $= \sqrt3\,V_L I_b$ (MVA); MC $= 2.55\,I_{\text{sym}}$ peak, $2.55 \approx 1.8\sqrt2$; first-pole-to-clear 1.5 ungrounded, 1.3 effectively grounded (neither applied in his worked numbers). §11.3, §11.7
Fuse$t = (I^2t\text{ value})/I^2$; total $=$ pre-arcing $+$ arcing; discriminate if $(I^2t)_{\text{total,down}} < (I^2t)_{\text{pre-arc,up}}$
Numerical relay$N = f_s/f$ samples per cycle; Nyquist $f_s/2$; anti-alias filter before the sampler

12.6 Self-test

Conceptual

  1. Why does a closed-form $t_{cr}$ exist for a terminal fault but not for a fault on one of two parallel lines?
    Answer

    For a terminal fault $P_e = 0$, so $P_a = P_m$ is constant, the swing equation becomes constant acceleration, and $\delta(t)$ is an exact parabola that inverts directly. For any other fault $P_e = P_{\max}\sin\delta$ varies with $\delta$, the equation is non-linear, and $\delta(t)$ has no closed form — you can still get $\delta_{cr}$ from the areas but must integrate numerically for the time. This was midterm Q6(e). §2.5

  2. What does the inertia constant $H$ physically mean, and what happens to $t_{cr}$ if it doubles?
    Answer

    $H$ is in MJ/MVA, which has units of seconds: roughly how long the machine could supply rated output from stored rotation alone. Since $t_{cr} \propto \sqrt{H}$, doubling it increases $t_{cr}$ by only $\sqrt2 = 1.41$ — a 41% gain, not 100%. §2.1

  3. Why must protection zones overlap rather than meet at a point?
    Answer

    Zone boundaries are defined by CT positions. If two zones met at one CT, a fault between that CT and the breaker would be outside both zones and nobody would trip. Overlapping means such a fault is seen by both — wasteful but fail-safe. §5.3

  4. Give the three terms of the coordination time interval and the course's overshoot rule.
    Answer

    $T_{RA} \ge T_{RB} + T_{CB,B} + T_{OS,A}$ — downstream relay time, downstream breaker time, upstream relay overshoot — with $T_{OS} = 0.1(T_{RB} + T_{CB,B})$, hence $T_{RA} \ge 1.1(T_{RB} + T_{CB,B})$. §5.5

  5. Why is a protective CT built differently from a metering CT?
    Answer

    A metering CT must be accurate around normal load and may saturate above it — which also protects the meter. A protective CT must stay linear to about twenty times rating, because that is the only region in which a relay has to judge anything, so it has a larger core and a longer linear region. Saturation makes a CT under-read a fault. §5.6

  6. Why is an inverse time/current characteristic the right shape? Give both reasons.
    Answer

    Damage: equipment damage curves are $\int i^2R\,dt$, so permissible time scales as $1/I^2$ — an inverse relay sits parallel to them. Grading: a distant fault draws less current, so an inverse characteristic makes the nearest relay automatically the fastest, with no arrangement needed. §6.1

  7. What is the difference between plug setting and time multiplier setting?
    Answer

    PS = sensitivity — it sets the current at which the relay picks up at all. TMS = selectivity — it scales the whole operating time without changing the curve's shape or the pickup. Your notes give exactly that one-line form. §6.2

  8. Why is the directional relay's operating region a 180° half-plane rather than a narrow band around $\tau$?
    Answer

    Because the torque goes as $\cos(\theta-\tau)$, whose sign — all a contact can respond to — is positive for the whole range $|\theta-\tau| < 90°$. A half-plane is also what you want: reversing the current shifts $\theta$ by 180°, which moves any operating point into the restraining region for every power factor. §7.2

  9. No bias slope can distinguish transformer inrush from an internal fault. Why, and what does distinguish them?
    Answer

    Inrush draws current into one winding and nothing out of the other, so the spill is the whole inrush current while the restraint is only half of it — the same trajectory as an internal fault, with gradient 2 on the bias plane, which no practical slope can sit above. They are distinguished by second-harmonic content (15–20% in inrush per the revision sheet, little in a fault), or by waveform dead-interval detection. §8.5, §9.7

  10. Why is the MHO relay inherently directional while the impedance relay is not?
    Answer

    The impedance relay's condition is $|Z| < \text{const}$, a disc centred on the origin, which contains points in every direction. The MHO's is $|Z| < D\cos(\theta-\tau)$, and $\cos$ is negative beyond 90° from $\tau$ while $|Z|$ cannot be — so the whole circle lies on one side of the origin and a reverse fault cannot be inside it. §10.2

  11. Why does arc resistance cause under-reach rather than over-reach?
    Answer

    Arc resistance is purely resistive, so it moves the measured point horizontally on the R–X plane — away from the line impedance vector and out of the MHO circle, which is narrow in the $R$ direction because its diameter is tilted along the line angle. The relay therefore sees a larger impedance and judges the fault further away than it is. §10.4

  12. Why can governor droop never restore 50 Hz, and what can?
    Answer

    $\Delta f = -\Delta P_D/\beta$ is zero only if $\Delta P_D$ is zero, so any real load change leaves an error — by design, because the sloping characteristic is what makes decentralised load sharing stable. An integral controller (AGC) restores it: integral action keeps acting until the error is exactly zero, which proportional action cannot. §4.3

  13. In a two-area system with $b_i = \beta_i$, why does only the disturbed area's AGC respond?
    Answer

    Substituting the steady-state $\Delta f$ and $\Delta P_{tie}$ into $\text{ACE}_i = \Delta P_{tie,i} + b_i\Delta f_i$ gives $\text{ACE}_1 = -\Delta P_{D1}$ and $\text{ACE}_2 = 0$ exactly. The helper area is invisible to its own ACE, so it does not act; the culprit sees its own disturbance and corrects it. That is why $b_i$ is chosen equal to $\beta_i$ — non-interactive control. §4.5

  14. Why does the priority list rank by average cost but dispatch by incremental cost?
    Answer

    Commitment decides whether to pay the no-load cost $c_i$ at all, so the ranking must include it — hence full-load average cost. Once committed, $c_i$ is sunk and cannot influence how much to load the unit, so dispatch uses incremental cost, which excludes it. Your revision sheet calls mixing them the most common error in Module 2. §3.7

Numerical

  1. A relay with CT 300/5, plug setting 150%, TMS 0.3, very inverse, sees a 6000 A fault. Operating time?
    Answer

    CT ratio $= 60$; $I_{\text{relay}} = 6000/60 = 100\ \text{A}$; $I_{pu} = 1.5 \times 5 = 7.5\ \text{A}$; $\text{PSM} = 100/7.5 = 13.33$. Very inverse: $t = 13.5(0.3)/(13.33-1) = 4.05/12.33 = \mathbf{0.328\ s}$.

  2. Same relay and fault, but normal inverse. Operating time?
    Answer

    $13.33^{0.02} = 1.05317$, so $t = 0.14(0.3)/0.05317 = 0.042/0.05317 = \mathbf{0.790\ s}$ — about 2.4 times slower than very inverse at this PSM, which is the §6.5 crossover in action.

  3. A differential relay has 40% bias and $k_0 = 0.3\ \text{A}$. Two CT secondaries read 12 A in and 9 A out. Trip?
    Answer

    Spill $= 3\ \text{A}$; restraint $= (12+9)/2 = 10.5\ \text{A}$; threshold $= 0.3 + 0.4(10.5) = 4.5\ \text{A}$. Since $3 < 4.5$, no trip.

  4. A line is $Z_L = 8 + j30\ \Omega$ and Zone 1 reaches 80%. A fault at 85% of the line has no arc. Does Zone 1 operate?
    Answer

    $|Z_L| = \sqrt{64+900} = 31.05$; reach $= 24.84\ \Omega$. $Z_{app} = 0.85(8+j30) = 6.8+j25.5$, $|Z_{app}| = 26.39\ \Omega$. $26.39 > 24.84$, so no — correctly, since the fault is beyond the 80% reach. Zone 2 would clear it after its delay.

  5. A 400 kV breaker on an effectively earthed system has a restriking-voltage natural frequency of 2 kHz. Maximum RRRV?
    Answer

    $V_m = 400 \times 0.8165 = 326.6\ \text{kV}$; $\omega_n = 2\pi(2000) = 12{,}566\ \text{rad/s}$; $\text{RRRV}_{\max} = 12{,}566 \times 326.6 \times 10^3 = 4.104\times10^9\ \text{V/s} = \mathbf{4.10\ kV/\mu s}$.

  6. Derive the restriking voltage across a breaker that interrupts a terminal fault, stating your approximation and initial conditions. Where is the RRRV greatest?
    Answer

    Freeze the 50 Hz source at $V_m$ (the current zero falls at the voltage peak, and the ringing is over in well under a millisecond). Initial conditions $i(0)=0$, $v_C(0)=0$. KVL: $V_m = L\,di/dt + (1/C)\int i\,dt$, so $I(s) = V_m/[L(s^2+1/LC)]$, $i = (V_m/L\omega_n)\sin\omega_n t$, and $v_C = V_m(1-\cos\omega_n t)$, peaking at $2V_m$. RRRV $= \omega_n V_m\sin\omega_n t$, greatest at $\omega_n t = 90°$. §11.2.

  7. A breaker chops 3 A of magnetising current. $L = 2$ H, stray $C = 4.5$ nF. Estimate the restriking voltage.
    Answer

    $\sqrt{L/C} = \sqrt{2/4.5\times10^{-9}} = \sqrt{4.44\times10^{8}} = 21{,}080\ \Omega$; $V = 3 \times 21{,}080 = \mathbf{63.2\ kV}$. An upper bound: losses and the energy already in $C$ make the real spike smaller. §11.5.

  8. For the circuit of the previous question, what resistor across the contacts gives critical damping? Would 50 kΩ damp it more or less?
    Answer

    $R_c = \tfrac12\sqrt{L/C} = \tfrac12 \times 21{,}080 = \mathbf{10.5\ k\Omega}$. 50 kΩ damps it less: the resistor is in parallel with $C$, so $\zeta = R_c/R = 0.21$, and the circuit still overshoots. A larger parallel resistor is closer to no resistor at all. §11.6.

  9. Why does rotor earth-fault protection need its own DC injection, and why is that supply fed through an isolation transformer?
    Answer

    The field circuit floats, so a single earth fault has no return path and draws no current; there is nothing for a relay to see unless the scheme supplies a voltage between the field circuit and earth. The isolation transformer keeps that injection source floating too; fed straight from the earthed 50 Hz supply, it would itself earth the field circuit. DC rather than AC so that the winding-to-rotor capacitance passes no standing current on a healthy rotor. §8.8.

  10. A machine has $H = 3$, $P_{\max} = 2.0$, $P_m = 1.0$, at 50 Hz. Terminal fault: find $\delta_{cr}$ and $t_{cr}$.
    Answer

    $\delta_0 = \sin^{-1}(0.5) = 0.5236\ \text{rad} = 30°$ — same as §2.6, since $P_m/P_{\max}$ is the same. So $\delta_{cr} = \mathbf{79.56°}$ again: the critical angle depends only on the ratio. But $t_{cr} = \sqrt{4(3)(0.86496)/(314.159 \times 1.0)} = \sqrt{10.3795/314.159} = \sqrt{0.033039} = \mathbf{0.1818\ s}$ — shorter than §2.6's 0.2213 s, because $H$ fell from 4 to 3 and $P_m$ rose from 0.9 to 1.0.

  11. An isolated area has $R = 2.5\ \text{Hz/pu MW}$ and $B = 0.02\ \text{pu MW/Hz}$. A 0.02 pu load step occurs. Steady-state $\Delta f$?
    Answer

    $\beta = 1/2.5 + 0.02 = 0.4 + 0.02 = 0.42\ \text{pu MW/Hz}$; $\Delta f = -0.02/0.42 = \mathbf{-0.0476\ Hz}$. Negative, because the load increased.

  12. Two units: $\text{IC}_1 = 18 + 0.04P_1$, $\text{IC}_2 = 22 + 0.02P_2$, both 0–300 MW. Demand 400 MW, lossless. Find $\lambda$, $P_1$, $P_2$.
    Answer

    $P_1 = (\lambda-18)/0.04$, $P_2 = (\lambda-22)/0.02$. Sum $= 400$: $25\lambda - 450 + 50\lambda - 1100 = 400 \Rightarrow 75\lambda = 1950 \Rightarrow \lambda = \mathbf{26\ Rs./MWh}$. Then $P_1 = 8/0.04 = \mathbf{200\ MW}$, $P_2 = 4/0.02 = \mathbf{200\ MW}$. Both inside their limits, so this is optimal. Check: $\text{IC}_1 = 18+8 = 26$, $\text{IC}_2 = 22+4 = 26$. ✓

  13. [Revision-sheet shape] CT 600/5, PS 150%, TMS 0.20, normal inverse, fault 6000 A primary. Operating time?
    Answer

    $I_{\text{sec}} = 6000/120 = 50$ A; pickup $= 1.5\times5 = 7.5$ A; $M = 6.667$. $t = 0.14(0.20)/(6.667^{0.02}-1) = 0.028/0.03867 = \mathbf{0.724\ s}$. PSM is a multiple of pickup, not of 5 A. §6.8.

  14. [Revision-sheet shape] Dual-slope relay: SLP1 30% up to a breakpoint of 3.0 pu, SLP2 60% beyond. Threshold at $I_{rt} = 4.0$ pu?
    Answer

    $0.30(3.0) + 0.60(1.0) = \mathbf{1.50\ pu}$. Not $0.60\times4.0 = 2.40$: the second slope applies only beyond the breakpoint. §9.8.

  15. [Revision-sheet shape] High-impedance busbar scheme: 30 kA through fault, CT 1500/1, $R_{CT} = 4\ \Omega$, one-way lead 2 Ω, relay setting 25 mA, four CTs each drawing 10 mA at setting. Find $V_s$, minimum $V_k$, $R_{\text{stab}}$ and the primary pickup.
    Answer

    $I_{\text{sec}} = 20$ A; $V_s = 20(4 + 2\times2) = \mathbf{160\ V}$; $V_k \ge \mathbf{320\ V}$; $R_{\text{stab}} = 160/0.025 = \mathbf{6400\ \Omega}$; $I_{\text{pri}} = 1500(0.025 + 4\times0.010) = \mathbf{97.5\ A}$. Count the lead twice. §9.10.

  16. [Revision-sheet shape] A generator with $K = 20$ suffers single-phasing while carrying 0.60 pu. How long may it run?
    Answer

    $I_2 = 0.60/\sqrt3 = 0.346$ pu; $t = 20/0.346^2 = \mathbf{167\ s}$. Taking $I_2 = 0.60$ would give 55.6 s and trip a healthy machine three times too early. §8.9.

  17. [Revision-sheet shape] 200 MVA, 20 kV generator, $X_d = 2.0$, $X_d' = 0.25$ pu. Set the loss-of-excitation offset mho in primary ohms.
    Answer

    $Z_{\text{base}} = 20^2/200 = 2.0\ \Omega$. Diameter $= 2.0\times2.0 = \mathbf{4.0\ \Omega}$; offset $= (0.25/2)(2.0) = \mathbf{0.25\ \Omega}$ into $-X$; far tip at $(0.125 + 2.0)(2.0) = \mathbf{4.25\ \Omega}$. §8.9.

  18. [Revision-sheet shape] 66 kV system, $L = 4$ mH, $C = 0.01\ \mu$F. Find $f_n$, the peak restriking voltage and when it occurs, $\text{RRRV}_{\max}$, and the critical resistance.
    Answer

    $\omega_n = 1/\sqrt{4\times10^{-11}} = 1.581\times10^5$ rad/s, so $f_n = \mathbf{25.2\ kHz}$. $E_m = \sqrt{2/3}(66) = 53.9$ kV; peak $2E_m = \mathbf{107.8\ kV}$ at $t = \pi\sqrt{LC} = \mathbf{19.9\ \mu s}$. $\text{RRRV}_{\max} = 53.9\times10^3 \times 1.581\times10^5 = \mathbf{8.52\ kV/\mu s}$. $R_c = \tfrac12\sqrt{L/C} = \mathbf{316\ \Omega}$. §11.4, Worked example 3.

  19. [Revision-sheet shape] A Zone-1 setting is 30 Ω primary. CT 800/1, VT 132 kV/110 V. Secondary setting?
    Answer

    $Z_{\text{sec}} = 30 \times 800/1200 = \mathbf{20\ \Omega}$. Inverting the ratio gives 45 Ω, which looks just as plausible. §10.9.

  20. Why does a phase-to-ground distance unit with $k_0$ wrongly set to zero refuse to trip for a solid fault at its own Zone-1 boundary?
    Answer

    For a solid single-line-to-ground fault $I_1 = I_2 = I_0$, so the loop impedance is $Z_1(1+k_0)$ per unit length, not $Z_1$. Without compensation the relay measures that larger value; with $k_0 = 0.667$ a fault at 80% of the line reads as if it were at 133%, which is 1.667 times the Zone-1 setting, so the relay under-reaches. §10.5.

12.7 Beyond the frontier

Watch out — what this document could not teach, and why

Examined but unnotated. Fuses, numerical-relay sampling and most of generator protection appear on the quizzes but in no page of your notes; those sections were reconstructed from the master-copy answers outwards. Circuit breakers and RRRV were in the same position until the notes of 27 October, which now cover them (§11.2–11.7), along with rotor earth-fault protection (§8.8). Those notes surfaced one real conflict: the lecture's RRRV model is not the one the quiz key used, and §11.4 is where that decides a mark. The professor's end-term revision sheet (10 November) then covered the whole of Module 4, including busbar and the full generator list, and sided with the lecture on RRRV.

Taught but not reached. The revision sheet defines the end-term's Module 4 as relays, overcurrent, distance, differential, transformer, generator, busbar, and arc and breakers. Everything on that list is now in this document. The topics below appear in neither the sheet nor the notes, so treat them as unlikely:

  • Travelling waves and surge protection — surge impedance, reflection and refraction coefficients, surge arresters. Would need the lecture notes.
  • Insulation coordination — BIL, protective margin.
  • Neutral earthing — solid, resistance, reactance and Petersen coil, and how the choice changes earth-fault current and the directional earth-fault polarising.
  • HVDC, FACTS, power-system security, state estimation — plausible continuations, no evidence.

If any of these is taught between now and the exam, this document can be extended: the chapter files are kept in study-documents/_source/ so new chapters append without rebuilding the rest.

12.8 If you have one evening

Triage — in this order
  1. §12.3, both quizzes, cold (35 min). You cannot plan revision until you know what you cannot do. Mark honestly.
  2. §12.5, the protection formula sheet (10 min). Copy the IEC constants, the RRRV formula and the differential threshold onto one card by hand. These three carry the most marks per line in the whole course.
  3. §6.6, the three IDMT worked examples (15 min). The most-asked topic, three appearances across two quizzes.
  4. §11.4, the RRRV watch-out (10 min). Three marks, and the lecture and the answer key give different numbers for the second blank. Know both, and which to write. Non-negotiable.
  5. §9.10 busbar and §8.9 generator (25 min). Both are new from the professor's revision sheet, both have one-line formulas with a classic slip ($2R_L$; $I/\sqrt3$), and neither was on a quiz, so they are the likeliest fresh end-term numericals.
  6. §12.9, his own "Remember" lists (15 min). Last thing, the evening before, exactly as he advises.
  7. §3.5, the penalty-factor watch-out (10 min). Same situation: the rigorous method scores zero.
  8. §10.4, the MHO worked example (15 min). Four blanks, and the difficulty is bookkeeping rather than physics.
  9. §9.6, the differential procedure (10 min). Two appearances, asked in both directions.
  10. §7.2, the four directional operating criteria (5 min). Especially $\theta = \tau \pm 90°$ does not operate.
  11. §12.4, the lab write-up template (5 min). Three marks for a recallable page.
  12. §2.6 and §4.3, the two revision worked examples (20 min). The midterm's own questions, in case the end-term reuses them — which, on the evidence of §2.10, it may.

That is a little over three hours and it covers, by the quizzes' own marks and the revision sheet's emphasis, the large majority of what is reliably askable. If you have only one hour, do items 2, 4, 5 and 6.

One last thing, because it is the only fact in this document that is about the examiner rather than the subject. He re-asks his own worked examples with the numbers unchanged. Midterm Q1 was page 14 of your tablet notes, verbatim, answer included. So the highest-yield revision activity available to you is not re-reading theory — it is working every solved example in your own notes until you can reproduce it without looking. This document works the ones that exist; the rest are in your notes, waiting. The same goes for the worked checks in his 10 November revision sheet: every one of them is worked in full somewhere in Chapters 5–11, and §12.9 lists where.

12.9 The professor's own "Remember" lists — the evening before revision sheet pp. 1–8

His revision sheet ends every section with a Remember / common mistakes box, and tells you to read those last, the evening before the exam, "because they list the errors that cost marks rather than the theory that earns them". Here they are in one place, each with the section that explains why. If a line does not make sense, that section is your last revision task.

ModuleThe mistake he warns aboutWhere
1 StabilityRadians inside every area integral and inside $t_{cr}$; degrees only as final labelsCh 2
Shade areas only after marking where $P_m > P_e$; $\delta_{\max}$ belongs to the post-fault curveCh 2
The rotor does not stop at 90°: that is the static limit, not a transient wallCh 2
Voltage instability ≠ low voltage; check the discriminant before solving for $V$Ch 2
2 EconomicAverage cost ranks the priority list; incremental cost dispatches. The ranking includes the fixed cost $c$, the dispatch ignores itCh 3
Min up/down times couple the hours, which is why UC cannot be solved hour by hourCh 3
3 LFC$\Delta f = -R\,\Delta P_D$, but the feedback path carries $1/R$Ch 4
Load increase ⇒ $\Delta f$ negative. A positive answer is wrong even if the magnitude is rightCh 4
Refer droops to a common base before comparing themCh 4
Two-area: $\beta_1 + \beta_2/a_{12}$, never $\beta_1 + \beta_2$; ACE $= \Delta f$ only for an isolated areaCh 4
4(a) PrinciplesOverlap is not a design error; a gap is§5.3
Unit protection is selective but gives no back-up; non-unit gives back-up but must be graded§5.12
The relay does not clear the fault, and relay time is not clearing time§5.12
Damping torque is zero at standstill, so pickup is a spring question§5.14
4(b) OvercurrentPSM is a multiple of pickup, not of the CT secondary rating: the commonest slip in the module§6.3, §6.8
TMS is a multiplier, not a time. Add the CTI to an operating time, then invert the curve§7.6
Grade at maximum fault current; set plugs from maximum load§7.6
The relay nearest the source is the slowest: the price of selectivity§7.4
4(c) DistanceA reactance relay takes Im($Z$) only. Writing $|Z|$ lets arc resistance back in§10.7
Secondary ohms = primary × CT ratio / VT ratio; the inverted answer looks plausible§10.9
Infeed causes under-reach, never over-reach§10.8
Z1 under-reaches by design; Z2 over-reaches by design§10.3
4(d–e) Differential, transformerCompare CT secondaries: convert first, then subtract§9.6
Second harmonic ⇒ inrush; fifth harmonic ⇒ overfluxing. Do not swap them§9.9
Buchholz has no electrical measurement and is the only detector of an incipient fault§8.4
Stability ratio: refer both currents across the turns ratio first (fourfold error at 132/33 kV)§9.8
4(f) Busbar$R_{CT} + 2R_L$: counting one lead halves $V_s$ and the scheme maloperates§9.10
Add the magnetising currents of all paralleled CTs to the relay current§9.10
Busbar schemes are designed from the external fault, not the internal one§9.10
4(g) GeneratorNegative sequence heats the rotor, at double frequency, though it is a stator current§8.9
Single-phasing: $I_2 = I/\sqrt3$. Single-phase load: $I_2 = I_a/3$§8.9
Loss of excitation is detected by impedance in $-X$, not by overcurrent§8.9
Reverse power protects the turbine, not the generator§8.7
4(h) BreakersInterruption happens at a current zero; a "3-cycle breaker" is a mechanism rating§11.1
Restriking voltage (transient) ≠ recovery voltage (power frequency). Examiners ask for one and students define the other§11.3
Making capacity exceeds breaking capacity because of the DC offset§11.7
Current chopping is dangerous precisely because the current was small§11.5
Key result — his three-sentence summary of Module 4

Every scheme is a compromise between dependability and security, and you can name it in each one: the 80% Zone-1 reach, the bias slope, the busbar check zone. Selectivity is bought with time, which is why the relay nearest the source is slowest and why only unit protection is both fast and selective, at the price of no back-up. And protection closes the loop to Module 1: clearing in 100 ms rather than 500 ms is about staying inside $t_{cr}$. In his words, "a protection engineer is a stability engineer working on a shorter timescale". That is this document's three clocks, said by the examiner.
